### [1] [Position: It is Time to Virtualize Foundation Models with a Self-evolving Operating System Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19203)
*Suparna Bhattacharya,Tarun Kumar,Cong Xu,Satish Kumar Mopur,Jiahao Li,Ashish Mishra,Aalap Tripathy,Annmary Justine Koomthanam,Martin Foltin,Ian Foster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) as a system layer to virtualize foundation model interactions, addressing fragmentation in current agentic systems by providing standardized runtime services analogous to how operating systems abstract hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI applications have evolved from monolithic foundation models to compound agentic systems, but the stack remains fragmented. Despite protocols facilitating tool/agent connectivity, each framework implements its own runtime for state, memory, budgets, and guardrails, leading to non-portable behavior and brittle governance. This situation is compared to computing before operating systems where every program re-implemented basic services.

Method: The paper advocates for the development of a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) - a system layer that virtualizes FM interactions similar to how virtual machines abstract physical hardware. The FMOS would provide applications with the illusion of dedicated, trustworthy FM instances with effectively unbounded capabilities.

Result: The proposed FMOS would internally orchestrate knowledge across memory tiers, handle model selection and resource allocation, and enforce verification and policies. It would learn when to intervene vs. let inference proceed directly, adapting policies based on operational experience, similar to the human brain switching between fast intuition and slow deliberation.

Conclusion: The field now needs a Foundation Model Operating System as a fundamental system layer to address the fragmentation and inefficiencies in current agentic AI systems, enabling more portable, reliable, and efficient AI applications through standardized virtualization of foundation model interactions.

Abstract: AI applications have shifted from single, monolithic foundation models (FM) to compound agentic systems. Yet today's stacks remain fragmented: even as protocols (e.g., MCP, A2A) ease tool/agent connectivity, each framework embeds an implicit runtime for state, memory, budgets, and guardrails, making behavior non-portable and governance brittle. It mirrors computing before operating systems, when every program re-implemented basic services. This position paper argues that the field now needs a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) -- a system layer that virtualizes FM interactions analogous to how virtual machines abstract physical hardware, giving applications the illusion of dedicated, trustworthy FM instances with effectively unbounded capabilities. Internally, the FMOS orchestrates knowledge across memory tiers, model selection and resource allocation, and verification and policy enforcement. Like the human brain switching between fast intuition and slow deliberation, the FMOS learns when to intervene and when to let inference proceed directly and continuously adapting its policies based on operational experience.

</details>


### [2] [Regularized Emphatic Temporal-Difference Learning: Stability under Constant Stepsizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19170)
*Xingguo Chen,Zhaohui Wu,Jinguo Ye,Chao Li,Shangdong Yang,Guang Yang,Skylar Liang,Wenhao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ETD learning has stability issues in sampled dynamics despite theoretical guarantees. The paper introduces Regularized Emphatic TD (RETD) which normalizes the emphatic signal and adds delayed corrections to ensure almost-sure convergence while maintaining ETD's fixed point properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emphatic temporal-difference learning (ETD) theoretically stabilizes off-policy TD updates and changes projection geometry, but these properties don't guarantee stability in constant-stepsize sampled dynamics. A counterexample shows ETD can have positive Lyapunov exponents despite mean map contraction, revealing instability in practical implementations.

Method: The authors introduce Regularized Emphatic TD (RETD), which stores the emphatic TD signal in a leaky scalar state and releases delayed corrections. This normalization-based post-shock repair preserves the trace and importance ratios while modifying dynamics. RETD maintains the ETD equilibrium as an affine shift and recovers the exact ETD fixed point through readouts.

Result: RETD achieves almost-sure convergence with harmonic diminishing stepsizes and has conditional constant-stepsize moment contraction from Markovian random-product bounds. It demonstrates certified negative Lyapunov exponents on problematic cases where ETD shows instability, while recovering ETD's fixed point exactly through readout mechanisms.

Conclusion: RETD provides a practical solution to ETD's stability problems in sampled dynamics by modifying post-shock dynamics without changing the shared follow-on-trace variance. It maintains ETD's desirable fixed-point properties while ensuring convergence, though task-dependent performance variations remain.

Abstract: Emphatic temporal-difference learning (ETD) stabilizes the expected off-policy TD update and changes its projection geometry, but neither property determines constant-stepsize sampled dynamics. We construct an ergodic two-state counterexample in which the ETD mean map contracts while the sampled product has a positive top Lyapunov exponent. Regenerative-cycle analysis separates this sign from the infinite variance of the follow-on trace. We introduce regularized emphatic TD (RETD), a normalized first-order post-shock repair that leaves the trace and importance ratios unchanged, stores the emphatic TD signal in a leaky scalar state, and releases a delayed correction. RETD's raw equilibrium is an affine shift of the ETD equilibrium; single- and two-regularization readouts recover the ETD fixed point exactly. We prove almost-sure convergence for harmonic diminishing stepsizes and a conditional constant-stepsize moment-contraction result from a Markovian random-product bound. RETD has certified negative exponents on the two-state construction and one Baird point, whereas the positive Baird ETD sign remains numerical. Paired 10,000-run experiments validate both separations, fixed-point recovery, a nonmonotone stability region, and task dependence. RETD changes post-shock dynamics; it does not reduce the shared follow-on-trace variance.

</details>


### [3] [Agentic AI Networking for Heterogeneous Unmanned Aerial Systems in Low-Altitude Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19538)
*Nguyen Duc Minh Quang,Chang Liu,Shuangyang Li,Derrick Wing Kwan Ng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hierarchical hybrid LLM-MARL architecture is proposed for coordinating heterogeneous services in low-altitude wireless networks, using an outer LLM loop to adapt objectives and an inner MARL loop to execute policies, enabling autonomous adaptation to changing requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-altitude wireless networks face challenges in coordinating heterogeneous UAV services with distinct, time-varying requirements in shared airspace. Conventional controllers with fixed objectives cannot adapt autonomously to changing service needs and resource priorities, limiting their effectiveness in dynamic environments.

Method: A hierarchical dual-loop architecture combining large language models (LLMs) and multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). The outer adaptation loop uses LLM-assisted game orchestration to interpret service requirements and operator intent, reconfiguring objectives and resource priorities. The inner loop executes decentralized, parameter-conditioned MARL policies under the configured game framework.

Result: A logistics-monitoring case study demonstrates that the framework facilitates coordinated coexistence among heterogeneous services, adapting to evolving operating conditions without retraining the underlying MARL policies. The approach shows promise for autonomous adaptation in dynamic LAWN environments.

Conclusion: The hybrid LLM-MARL architecture enables autonomous adaptation to changing service requirements in low-altitude wireless networks. The paper identifies challenges and research directions toward scalable, trustworthy, and adaptive agentic LAWNs, pointing to the need for further work in this emerging area.

Abstract: Low-altitude wireless networks (LAWNs) are emerging as a key infrastructure for heterogeneous unmanned aerial systems that support concurrent services within a shared three-dimensional airspace. Their coexistence creates strong coupling among mobility, connectivity, and shared network resources, while heterogeneous services impose distinct and time-varying requirements. These interactions naturally form a dynamic non-cooperative game in which both operating conditions and coordination objectives evolve over time. Conventional optimization and learning-based controllers typically rely on predefined objectives, limiting their ability to adapt autonomously to changing service requirements and resource priorities. To address this challenge, we propose a hierarchical hybrid large language model (LLM)- multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) architecture organized as a dual-loop structure. Specifically, an outer adaptation loop employs LLM-assisted game orchestration to interpret service requirements and operator intent, and reconfigure objectives and resource priorities, while an inner loop executes decentralized, parameter-conditioned MARL policies under the configured game. A logistics-monitoring case study illustrates how the proposed framework facilitates coordinated coexistence among heterogeneous services, adapting to evolving operating conditions without retraining the underlying MARL policies. Finally, we discuss key challenges and research directions toward scalable, trustworthy, and adaptive agentic LAWNs.

</details>


### [4] [BioPhys-Bridge: A Benchmark for Interdisciplinary Scientific Reasoning in Physics-Grounded Biological Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19180)
*Qingyang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioPhys-Bridge is a new benchmark dataset for evaluating language models' ability to perform evidence-grounded scientific reasoning in biophysics literature, requiring data grounding in source evidence, physics model interpretation, and biological mechanism linking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models struggle with interdisciplinary scientific research literature, particularly in biophysics where faithful answers require grounding observed data in source evidence, interpreting through quantitative physics models, and linking to biological mechanisms.

Method: Created BioPhys-Bridge benchmark dataset containing 500 cases with evidence blocks, stable evidence IDs, quantitative values, units, equations, assumptions, mechanisms, and next decisions. Includes 1,517 agent-facing tasks across six biological domains and nine physical model families, with strict quality gates and expert review for 81 cases.

Result: Preliminary evaluations show DeepSeek-V4-Flash achieved highest evidence-ID F1 score (0.360), followed by Qwen3.7-Max (0.316) and GPT-4o-mini (0.294). Dataset enables evaluation of attribution, faithfulness, hallucination reduction, and biological experiment design.

Conclusion: BioPhys-Bridge serves as an interdisciplinary benchmark for evaluating complex multi-step scientific reasoning in biophysics, with plans to increase dataset size and complexity in future work.

Abstract: Language models face unique challenges in analyzing interdisciplinary scientific research literature. In biophysics research, faithful answers require grounding observed data in source evidence, interpreting it through a quantitative physics model, and linking it to a biological mechanism. To address this challenge, we introduce BioPhys-Bridge, a novel benchmark dataset for evidence-grounded scientific reasoning over biophysical literature. Each case contains evidence blocks, stable evidence IDs, quantitative values, units, equations, assumptions, mechanisms, and next decisions as grounding targets for question answering (QA) and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The initial release contains 500 cases, 1,517 agent-facing tasks, and covers six biological domains and nine physical model families, including three sparse families reserved for future expansion. We enforce strict quality gates for all cases in schema, evidence-integrity, quantitative-grounding, source-license, duplicate, unit-normalization, with domain expert review and annotation for 81 cases. Preliminary evaluations show that DeepSeek-V4-Flash obtain the highest evidence-ID $F_1$ score (0.360), followed by Qwen3.7-Max (0.316) and GPT-4o-mini (0.294). BioPhys-Bridge is an interdisciplinary benchmark for evaluating attribution, faithfulness, hallucination reduction, and biological experiment design with complex, multi-step scientific reasoning. Future works will increase the size and complexity of the dataset and perform comprehensive evaluations. Code and data are available in the GitHub repository and on Hugging Face.

</details>


### [5] [Replan, Repair, or Edit? A Unified Empirical Evaluation of Travel Agents for Itinerary Revision under Resource Disruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19654)
*Xiaofei Yuan,Yan Zhang,Shaobo Qiao,Huangleshuai He,Leyan Ni,Mingchen Ju,Lujia Yang,Sijia Xu,Yifu Tang,Zhengyi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systematic study comparing three travel itinerary revision methods (full replanning, hierarchical repair, local revision) shows LLM-based full replanning performs best on compound disruptions, while repair methods preserve more of the original plan with fewer edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Travel itineraries often become infeasible after acceptance due to disruptions like flight cancellations or hotel unavailability. Existing revision methods have different task formulations and evaluation protocols, making meaningful comparison difficult.

Method: Systematic empirical study using 500 single-disruption cases (feasible and infeasible) and 200 feasible simultaneous compound-disruption cases from TREK-derived benchmarks. Compared three approaches: LLM-Z3 full replanning, IPyHOPPER hierarchical repair, and iTIMO local-revision adapter.

Result: LLM-Z3 with Gemini achieved highest compound-disruption success. IPyHOPPER nearly matched LLM-Z3's single-disruption success while preserving more of the accepted itinerary. Repair methods made fewer edits and retained more commitments than full replanning. Computational profiles differed significantly across methods.

Conclusion: The study provides practical guidelines for balancing feasibility recovery, commitment preservation, and computational cost in travel itinerary revision, showing trade-offs between different approaches depending on disruption type and preservation priorities.

Abstract: Travel-planning agents generate itineraries that may become infeasible after acceptance because of flight cancellations, hotel unavailability, or attraction closures. Revising these itineraries involves full replanning, classical plan repair, and LLM-based travel-agent revision, whose differing task formulations and evaluation protocols hinder comparison. We conduct a systematic empirical study using two TREK-derived benchmark sets: 500 single-disruption cases, including feasible and infeasible instances, and 200 feasible simultaneous compound-disruption cases. We compare LLM-Z3 full replanning, IPyHOPPER hierarchical repair, and an iTIMO local-revision adapter across effectiveness, plan stability, and computational cost. LLM-Z3 with Gemini achieved the highest observed compound-disruption success. IPyHOPPER nearly matched that configuration's single-disruption overall success, while preserving substantially more of the accepted itinerary on successful repairs. Successful hierarchical and local repairs made fewer edits and retained more accepted commitments than full replanning. Computational profiles differed: IPyHOPPER used no LLM inference, the evaluated LLM-Z3 adapter used compact one-call inference, and the iTIMO adapter consumed substantially more tokens. The study provides practical guidelines for balancing feasibility recovery, commitment preservation, and computational cost within evaluated settings.

</details>


### [6] [What Do We Expect from LLMs? Mapping the Design of LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19182)
*Chao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers analyzed 14,767 papers on LLM evaluation benchmarks from 2022-2026, tracking how evaluation requirements evolve toward action/interaction/professional applications, with uneven model participation in scoring vs. test generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLM benchmarks drive progress measurement, model rankings alone don't reveal how evaluation requirements themselves are changing. The expanding variety of benchmarks reflects what researchers expect LLMs to do and what counts as successful performance.

Method: Systematic mapping of 14,767 papers introducing or updating evaluation resources from arXiv submissions (Jan 2022-Aug 2026), using staged screening and automated full-text coding to examine changes in target systems, domains, evaluation materials/conditions, and scoring mechanisms.

Result: The collection shows: 1) growing emphasis on action, interaction, and professional applications; 2) coexistence of established and newer design elements; 3) uneven model participation - LLM-based scoring grows within both agent and non-agent groups, but model-generated materials show no comparable sustained increase in recent cohorts.

Conclusion: The findings illuminate how public research translates capability expectations into concrete tests and criteria for success. As AI participates in constructing tests, performing tasks, and judging responses, this raises critical questions about whether expanding evaluation provides more independent evidence or risks reproducing the preferences and blind spots of participating models.

Abstract: Benchmarks are central to how progress in large language models (LLMs) is assessed and communicated. Yet model rankings alone reveal little about how evaluation requirements themselves are changing. The expanding variety of benchmarks offers another perspective: what researchers expect LLMs to do, and what they count as successful performance. We systematically map 14,767 papers introducing or updating evaluation resources from arXiv submissions between January 2022 and August 2026. Using staged screening and automated full-text coding, we examine changes in target systems and domains, evaluation materials and conditions, and scoring mechanisms. The collection shows growing emphasis on action, interaction, and professional applications, while established and newer design elements frequently coexist. Model participation also develops unevenly: LLM-based scoring grows within both agent and non-agent groups, whereas model-generated materials show no comparable sustained increase in recent cohorts. These findings illuminate how public research translates capability expectations into concrete tests and criteria for success. As AI participates in constructing tests, performing tasks, and judging responses, they also raise a question: does expanding evaluation provide more independent evidence, or risk reproducing the preferences and blind spots of its participating models?

</details>


### [7] [FINSKILLOPS: A Self-Evolving Multi-Agent System for SEC Filing QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19680)
*Yanzhang Ma,Zhenghan Tai,Hanwei Wu,Sizhe Guan,Jianliang Lei,Hailin He,Chaolong Jiang,Jijun Chi,Tung Sum Thomas Kwok,Bohuai Xiao,Jingrui Tian,Xinlu Wu,Xingao Zhan,Peng Lu,Muzhi Li,Yihong Wu,Liheng Ma,Sicheng Lyu,Tianshuo Yan,Junhao Zhu,Yaqian Xu,Lei Ding,Yufei Cui,Ziquan Liu,Boyu Han,Hengli Liu,Ling Zhou,Xinyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FINSKILLOPS enables controlled behavioral maintenance for financial QA systems by creating scoped skill patches from failure diagnoses, with governance to prevent regressions while improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial QA systems have fixed reliability behavior after deployment, while new SEC-filing questions repeatedly expose heterogeneous errors. Existing self-improvement methods lack control over where corrections apply and may break previously correct answers.

Method: FINSKILLOPS is a multi-agent system that derives reusable skills from evidence-grounded, typed failure diagnoses and governs them through targeted validation, protected-case regression checks, negative controls, and versioned replacement/retirement.

Result: Across six financial QA benchmarks, FINSKILLOPS achieves highest verdict-weighted correctness and reference consistency. Evolved skills raise correctness from 3.70 to 4.55 on enhanced benchmark. In operational study, only 6 of 33 proposed skills promoted, reducing monitoring non-correct rate from 20.0% to 12.5%.

Conclusion: Controlled skill scope, admission, and lifecycle management form the foundation for reliable self-improvement in financial QA systems, enabling scoped skill patches that fix failures without introducing regressions.

Abstract: Financial QA systems are typically improved before deployment through better retrieval, prompting, or agent coordination, leaving their reliability behavior fixed thereafter. In practice, new SEC-filing questions repeatedly expose heterogeneous errors in period, entity, evidence use, and calculation. Existing self-improvement methods can turn failures into new behaviors, but offer limited control over where a correction should apply or which previously correct answers it may break. We therefore frame post-deployment improvement as controlled behavioral maintenance: recurring failures should become scoped skill patches, and each patch should earn deployment with- out introducing regressions. We instantiate this view in FINSKILLOPS, a multi-agent system for SEC filing QA. FINSKILLOPS derives reusable skills from evidence-grounded, typed failure diagnoses and governs them through targeted validation, protected-case regression checks, negative controls, and versioned replacement or retirement. Across six financial QA benchmarks, a single frozen skill registry achieves the highest verdict-weighted correctness and reference consistency among the evaluated systems. Evolved skills raise correctness from 3.70 to 4.55 on our enhanced benchmark. In a separate 12-round operational study, only six of 33 proposed skills are promoted, while the monitoring non-correct rate falls from 20.0% to 12.5%. These results establish controlled skill scope, admission, and lifecycle management as the foundation for reliable self-improvement.

</details>


### [8] [LearnActCoder: Role-Aware Error Memory for Adaptive Clinical Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19721)
*Meysam Ghaffari,Bhaskar Sen,Nasim Sabetpour,Nina Fatehi,Animesh Agarwal,Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Learn-Then-Act framework improves clinical coding through structured error memory that converts coding mistakes into actionable knowledge without model retraining


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical coding agents frequently encounter recurring failure modes like unsupported codes, missed conditions, specificity errors, and procedure-coding convention mismatches, requiring systematic error correction approaches.

Method: LearnActCoder framework with Mistake Knowledge Database (MistakeKDB) created from labeled examples; false-negative lessons routed to recall-oriented Coder, false-positive lessons to precision-oriented Judge; uses Coder-Judge pipeline with lookup-table grounding.

Result: On MIMIC-III notes: CPT F1 improved by 5.9 percentage points with structured MistakeKDB; ICD-9 improvement not significant. On MIMIC-IV: memory shifted ICD-10 coding toward higher precision with recall cost, F1 unchanged. Memory maintained stable ICD operating point on 1,000 held-out notes.

Conclusion: Structured, feedback-derived error memory is useful for adapting clinical coding behavior across cases without weight updates or workflow changes, though CPT/HCPCS performance remains low and evaluation is retrospective rather than clinical deployment.

Abstract: Clinical coding agents repeatedly encounter the same failure modes, including unsupported codes, missed documented conditions, specificity errors, and procedure-coding convention mismatches. We introduce Learn-Then-Act, an inference-time adaptation framework that converts errors from a small labeled LEARN batch into a structured Mistake Knowledge Database (MistakeKDB). False-negative lessons are routed to a recall-oriented Coder, while false-positive lessons are routed to a precision-oriented Judge. We instantiate the framework in LearnActCoder, a Coder-Judge clinical coding pipeline with lookup-table grounding where available. On 150 matched MIMIC-III notes, structured MistakeKDB improves CPT F1 by 5.9 percentage points, while raw-example and reflection-style memories remain near the no-memory baseline; the ICD-9 improvement is not significant. On a matched MIMIC-IV cohort, memory shifts ICD-10 coding toward higher precision at a recall cost, leaving F1 statistically unchanged. Applying the same memory to 1,000 held-out MIMIC-III notes maintains a stable ICD operating point, providing scale/stability evidence. Overall, the results are consistent with structured, feedback-derived error memory being useful for adapting clinical coding behavior across cases without weight updates or changes to the underlying workflow. Absolute CPT/HCPCS performance remains low, and the system is evaluated retrospectively rather than in clinical deployment.

</details>


### [9] [What Do Current Systematic Generalization Tasks Miss? A Reasoning-Centered Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19212)
*Chengwen Qi,Deheng Ye,Yatao Bian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TranSGrid is a new testbed for systematic generalization that combines deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning, revealing that current Transformer models perform poorly on comprehensive reasoning tasks despite strong performance on simpler benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies of systematic generalization rely on simplifications that omit essential aspects of this capability, such as linear action composition, productivity-based tests, and action-explicit goals. These simplifications make systematic generalization easier to study but don't fully capture the reasoning demands involved in this cognitive ability.

Method: The authors introduce TranSGrid, a unified testbed that integrates deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning. They test seven Transformer models on 4,800 TranSGrid instances and create variants that reintroduce the common simplifications: one making actions compose almost linearly (reducing inductive demand) and another making goals action-explicit (reducing abductive demand).

Result: Models perform much worse on TranSGrid than on held-out test sets: the largest model solves 79.6% of the test set but only 55.3% of TranSGrid and 15.8% of the hardest subset. The performance gap persists within the training length range, showing productivity alone isn't sufficient. When reintroducing the simplifications, solve rates return to roughly test set levels, indicating that either simplification alone reduces TranSGrid to an ordinary test set.

Conclusion: Existing systematic generalization tasks reduce either inductive or abductive reasoning demands, and comprehensive measurement requires tasks involving all three forms of reasoning (deductive, inductive, and abductive). Productivity alone is insufficient for evaluating systematic generalization.

Abstract: Systematic generalization, the ability to solve novel problems by recombining known atomic elements, is central to human intelligence but difficult to study rigorously under controlled settings. Existing studies therefore rely on simplifications such as approximately linear action composition, productivity-based tests, and action-explicit goals, which make systematic generalization easier to study but omit some essential aspects of this capability. To characterize what these simplifications miss, we adopt a reasoning-centered lens and introduce TranSGrid, a testbed that brings deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning together within a unified task. Experiments with seven Transformers on 4,800 TranSGrid instances show that all models perform much worse on TranSGrid than on a held-out test set: the largest model solves 79.6% of the test set, but only 55.3% of TranSGrid and 15.8% of the hardest subset. The gap remains within the training length range, showing that productivity alone is not sufficient to evaluate systematic generalization. Additionally, we reintroduce the other two simplifications into TranSGrid: one variant makes actions compose almost linearly (reducing the inductive demand), the other makes goals action-explicit (reducing the abductive one). In both, solve rates return to roughly the test set level, showing that either simplification alone is enough to reduce TranSGrid to an ordinary held-out test set. Together, our results show that existing tasks reduce either or both of the inductive and abductive demands, and that comprehensively measuring systematic generalization requires a task that involves all three forms of reasoning.

</details>


### [10] [A Proposal for an Agentic AI Architecture to Support Multi-Domain Decision-Making in the Brazilian Armed Forces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20080)
*Gioliano de Oliveira Braga,Sidnei Barbieri,Ágney Lopes Roth Ferraz,Wagner Comin Sonaglio,Henrique Curi de Miranda e Lourenço Alves Pereira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an Agentic AI architecture for autonomous, auditable decision support across Brazilian Armed Forces to handle complex multi-domain data challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing complexity of multi-domain operational environments (land, aerospace, naval, cyber, electromagnetic spectrum) has overwhelmed command-and-control centers with data volume and velocity, straining the OODA decision cycle. Current defense AI systems are reactive, isolated tools requiring heavy human operator involvement for information integration, scenario assessment, and course of action formulation.

Method: Proposes a conceptual Agentic AI architecture that enables AI systems to: 1) plan autonomously, 2) access diverse data sources, 3) execute tools, and 4) act autonomously with auditability. The architecture is designed to support decision-making across Brazilian Armed Forces (Navy, Army, Air Force), with discussion of data/sensor access requirements and security/permission safeguards.

Result: Identifies four key application fronts: 1) decision support, 2) situational analysis, 3) feasibility studies, and 4) countermeasure suggestion. The framework addresses administrative, strategic, operational, and tactical contexts while incorporating necessary security controls for responsible employment.

Conclusion: A transition from reactive, isolated AI tools to proactive, integrated Agentic AI systems is needed to handle modern multi-domain warfare challenges. The proposed architecture provides an auditable, autonomous solution for decision support across Brazilian military domains with appropriate safeguards for responsible implementation.

Abstract: The growing complexity of multi-domain operational environments (land, aerospace, naval, cyber, and electromagnetic spectrum) has increased the volume and velocity of data reaching command-and-control (C2) centers, straining the observe-orient-decide-act (OODA) decision cycle. Artificial Intelligence (AI) systems currently employed in defense are, in general, reactive and isolated tools that still rely heavily on human operators to integrate information, assess scenarios, and formulate courses of action. This paper proposes a conceptual Agentic AI architecture for AI systems that can plan, access data sources, execute tools, and act autonomously and audibly, aimed at supporting decision-making across the three Brazilian Armed Forces (Navy, Army, and Air Force). Four application fronts are discussed (decision support, situational analysis, feasibility studies, and countermeasure suggestion), as well as the data and sensor access requirements and the security and permission safeguards necessary for responsible employment across administrative, strategic, operational, and tactical contexts.

</details>


### [11] [Characterizing Web Search by Conversational LLM Agents: From Search Decisions and Strategies to Results and Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19244)
*Mahsa Amani,Seungeon Lee,Abhisek Dash,Asmaa El Fraihi,Yunah Jang,Elisabeth Kirsten,Qinyuan Wu,Krishna P. Gummadi,Manish Gupta,Abhilasha Ravichander,Muhammad Bilal Zafar,Soumi Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First comprehensive study of Web search across four major conversational AI platforms reveals significant variations in search invocation decisions, query strategies, domain preferences, and citation practices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The end-to-end lifecycle of agentic Web search in conversational LLMs remains poorly understood, despite increasing reliance on search functionality. Current research lacks comprehensive analysis of search invocation decisions, query formulation, result domain preferences, and result transformation.

Method: Combined real-world user interactions (invivo) with controlled experiments using the same platforms' models via APIs (invitro). Studied four major conversational platforms: ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, and DeepSeek. Investigated search invocation decisions, query formulation strategies, domain preferences in search results, and transformation of results into grounded responses.

Result: Web-search decisions vary substantially across platforms and models; more frequent search invocation doesn't necessarily improve response quality. Agents use complex querying strategies, and platform-specific search engines return results from preferred domains. Responses are largely grounded in search results, but some claims rely on uncited results, raising attribution and reliability concerns.

Conclusion: Findings have important implications for designing future AI agents and Web search tools optimized for conversational retrieval, highlighting the need for better search invocation mechanisms, transparent result sourcing, and improved citation practices to ensure reliability.

Abstract: Conversational LLM agents increasingly rely on Web search, yet the end-to-end lifecycle of agentic search remains poorly understood. We present the first study of Web search across four major conversational platforms (ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, and DeepSeek), combining real-world user interactions (invivo) with controlled experiments using the same platform's models by their APIs (invitro). We investigate the quality of agentic decisions to invoke Web search, their strategies to formulate queries, the potential domain preferences in the search results they receive, and the choices they make when transforming search results into grounded responses. We find that Web-search decisions vary substantially across platforms and models, while more frequent Web-search invocation does not necessarily yield better response quality. We further show that conversational agents employ different complex querying strategies and that platform specific search engines return search results from their preferred domains. Finally, although responses are largely grounded in search results, some claims rely on uncited search results, raising concerns about attribution and reliability. Our findings have important implications for the design of future AI agents and Web search tools optimized for conversational retrieval.

</details>


### [12] [Language-model groups overstate consensus when replaying human deliberation on a reasoning task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20543)
*Tengfei Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agent groups show dramatically higher consensus rates than human groups in Wason reasoning tasks, but this artificial consensus doesn't translate to better collective accuracy and may bias estimates of human deliberative outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-consensus rates are commonly used as indicators of collective cognition, but they depend heavily on how participation and final states are operationalized. There's a need to understand how LLM agent simulations compare to real human group deliberation outcomes.

Method: Researchers replayed 100 held-out human Wason group discussions using matched LLM agent groups, seeding one belief-anchored agent per participant's pre-discussion answer. They scored both agents and humans with the same code and conducted multiple sensitivity analyses (submit-based and participation-matched comparisons).

Result: Agent groups showed much higher consensus rates (gaps of 34-44 percentage points) than human groups across different scoring definitions. Human consensus estimates ranged from 24.0% to 57.0%. The consensus gap persisted across sensitivity analyses and under different conditions. Notably, agent groups achieved near-unanimous agreement on incorrect answers, showing that simulated consensus doesn't track collective accuracy.

Conclusion: LLM agent groups are biased estimators of human group-outcome distributions in deliberative settings. The scoring-explicit approach provides a basis for assessing simulated-group estimates of human deliberative outcomes, highlighting that artificial consensus in LLM agents doesn't necessarily indicate better collective reasoning.

Abstract: Full-consensus rates are often treated as indicators of collective cognition, yet depend on how participation and final states are operationalized. We replayed 100 held-out human Wason groups with matched large language model (LLM) agent groups, seeding one belief-anchored agent per participant's pre-discussion answer and scoring agents and people with the same code. Across human scoring definitions, estimates ranged from 24.0% to 57.0%; about one fifth of participants never posted, whereas agents almost always did. Agent groups remained more consensual in two post-unblinding sensitivity analyses: the submit-based comparison (n = 98) yielded gaps of 34.0 and 43.9 percentage points for chat and reasoning modes, and the participation-matched comparison (n = 45) yielded gaps of 34.1 and 44.4 points. These complementary routes reduced different measurement asymmetries yet converged within 0.5 percentage points. The gap persisted without early stopping and under a reparameterization removing the memorizable answer; reasoning-mode groups then agreed nearly unanimously, mostly on incorrect answers. Simulated consensus did not track collective accuracy, and belief-anchored agent groups were biased estimators of the human group-outcome distribution in this setting. These analyses provide a scoring-explicit basis for assessing simulated-group estimates of human deliberative outcomes.

</details>


### [13] [Do AI Agents Understand Computer Architecture?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19387)
*Ambika Sharan,Grigory Chirkov,Soheil Abbasloo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoTuring measures whether agents improve accelerators through architectural reasoning vs. blind knob optimization by testing the same agent on the same design space twice: once with meaningful architectural parameters and once with anonymous variables, holding all else equal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations cannot distinguish whether agents improve hardware through genuine architectural reasoning or just competent search over meaningless knobs. Only architectural understanding transfers to new architectures, so it's important to measure this capability.

Method: AutoTuring tests the same agent on the same 15-dimensional accelerator space twice: 1) with named architectural knobs and simulator counters (meaningful version), 2) as anonymous variables on [0,1] (blind version). Same evaluator, legal space, and reachable optima ensure only meaning varies.

Result: Architectural reasoning pays: the agent with architectural knowledge outperforms a modeled H200 by 5.4% and its blind counterpart by 12.3% on average, using 70.1% fewer simulator calls. Adding a critic loop helps blind agents recover most of the gap but doesn't benefit architects, suggesting architectural knowledge and structured critique are substitutes rather than complements.

Conclusion: The key contribution is not the accelerator design but the comparison method itself, which can distinguish architectural reasoning from blind optimization. Preliminary findings (5-6 runs per condition) suggest architectural knowledge provides real benefits but can be partially substituted by structured critique.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly asked to design hardware, and increasingly reported to succeed. Such reports establish that a design improved; they cannot establish why. An agent that improves an accelerator may be reasoning about the machine, or may be searching competently over knobs whose meaning it never recovers -- and only the first transfers to the next architecture. Existing evaluations cannot tell the two apart, because they vary the agent while holding the framing of the problem fixed. We do the opposite. AutoTuring hands the same agent the same 15-dimensional accelerator space twice: once as named architectural knobs with simulator counters, once as anonymous variables on [0,1], with the evaluator, the legal space and the reachable optima held identical, so that the only thing that varies is whether the problem means anything. The gap between the two is the measurement. On a nine-kernel FP16 GEMM basket, meaning pays: the architect beats a modeled H200 by 5.4% and its blind counterpart by 12.3% on average, with 70.1% fewer simulator calls. It does not pay uniquely: a critic loop recovers most of that gap for the blind agent and buys the architect nothing, so architectural knowledge and structured critique behave as substitutes rather than as complements. We report these as preliminary findings -- five to six runs per condition on a single modeled accelerator -- and take the comparison itself, not the accelerator, to be the contribution.

</details>


### [14] [MAGS: Multi-agent Auto-formalization Guarantees Safety for Agentic Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19391)
*Albert Wu,Nicholas Roberts,Tzu-Heng Huang,Haoran Lin,Gil Friedman,Sungjun Cho,Gabriel Orlanski,Frederic Sala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGS is a multi-agent framework that automatically generates executable programs with formal safety guarantees by using Dafny as a verification-aware intermediate representation, achieving 100% success rate in producing safe programs across CUDA kernels, terminal scripts, and robotic-arm tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM coding agents generate complex programs at a scale that makes thorough human review difficult, increasing safety and security risks. Existing approaches (fuzz testing, static analysis, LLM-as-a-Verifier) struggle to cover all edge cases, while formal verification requires substantial manual specification and proof engineering.

Method: A unified multi-agent framework (MAGS) that: 1) formalizes and freezes human-audited APIs and safety requirements, 2) translates generated code into Dafny (a verification-aware intermediate representation), 3) repairs violations using verifier feedback, and 4) compiles verified programs back into executable code.

Result: Evaluation on 220 examples (100 CUDA kernels, 100 terminal scripts, 20 robotic-arm tasks) shows 100% success rate in producing programs with non-trivial safety guarantees against frozen specifications. Strong performance across all domains, though failures occur when auto-formalized semantics don't fully capture target behavior.

Conclusion: MAGS demonstrates that automated formal verification is feasible and effective for generating safe programs across diverse domains, addressing the limitations of traditional verification methods while maintaining the rigor of formal guarantees. The framework represents significant progress toward trustworthy AI-generated code.

Abstract: LLM coding agents now generate complex programs at a scale that makes thorough human review increasingly difficult, raising the risk of safety and security failures. Common approaches, including fuzz testing, static analysis, and LLM-as-a-Verifier, can detect many failures but struggle to cover all possible edge cases. Formal verification addresses this by providing machine-checkable guarantees over specified properties, but traditionally demands substantial manual specification and proof engineering. We introduce a unified multi-agent framework, MAGS, that generates executable programs with formal safety guarantees, using Dafny as a verification-aware intermediate representation where safety properties can be mechanically checked. MAGS formalizes and freezes human-audited APIs and safety requirements, translates generated code into Dafny, repairs violations using verifier feedback, and compiles verified programs back into executable code. We evaluate MAGS on 100 CUDA kernels, 100 terminal scripts, and 20 robotic-arm tasks. Across all 220 examples, it achieves a 100% success rate in producing programs with non-trivial safety guarantees against frozen specifications. Independent safety and functional evaluations further show strong performance across all three domains, while revealing failures when the auto-formalized semantics do not fully capture the target behavior.

</details>


### [15] [Closed-World Resolution Against Tool Hallucination in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19425)
*Laxmipriya Ganesh Iyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents often hallucinate tool calls to non-existent tools with undeclared arguments, creating a structural blind spot that existing tool-selection and security methods cannot address.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing defenses for LLM tool calls focus on selecting the right tool or constraining access to real tools, but they fail to address a fundamental problem: LLMs can hallucinate calls to tools that don't exist at all. This creates a structural blind spot where no security gate can reject such calls because they don't refer to any real tool in the registry.

Method: The authors create a five-class taxonomy of tool hallucination (H1-H5) and introduce the Resolution Rung as a training-free, closed-world resolver that serves as a reference point. They prove that hallucination defense must precede any causal gate. They conduct extensive measurements across ten hosted models under two invocation surfaces, measuring 322 genuine hallucinations. They also extend analysis to the Model Context Protocol (MCP) with a second taxonomy (M1-M5) and release the Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB).

Result: Key findings: Fabricated-tool calls concentrate on unconstrained raw-JSON surfaces (34 vs. 3); model scale doesn't help reduce hallucinations (675B model matches 7-8B models). On MCP surfaces, they measured 154 hallucinations, including from frontier models that were clean on single-registry surfaces. Merging multiple servers creates hallucination surfaces that single registries cannot express, due to structural issues with collisions and shadowing.

Conclusion: Tool hallucination is a fundamental structural problem in LLM tool-augmented agents that cannot be addressed by existing approaches. A new class of defenses is needed that operates before any tool-selection gate, specifically targeting hallucinated calls to non-existent tools. The released benchmark enables systematic comparison of different resolution approaches.

Abstract: Tool-augmented large language model (LLM) agents fail in a way no tool-selection or tool-security method addresses: they call tools that do not exist and pass arguments no schema declares. Existing defenses either pick the right tool (selection) or constrain what an agent may do with real tools (gating), both of which presuppose the emitted call refers to a real tool at all. We show this is a structural blind spot: a hallucinated call is by construction not a decision any gate made, so no gate can reject it. This paper is primarily a measurement and benchmark study. We give a five-class taxonomy of tool hallucination (H1-H5) and, as a reference point, the Resolution Rung: a training-free, closed-world resolver (registry membership plus a signature check) whose interest is where it must sit, not what it computes. We prove hallucination defense must precede any causal gate, and characterize the one irreducible residue (borrowed arguments schema-indistinguishable from a valid call). Across ten hosted models under two invocation surfaces we measure 322 genuine hallucinations; fabricated-tool calls concentrate on the unconstrained raw-JSON surface (34 vs. 3), and model scale does not help (a 675B model matches a 7-8B one). We then extend to the Model Context Protocol, where merging several servers into one namespace creates hallucination surfaces a single registry cannot express (a second taxonomy, M1-M5); on the live MCP surface we measure 154 hallucinations, including from frontier models that were clean on the single-registry surface, because collisions and shadowing are structural to the merge. We release the versioned Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB) so any resolver is comparable across submissions.

</details>


### [16] [The syntax and semantics of goals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19448)
*David M. Abel,Mark K. Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Goals are analyzed as compositional cognitive representations that bridge syntax (form) and semantics (meaning), similar to linguistic structures, enabling a systematic study of their expressivity, design constraints, and relationship to rational behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to reframe how goals are conceptualized in cognitive and computer science by treating them as representations with both form and content, highlighting parallels with linguistics/logic and opening up foundational questions about their design properties and expressivity.

Method: The authors synthesize existing work on goal representations, proposing they form points in a broader design space, and analyze goals through the lens of a syntax-semantics interface similar to linguistics.

Result: By distinguishing between the form and meaning of goals, the paper establishes a framework for examining the implicit assumptions about goals, their expressive power, design constraints, and how different representations capture the same behavior.

Conclusion: This representational approach to goals provides a valuable framework for studying higher-level cognition and motivation, isolates dimensions of variation in goal concepts, and connects cognitive science more closely to formal systems like linguistics and logic.

Abstract: In both cognitive science and computer science, goals are conceptualized as cognitive states that flexibly combine with world knowledge to organize and specify purposeful behavior. In this way, goals are compositional representations whose content relates to rational behavior. We here draw attention to goals as representations and their content because it highlights a parallel with other areas in cognitive science - in particular, the syntax-semantics interface in linguistics and logic - while also foregrounding foundational questions about the expressivity, design, and efficiency of different goal representations. For example, goals are typically taken as fixed and imposing constraints on desirable behaviors, but we can also identify constraints on goal representations themselves, such as whether a particular goal language is sufficiently expressive to capture behaviors of interest, or whether different goal representations capture the same behavior. Here, we synthesize work that aims to characterize the properties of different goal representations and suggest these are points of a broader design space. We close by discussing how distinguishing the form and meaning of goals can elucidate the implicit assumptions we make about goals, inform the study of interactions between higher-level cognition and motivation, and isolate axes of variation for different conceptions of goals.

</details>


### [17] [Compositional Reasoning in Language Models under Reinforcement Learning Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19465)
*Yu He,Yingxi Li,Yifei Wang,Ellen Vitercik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-training methods like RL improve LM reasoning but have unclear effects on compositional reasoning. The paper proposes a dependency-graph framework, finds decomposed-to-composed asymmetry in skill transfer, and validates findings with theoretical analysis and practical benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compositional reasoning is critical for real-world problem solving since training data is limited and models must generalize by composing learned skills in new ways. While RL has improved LM reasoning, its effects on compositional reasoning remain poorly understood.

Method: Proposes a dependency-graph framework with three levels of compositionality complexity. Instantiates this framework with data-structure tasks that provide deterministic rewards and clear compositional structure. Conducts empirical studies including length extrapolation, structural distribution shift, and skill transfer analysis.

Result: Finds consistent decomposed-to-composed asymmetry: decomposed-skill training doesn't reliably transfer to composed tasks, but composed-task training transfers more easily back to decomposed tasks. Provides theoretical explanation for this asymmetry and extends findings to real-world tool-calling benchmarks.

Conclusion: The study reveals fundamental limitations in how current post-training methods like RL handle compositional reasoning, particularly showing that skill decomposition doesn't guarantee composition ability. This asymmetry has implications for designing more effective training approaches that can better handle real-world compositional problems.

Abstract: Compositional reasoning is critical for real-world problem solving: since training data is necessarily limited, models must generalize by composing learned skills in new ways. While post-training methods such as reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the reasoning abilities of language models (LMs), their effects on compositional reasoning remain less well understood. We propose a dependency-graph framework to formalize compositional reasoning, yielding three levels of compositionality with increasing complexity. Empirically, we instantiate this framework with data-structure tasks, which provide deterministic reward computation and clear compositional structure. We find a consistent decomposed-to-composed asymmetry: decomposed-skill training does not reliably transfer to composed tasks, whereas composed-task training transfers more readily back to decomposed tasks. We provide theoretical explanation for this asymmetry, and further evaluate compositional generalization under length extrapolation, structural distribution shift, and transfer to tasks requiring unseen skills. Finally, we present a pilot study on real-world tool-calling benchmarks, showing preliminary evidence that the decomposed-to-composed asymmetry can extend to practical settings.

</details>


### [18] [Safety Beyond the Interface: Detecting Harm via Latent States in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19472)
*Alizishaan Khatri,Chiquita Prabhu,Omkar Neogi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lightweight MLP probes trained on LLaMA-3.1-8B activations can detect harmful prompts with high accuracy while significantly reducing latency and compute costs compared to external guardrail models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous systems using LLMs face safety infrastructure challenges: external guardrail models introduce latency/compute overhead and are blind to the model's internal states, creating an assurance gap. The researchers explore whether LLMs already have internal knowledge of harmful content.

Method: Extract activations from LLaMA-3.1-8B and train lightweight MLP classifier probes (12.6M parameters) to detect harmful prompts based on the model's internal states rather than external analysis.

Result: Probes achieve F1 scores of 99% on WildJailbreak, 83% on Beavertails, and 84% on AEGIS 2.0, competitive with guard models 1000x larger while dramatically reducing latency and compute costs.

Conclusion: LLMs contain internal knowledge about harmful content that can be effectively extracted using lightweight probes, offering a more efficient safety solution than external guardrails for resource-constrained deployments.

Abstract: Autonomous systems increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) yet the safety infrastructure surrounding these models introduces latency and compute overhead. This limits utility in resource-constrained, time-critical deployments. Existing external guardrail models remain blind to the model's internal workings, creating a fundamental assurance gap. We ask: does the model already know when the content is harmful? We extract activations from LLaMA-3.1-8B and train lightweight MLP classifier probes (12.6M parameters) to detect harmful prompts. Evaluated on WildJailbreak, Beavertails, and AEGIS 2.0, our probes achieve F1 scores of 99%, 83%, and 84%, respectively competitive with 1000x larger guard models while cutting latency and compute costs.

</details>


### [19] [QVAC Genesis III: A Large-Scale, High-Quality Open Synthetic STEM Corpus for Efficient Language Model Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19513)
*Davide Vitabile,N. Ranjan,Akshay Nambiar,Kamal K. Gupta,Amril Nazir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QVAC Genesis III is a 191.43B-token STEM-focused synthetic corpus that uses targeted teacher distillation with weak student failures/successes to create high-value training data for edge-scale language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a lack of STEM-focused synthetic datasets with high per-token learning value for small models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment, where token budgets are constrained and major organizations use private corpora.

Method: Dual generation strategy: using a weak edge-scale student model, converting its failures into corrective explanations and expanding its successes into contrastive option-level reasoning. Also introduces LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol for answer extraction and validity tracking.

Result: Models trained with QVAC Genesis III outperform both Cosmopedia-v2 trained models and Cosmo-1B model across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E, +21.35% on ARC-C, with Valid Answer Rate up to 99.45%.

Conclusion: QVAC Genesis III addresses the STEM-focused synthetic dataset gap for edge AI models, delivering high-quality training data that significantly improves performance on STEM benchmarks for small, resource-constrained models.

Abstract: High-quality pre-training data is a critical bottleneck for educational and STEM-specific language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment where token budgets are tightly constrained. While major organizations train ever-larger models on private corpora, the open ecosystem lacks STEM-focused synthetic datasets that deliver high per-token learning value efficiently for small models. To address this gap, we introduce QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token, STEM-focused multi-domain synthetic corpus covering 19 domains across several difficulty levels and different educational styles. QVAC Genesis III is built via a dual generation strategy that performs targeted teacher distillation using a weak edge-scale student model as signal: the student's failures are converted into corrective explanations, while its successes are expanded into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. We further introduce an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity. To validate the effectiveness of our QVAC Genesis III data, we conduct controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, showing that models trained with QVAC Genesis III consistently outperform both models trained with the open-source synthetic corpus Cosmopedia-v2 and the publicly released Cosmo-1B model across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C, while reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.

</details>


### [20] [LLM-as-an-Improver: Turning Verification into Better Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19515)
*Akiyoshi Tomihari,Yuma Ichikawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VRR (Verify-Repair-Reselect) framework uses verification feedback to not just rank but also improve LLM-generated solutions through repair and regeneration, outperforming traditional verifier-based selection methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current verifier-based selection methods for LLMs only use verification for ranking and discard feedback after evaluating a fixed candidate pool. The authors investigate whether verification feedback can also improve the candidate solutions themselves rather than just selecting among them.

Method: The paper introduces LLM-as-an-Improver and proposes Verify-Repair-Reselect (VRR) framework. VRR retains the initial winner while generating three complementary alternatives: repaired versions of the winner and runner-up, and a solution based on a new approach. It filters invalid and duplicate candidates using inference-time information and reselects the final answer under original evaluation criteria.

Result: Across diverse models and code-generation/reasoning benchmarks, VRR improves over fixed-pool verifier-based selection in many settings. It can recover correct solutions even when all candidates in the initial pool are incorrect. The method effectively constructs stronger candidates beyond the initial pool.

Conclusion: Verification feedback can serve a broader role in LLM systems - not just for selecting among existing solutions but also for constructing improved candidates. The proposed VRR framework demonstrates that LLMs can function effectively as improvers, enhancing solution quality beyond what's achievable through simple ranking approaches.

Abstract: Verifier-based selection improves LLM performance by generating multiple candidate solutions and using a verifier to select the most promising one. However, existing methods typically treat verification only as a ranking step and discard its feedback once a fixed candidate pool has been evaluated. In this paper, we ask whether verification can also improve the candidate set itself. To this end, we introduce LLM-as-an-Improver and propose Verify--Repair--Reselect (VRR), which uses verification feedback to generate and reselect improved candidates. VRR retains the initial winner while conditionally generating three complementary alternatives: repaired versions of the winner and runner-up, and a solution based on a new approach. It filters invalid and duplicate candidates using only inference-time information and then reselects the final answer under the original evaluation criteria. Across diverse models and code-generation and reasoning benchmarks, VRR improves over fixed-pool verifier-based selection in many settings and can recover correct solutions even when all candidates in the initial pool are incorrect. These results highlight a broader role for LLMs as improvers: verification feedback can not only select among existing solutions but also construct stronger candidates beyond the initial pool.

</details>


### [21] [An Architecture for Long-Horizon Agents: Levels, Ticks and Cascaded Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19519)
*Erik Nijkamp,Anurag Koul,Egor Pakhomov,Bo Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hierarchical agent architecture for long-horizon AI tasks that runs continually across time scales, using bounded file summaries and cascaded intelligence to maintain context beyond any window limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-model agents are increasingly being used for long-duration tasks (days/weeks) that outlive context windows, processes, and human attention intervals. Current systems fail to operate continuously without forgetting, which is essential for true continual learning in long-horizon scenarios.

Method: Proposes a hierarchical architecture with three components: (1) multiple levels indexed by time scale, each maintaining a bounded summary file of the level below; (2) a clocked tick as the unit of autonomous action; and (3) cascaded intelligence where work escalates to more capable models only after failing review.

Result: Tested in a 10-day campaign where an agent reproduced a published reinforcement-learning result with only daily human attendance. The agent successfully: (1) maintained context across all resets and session boundaries, (2) used early-written operating knowledge to change later behavior without weight changes, and (3) identified where learned components would fit in such systems.

Conclusion: Continual learning for long-horizon agents requires a substrate that outlives context and processes, with the learning components belonging in the review checks that the agent harness already performs, rather than requiring model weight changes.

Abstract: Language-model agents are increasingly asked to carry out work spanning days or weeks, such as an operations remediation or a research programme. Such a task outlives any context window, any process and any interval at which a person can attend. In this paper, we argue that a long-horizon agent must run continually without forgetting before it can learn continually. This ability lies in the harness around the model rather than in the model itself. We derive seven bottlenecks from the long-horizon setting and answer them with a hierarchical architecture of three parts: (i) levels indexed by time scale, each keeping a bounded file summarising the level below; (ii) a clocked tick as the unit of autonomous action; and (iii) cascaded intelligence, where work is escalated to a more capable model only after failing review. We report on a ten-day campaign in which an agent built on this architecture reproduced a published reinforcement-learning result with a human attending once a day, and show (1) the agent kept the thread across every context reset and session boundary of the campaign, (2) operating knowledge written early changed later behaviour with no change to model weights, and (3) where learned components would enter such a system. Overall, our experience suggests continual learning for these agents needs a substrate outliving every context and process, and the checks the harness already runs are where a learner belongs.

</details>


### [22] [EconSkills: Studying Skill Transfer and Retrieval for Web Agents on Live Economic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19523)
*Yinzhu Quan,Zefang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EconSkills: A framework that distills verified web trajectories into reusable skills for economic data retrieval, enabling transfer learning across tasks through parameterized procedures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Web agents frequently revisit the same sites but current evaluations discard procedures learned from previous successful interactions, wasting accumulated knowledge and requiring redundant learning.

Method: Create a skill library that distills EconWebArena trajectories into parameterized standard operating procedures with placeholders. Skills include scope, navigation procedures, site-specific guidance, verification checks, and recovery steps. Evaluate both controlled transfer (matching skills to tasks) and library-scale retrieval.

Result: Matched skills improve success rates and reduce steps compared to no-skill prompting. Abstraction is more effective than replaying raw trajectories. At library scale, retrieval competes with no-skill baseline and performs best on directly covered tasks. Browser trajectories show when procedural guidance shortens navigation vs. when semantic verification is still needed.

Conclusion: Reusable economic web procedures can transfer across task instances. The framework establishes concrete design targets for coverage-aware skill selection and context delivery in web automation systems.

Abstract: Web agents often revisit the same sites, yet most evaluations discard the procedures learned in earlier successful interactions. We introduce EconSkills, a skill library and evaluation framework that distills verified EconWebArena trajectories into parameterized standard operating procedures for retrieving live economic data. Each skill records its scope, navigation procedure, site-specific guidance, verification checks, and recovery steps while replacing source-instance values with placeholders. EconSkills separates two questions: whether a known relevant procedure transfers to a held-out task, and whether an agent can retain that benefit when selecting from a library. In controlled transfer, matched skills improve success over no-skill prompting and require fewer steps on paired successes, while abstraction is substantially more effective than replaying raw trajectories. At library scale, retrieval is competitive with the no-skill baseline overall and performs best on directly covered tasks; coverage-stratified outcomes show that approximate matches on uncovered tasks offset these gains. Browser trajectories further identify when procedural guidance shortens portal-specific navigation and when semantic verification remains necessary. These results establish that reusable economic web procedures can transfer across task instances and provide a concrete design target for coverage-aware selection and context delivery.

</details>


### [23] [A Unified Evaluation Framework for Trustworthy Large Language Models, Agentic AI, and Multimodal Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19524)
*Shaina Raza,Ahmed Y. Radwan,Imran Liaquat,Kathryn Hume*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified framework for AI trustworthiness assessment that connects output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal evaluation across eight dimensions with safety-critical overrides and meta-evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark scores alone are insufficient for assessing trustworthiness of modern AI systems (LLMs, agentic systems, MLLMs). Current evaluation approaches lack interpretability for development and oversight, and need to connect technical assessment with governance frameworks and regulatory requirements.

Method: Proposes a unified framework with eight trustworthiness dimensions (capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, efficiency). Preserves system-specific metrics while mapping to common performance bands with uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence. Includes meta-evaluation layer for validity, reliability, reproducibility, and safety-critical overrides to prevent masking failures. Maps to governance frameworks and regulatory requirements.

Result: Framework provides structured basis for assessing both system performance and credibility of evaluation evidence. Enables multidimensional profiles to expose strengths/weaknesses while preventing critical failures from being masked by aggregate scores.

Conclusion: The framework addresses limitations of benchmark-only assessment by providing comprehensive, interpretable evaluation that connects technical measurement with oversight needs. Empirical validation across deployment contexts is identified as essential next step.

Abstract: Benchmark scores alone provide an incomplete basis for assessing the trustworthiness of modern artificial intelligence systems. Large language models (LLMs), agentic systems, and multimodal models (MLLMs) require different forms of assessment, yet their evaluation evidence must remain interpretable for development and oversight. We propose a unified framework that connects output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal assessment through eight trustworthiness dimensions: capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, and efficiency. The framework preserves system-specific metrics while mapping native measurements to common performance bands, accompanied by uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence. A meta-evaluation layer examines the validity, reliability, and reproducibility of the evaluation itself. Multidimensional profiles expose strengths and weaknesses, while safety-critical overrides prevent aggregate scores from masking critical failures. Mappings to governance frameworks, international standards, and European Union regulatory requirements connect technical assessment with oversight needs. The framework provides a structured basis for assessing both system performance and the credibility of the evidence supporting it, with empirical validation across deployment contexts remaining an essential next step.

</details>


### [24] [Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19526)
*Xinghong Fu,Aravinth Kulanthaivelu,Yutaro Yamada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIFT: A simple, sample-efficient self-improvement framework that uses LLM-as-a-judge pairwise comparisons and regularized Bradley-Terry models within lightweight tree search to significantly reduce computational costs while improving coding performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to coding agent self-improvement are costly and compute-intensive, particularly due to the bottleneck of evaluating candidate self-modifications by re-running benchmark tasks. There's a need for more efficient methods that work under strict budget constraints.

Method: Recursive Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search (SIFT) uses LLM-as-a-judge signals to perform pairwise comparisons between candidate patches, aggregates win-loss records with a regularized Bradley-Terry model, and uses resulting strength scores for rank-based parent sampling in a lightweight disaggregated tree search. Expensive downstream evaluations are reserved only for most promising nodes.

Result: SIFT outperforms existing tree-search based self-evolution frameworks on the full Polyglot benchmark while requiring significantly lower resources in terms of CPU hours, wall clock time, and API cost.

Conclusion: SIFT demonstrates that efficient self-improvement for coding agents is possible by using fast LLM-based evaluation signals to guide search, significantly reducing computational overhead while maintaining or improving performance on coding benchmarks.

Abstract: Coding agents can recursively modify their own implementations, forming a loop of self-improvement. While prior work shows this can boost performance on coding benchmarks, existing approaches are costly and compute-intensive. We introduce a simple, sample-efficient self-improvement framework that significantly improves coding performance under strict budget constraints. We identify evaluation of candidate self-modifications as the main runtime bottleneck since prior approaches estimate their effectiveness by re-running a subset of benchmark tasks with the modified agent, which is time-consuming. We introduce Recursive Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search (SIFT), which augments these downstream task evaluations with an LLM-as-a-judge signal that performs pairwise comparisons between candidate patches, where the win-loss record is aggregated with a regularized Bradley-Terry model, and the resulting strength scores drive rank-based parent sampling inside a lightweight disaggregated tree search. Expensive downstream task evaluations are reserved only for the most promising nodes. Using a fully disaggregated tree search pipeline, the judge scores provide intermediate signal to guide exploration on promising candidate patches without being bottlenecked by slow evaluation runs. SIFT outperforms existing tree-search based self-evolution frameworks on the full Polyglot benchmark with significantly lower resource requirements in terms of CPU hours, wall clock time, and API cost.

</details>


### [25] [When Hiring Becomes Agent-Mediated: Evaluating Access and Recurrence in Two-Agent Résumé Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19530)
*Jian Gao,Hang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-agent hiring screening (employer vs. candidate agents exchanging evidence) advances more applications than traditional one-call résumé screening and changes decisions in both directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated résumé screening treats hiring as a static, one-way judgment, but real hiring is bilateral with candidates presenting evidence. The paper explores whether modeling this interaction with two agents improves screening.

Method: The study compares traditional one-call screening (single judgment on résumé-job pair) with a two-agent alternative where employer-side and candidate-side agents exchange evidence and update judgments. Tests were conducted on 600 constructed résumé-job pairs using GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7.

Result: Two-agent screening advances more applications (33.3% to 39.3% for GPT-5.5; 34.0% to 35.5% for Claude Opus). It also increases pass rates for borderline cases (4.5% to 26.2% for GPT-5.5; 6.5% to 16.1% for Opus). The approach changes decisions in both directions - rejecting some one-call advances while advancing others. Two-agent-only selections are less reproducible than shared selections.

Conclusion: As hiring becomes agent-mediated on both sides, the screening procedure itself (not just the AI model) significantly shapes who reaches human review and how reliably that access recurs, suggesting the need to consider bilateral interaction in automated screening systems.

Abstract: Hiring is bilateral: employers assess fit, while candidates present and defend evidence of their qualifications. Yet résumé screening, the first gate, is commonly automated as a static, one-call judgment over a résumé-job pair. We study a two-agent alternative in which employer-side and candidate-side agents represent these roles, exchange evidence, and update their judgments before deciding who advances. We compare procedures on 600 constructed résumé-job pairs using GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. Two-agent screening advances more applications (33.3% to 39.3% for GPT-5.5; 34.0% to 35.5% for Opus 4.7). Across three runs on the common 191-pair borderline pool, pass-instance rates rise from 4.5% to 26.2% and from 6.5% to 16.1%, respectively. This is not a uniform relaxation: two-agent screening rejects applications one-call advances, changing decisions in both directions.
