At similar pass volumes, the procedures advance different applications, and no one-call threshold recovers applications consistently selected by two-agent screening. Among discovery-selected cases re-executed in fresh runs, two-agent-only selections recur less often than shared selections, clearly under GPT-5.5 and less certainly under Opus 4.7, while a separate one-call follow-up shows no comparable decline. As hiring becomes agent-mediated on both sides, the screening procedure, not only the model behind it, shapes who reaches human review and how reliably that access recurs.

</details>


### [26] [Continual Enterprise World Model Discovery in Dynamic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19551)
*Shambhavi Mishra,David Vazquez,Perouz Taslakian,Marco Pedersoli,Jose Dolz,Issam H. Laradji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An agent discovers hidden business rules in enterprise systems by interacting with records, building a world model that adapts to rule changes, outperforming lookup-based approaches by up to 8.98 IoU points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise systems have complex business rules (updating fields, creating records, starting approvals) that are organization-specific and evolve over time. Agents working in such systems cannot predict the results of their actions without knowing these hidden, changing rules.

Method: The paper introduces Continual Enterprise World Model Discovery where an agent starts without knowledge of business rules and discovers them through interaction. The agent builds and revises a world model as rules change. Evaluation uses EnterpriseWorldShift benchmark on a live ServiceNow environment with 9 tables, 25 hidden rules, and 600 evaluation actions, testing four versions of the same enterprise world with rule modifications.

Result: The Continual Discovery Agent (CDA) successfully builds and carries world models across changing environments. It predicts effects of hidden rules more accurately than lookup-based approaches (prior work) by up to 8.98 IoU points, and can answer queries using its own model without querying the running system.

Conclusion: Continual world model discovery enables agents to effectively learn and adapt to hidden, evolving business rules in enterprise systems, improving prediction accuracy and reducing reliance on real-time system queries compared to traditional lookup approaches.

Abstract: In an enterprise system, updating one field can set another, create a record, or start an approval. These effects are produced by business rules that are not built into the platform but written by each organization and revised over time. An agent working in such a system cannot predict the result of its own actions without knowing these rules. We study continual enterprise world model discovery, where an agent starts without knowledge of these business rules and discovers them by interacting with records and observing the outcomes. From those observations it builds a world model, which it revises as the rules change. To evaluate this, we introduce EnterpriseWorldShift, built on a live ServiceNow environment with nine tables, 25 hidden rules and 600 evaluation actions. It presents four versions of the same enterprise world, with the tables and records held fixed while a rule is modified, then added, then removed, so that discovery, revision, extension and retirement are each tested in turn. Our Continual Discovery Agent (CDA) builds such a model and carries it from one world to the next. It predicts the effects of the hidden rules more accurately than looking them up for each question, the approach taken by prior work, by up to 8.98 IoU points, and it answers from its own model without querying the running system.

</details>


### [27] [SIMLIFE: Pattern Understanding for Long-Horizon Human-Agent Partnership](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19610)
*Run Peng,Zinnia Nie,Jing Ding,Yinpei Dai,Yichi Zhang,Zengqing Wu,Yao Fu,Ziqiao Ma,Jiayuan Mao,Joyce Chai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SimLife is a simulation platform for studying long-term household behavior, with SimLife-BP benchmark evaluating AI models' ability to understand behavioral patterns over extended periods, revealing current models' limitations in comprehensive rule inference and adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable embodied agents to truly understand human behavior over long horizons, including how routines form, why they repeat, and when they change, which requires inferring latent behavioral rules from extended observations of everyday life.

Method: Created SimLife platform for simulating long-term household life with visual observations, action logs, and synthetic dialogues with audio. Built SimLife-BP benchmark containing 106 episodes (avg 15.49 hours/38.57 in-game days) with 1,439 question-answer pairs evaluating direct, counterfactual, noisy, and inverse reasoning with rule hints.

Result: Current models achieve only surface-level prediction without comprehensive rule understanding, rely on frequency-based heuristics rather than evidence-based if-then reasoning, and struggle to adapt when behavioral patterns change, showing long-context pattern understanding remains a major bottleneck.

Conclusion: SimLife enables broader study of memory, personalization, adaptation, and long-horizon planning in human-AI interaction, while identifying long-context pattern understanding as a key challenge for future embodied agents.

Abstract: Understanding humans over long horizons requires agents to infer not only what people need in the moment, but also how routines form, why they repeat, and when they change. We introduce SimLife, a scalable platform for simulating long-term household life with rich visual observations, ground-truth action logs, and synthetic dialogues with audio. Built on SimLife, SimLife-BP evaluates long-context pattern understanding: the ability to infer latent behavioral rules from weeks or months of everyday observations. The benchmark contains 106 episodes averaging 15.49 hours and 38.57 in-game days, and 1,439 question-answer pairs. Each task probes direct, counterfactual, noisy, and inverse reasoning under different levels of rule hints. Evaluating frontier models and architectures, we find that current models often achieve surface-level prediction without comprehensive rule understanding, rely on frequency-based heuristics rather than if-then reasoning over evidence, and struggle to adapt when behavioral patterns change. These findings suggest that long-context pattern understanding remains a major bottleneck for future embodied agents, while SimLife opens a broader space for studying memory, personalization, adaptation, and long-horizon planning in everyday human-AI interaction.

</details>


### [28] [From Intent to Action: Benchmarking LLM Safety in Vehicle Voice Command Authorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19630)
*Diba Afroze,Xingli Zhang,Yazhou Tu,Xiali Hei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper introduces a benchmark for evaluating LLMs' pre-action safety decisions in vehicle voice assistants, finding that even best models make critical errors and need independent safety layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used in vehicle voice assistants, creating safety-critical authorization problems where systems must make correct decisions before executing commands, but prior evaluations don't adequately isolate these pre-action decisions across key factors like speaker role, authentication status, vehicle state, and tool availability.

Method: Introduced a 202-scenario benchmark with Reference Decisions using a seven-class taxonomy, then evaluated five LLMs (two local open-weight models and three API-based) using Decision Alignment and safety-specific error metrics, including controlled ablation studies.

Result: Alignment ranged from 40.1% for Llama 3.2 3B to 89.1% for Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview, with API models scoring 83.2-89.1%. Even best models produced 2-3 False Executes among 161 non-execution scenarios, with persistent errors in confirmation and manual-control decisions. Structured authorization improved alignment but didn't eliminate False Executes.

Conclusion: Structured LLM decisions are insufficient as a standalone safety mechanism for vehicle voice assistants. Deployment requires an independent enforcement layer that verifies tool permissions and vehicle-state constraints before invoking any vehicle function.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into vehicle voice assistants. But linking natural-language requests to vehicle functions creates a safety-critical authorization problem. Before executing a command, the system must choose whether to execute, refuse, clarify, require confirmation, defer to manual control, trigger an emergency response, or make no tool call. To our knowledge, prior evaluations do not isolate this pre-action decision across speaker role, authentication status, vehicle state, and tool availability. We introduce a 202-scenario benchmark with Reference Decisions under a seven-class taxonomy. We evaluate two local open-weight models and three API-based LLMs using Decision Alignment and safety-specific error metrics. Alignment ranges from 40.1% for Llama 3.2 3B to 89.1% for Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview. The API-based models score between 83.2% and 89.1%, with no statistically significant differences among them. Even these models produce two to three False Executes among 161 non-execution scenarios, and persistent errors remain in confirmation and manual-control decisions. A controlled Llama 3.2 3B ablation increases alignment to 40.1% under the structured authorization policy, versus 28.2-29.2% under schema-only and generic-safety baselines, but it does not eliminate False Executes. Structured LLM decisions are therefore insufficient as a standalone safety mechanism, and deployment requires an independent enforcement layer that verifies tool permissions and vehicle-state constraints before invoking any vehicle function.

</details>


### [29] [Reach or Solve? Attributing Agentic RL Gains with Checkpoint Handoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19636)
*Xuan Liu,Jingbin Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "checkpoint handoff" to separate endpoint success in RL-trained language models into REACH (ability to arrive at success-proximal states) and SOLVE (ability to finish from those states), showing RL improves both components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When evaluating RL-trained language model agents in sequential decision-making tasks, traditional endpoint success metrics conflate two different capabilities: (1) the agent's ability to navigate to promising states (REACH) and (2) its ability to solve tasks from those states (SOLVE). Since RL agents operate in closed loops where their actions determine future states, comparing SFT and RL checkpoints becomes confounded by different state distributions.

Method: The authors introduce "checkpoint handoff" - an evaluation protocol that clones states reached by one checkpoint (the reacher) and hands them to another checkpoint (the solver) without retraining. By crossing reacher and solver roles between SFT and RL checkpoints, they separate endpoint success into REACH (frequency of arriving at states near success) and SOLVE (frequency of completing tasks from identical cloned states).

Result: Across two benchmarks (ALFWorld and another) and two independent pipelines, the reacher-solver interaction is consistently positive. RL improves both REACH and SOLVE components. On ALFWorld specifically, RL improves both terms, and the SFT solver never succeeds where the RL solver fails. The independent REACH and SOLVE gaps accurately predict the aggregate interaction pattern.

Conclusion: Checkpoint handoff provides a more nuanced evaluation framework for long-horizon language model agents, separating navigation and completion capabilities. This reveals that RL training improves both components rather than just overall success rates, and that state distributions matter significantly when comparing different training paradigms.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning now trains language-model agents that act over dozens of steps in live environments. The gains are large, and they are read as better decision-making. An agent in a closed loop writes its own inputs. Each observation follows from its own earlier actions, so the states it meets late in an episode are partly of its own making. An SFT checkpoint and an RL checkpoint are then scored from different states, even on identical tasks. Endpoint success mixes two changes: where the agent arrives, and what it does once it is there. Restricting the comparison to states both policies reach does not separate them. That restriction selects on an outcome, and in our data it flips the sign of the effect. We introduce checkpoint handoff, an evaluation protocol that clones a state one released checkpoint reached and hands it to another, with no retraining. Crossing a reacher role and a solver role over SFT and RL splits an endpoint gain into REACH and SOLVE. REACH is how often a policy arrives at a state the environment confirms is a fixed number of actions from success. SOLVE is how often it finishes from an identical cloned state. Across two benchmarks and two independently released pipelines, the reacher by solver interaction is positive in all five conditions. An RL history is worth more to an RL solver than the same history is to an SFT solver. On ALFWorld, RL improves both terms, and the SFT solver never succeeds where the RL solver fails. Independent REACH and SOLVE gaps predict the aggregate interaction. Handoff asks only that one checkpoint's history can be replayed under another, so long-horizon evaluation can report arrival and completion beside endpoint success.

</details>


### [30] [ScientistTwo: Pioneering the Human Knowledge Frontier with Autonomous AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19644)
*Jaehyun Nam,Jinsung Yoon,Yanzhou Pan,Yubo Wang,Rui Meng,Parthasarathy Ranganathan,Tomas Pfister*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ScientistTwo is a fully autonomous multi-agent AI framework that independently conducts end-to-end scientific research, generating publishable papers and executable codebases that outperform human state-of-the-art solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ultimate vision for AI in science is problem-driven autonomous research where AI can independently navigate scientific landscapes, identify knowledge boundaries, and expand frontiers of knowledge without human intervention.

Method: A fully autonomous multi-agent framework that takes initial problems as input, establishes baselines, formulates novel hypotheses, coordinates specialized agents, conducts experiments with diverse datasets and metrics, refines methodologies through automated ablation studies, and validates findings via a closed-loop simulated peer-review rebuttal engine.

Result: ScientistTwo autonomously generates expert-level, publishable papers and fully verified executable codebases. Its solutions consistently outperform human state-of-the-art models and achieve higher average review ratings than human-authored papers under automated AI review agents when benchmarked against top-tier conference papers (ICLR, ICML, NeurIPS).

Conclusion: ScientistTwo represents more than just an assistive tool - it's an autonomous scientific pioneer capable of pushing the frontiers of human discovery by independently conducting high-quality research that meets and exceeds human scientific standards.

Abstract: Scientific discovery is defined by the ability to identify the boundaries of existing knowledge and venture into unexplored territory. The ultimate vision for AI in science is problem-driven autonomous research: given a fundamental challenge by a human expert, the AI independently navigates the scientific landscape, uncovers theoretical and empirical bottlenecks, and systematically expands the frontier of knowledge. In this paper, we introduce ScientistTwo, a fully autonomous multi-agent framework designed to realize this vision. Specifically, ScientistTwo takes an initial problem as input, establishes state-of-the-art baselines, formulates novel hypotheses, and coordinates specialized agents to orchestrate an end-to-end discovery cycle without human intervention. Moreover, the framework rigorously conducts experiments using diverse datasets and metrics, refines methodologies through automated ablation studies, and validates research findings via a closed-loop simulated peer-review rebuttal engine. To evaluate ScientistTwo's capabilities against the highest standards of human scientific achievement, we benchmark it across papers accepted at top-tier conferences such as ICLR, ICML, and NeurIPS. As a result, ScientistTwo autonomously generates expert-level, publishable papers and fully verified, executable codebases. Its solutions consistently outperform human state-of-the-art models, and achieve higher average review ratings than human-authored papers under automated AI review agents. These results show that ScientistTwo is not merely an assistive tool but an autonomous scientific pioneer capable of pushing the frontiers of human discovery. Project website: https://scientist-two.github.io/

</details>


### [31] [When2Think: Learning Difficulty-Aware Length Control for Efficient Hybrid Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19671)
*Jaejun Shim,HyunJin Kim,Young Jin Kim,JinYeong Bak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: When2Think is a post-training framework for Large Reasoning Models that dynamically allocates computation based on problem difficulty, using Instance-level Difficulty-Aware Control to balance accuracy and efficiency without requiring learned reward models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) exhibit systematic inefficiency - they overthink easy problems and underthink hard ones. Existing approaches with uniform penalties or rigid routing cause accuracy loss on hard instances when trying to improve efficiency.

Method: When2Think introduces Instance-level Difficulty-Aware Control (IDAC), a reward-shaping mechanism using pre-computed reference statistics (accuracy and token usage) to regulate reasoning depth. Combined with verifier-based rewards and batch-wise standardized advantages, IDAC enables stable critic-free optimization without learned reward models or online reference-model queries.

Result: Experiments on mathematical benchmarks show improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs: on AIME24, Pass@3 increased by 10.0% while token usage reduced by 27.9% relative to base model. On AIME25, When2Think achieved 40.0% Pass@3, outperforming compression and routing-only baselines.

Conclusion: When2Think effectively learns when to use System 1 (direct answering) versus System 2 (extended reasoning), enabling instance-adaptive computation allocation that improves both accuracy and efficiency compared to existing approaches.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve strong performance on complex tasks but exhibit systematic inefficiency: they often overthink easy problems and underthink hard ones. Existing approaches based on uniform length penalties or rigid routing incur an efficiency tax, trading reduced computation on easy instances for accuracy loss on hard instances. We formulate efficient reasoning as an instance-adaptive computation allocation problem and propose When2Think, a post-training framework for hybrid reasoning that dynamically allocates computation based on problem difficulty. Our method introduces Instance-level Difficulty-Aware Control (IDAC), a reward-shaping mechanism that leverages pre-computed reference statistics (accuracy and token usage) to regulate reasoning depth. Combined with verifier-based rewards and batch-wise standardized advantages, IDAC enables stable critic-free optimization without learned reward models or online reference-model queries. When2Think encourages direct answering on easy instances while preserving extended reasoning on hard instances, thereby learning when to use System 1 (NoThink) versus System 2 (Think). Experiments on mathematical benchmarks demonstrate improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs: on AIME24, Pass@3 increases by 10.0% while token usage is reduced by 27.9% relative to the base model, and on AIME25, When2Think achieves 40.0% Pass@3, outperforming compression and routing-only baselines.

</details>


### [32] [AutoData: Agentic Search for Pre-training Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19754)
*Yan Meng,Dhruv Srikanth,Bingchen Zhao,Zhengyao Jiang,Yuxiang Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoData is an LLM agent that autonomously discovers data selection algorithms through iterative search and execution feedback, outperforming human-designed curation pipelines and showing transferable improvements to larger scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents focus on automating model and training code optimization, but data selection remains largely manual and outside the agentic optimization loop. The paper aims to extend autonomous research to data engineering by framing pre-training data selection as a problem of heuristic engineering over document features.

Method: AutoData searches directly over executable selection algorithms in a rich program space including scoring, stratification, and stochastic selection rules. It uses iterative refinement with validation feedback from a proxy model, automatically discovering feature interactions through overnight searches.

Result: AutoData discovers selection algorithms that outperform existing human-designed curation pipelines. Despite being searched only on a small proxy model, the discovered algorithms transfer effectively to larger scales and improve the downstream CORE metric.

Conclusion: Data engineering can be successfully treated as an agentic machine learning problem, extending autonomous research capabilities from model and training-code optimization to the data selection domain, with discovered algorithms showing strong generalization across different scales.

Abstract: LLM agents have recently shown promise in automating machine learning engineering by editing model and training code under execution feedback. Data, however, remains largely outside this agentic optimisation loop. We frame pre-training data selection as heuristic engineering over per-document features, i.e., lexical statistics, categorical labels, and perplexity. We introduce AutoData, an agent that searches directly over executable selection algorithms. Unlike prior data mixture methods that optimise weights over a fixed set of domains, AutoData searches a richer program space of scoring, stratification, and stochastic selection rules, discovering feature interactions automatically by iteratively refining algorithms with validation feedback from a proxy model. Within an overnight search, AutoData discovers a selection algorithm that outperforms existing human-designed curation pipelines. Despite being searched only on this small proxy, the discovered recipe transfers to larger scales and improves the downstream metric CORE. These results suggest that data engineering can be treated as an agentic machine learning problem, extending autonomous research from model and training-code optimization to the data.

</details>


### [33] [Rethinking Multi-Agent Collaboration: When More Is Less](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19759)
*Yishuo Yuan,Yibo Wu,Yihan Zhang,Minyuan Sun,Shenliang Li,Xinkai Ma,Yifan Li,Jiaheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-agent collaboration only benefits long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies, not tightly coupled workflows. SAIGE introduces a semantic-aware incremental graph evolution approach that achieves context efficiency without consistent improvement from agent scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With rapid advances in large language models and single-agent systems, there's a critical question of when multi-agent collaboration actually provides value. As individual agents become more capable, multi-agent collaboration faces diminishing returns while increasing context overhead, requiring systematic analysis of when collaboration is beneficial.

Method: The paper proposes SAIGE (Semantic-Aware Incremental Graph Evolution), a lightweight multi-agent collaboration mechanism that models collaboration as a dynamically evolving graph. Nodes represent agent instances spawned on demand, and edges encode semantic dependencies established through content-based information retrieval.

Result: Experiments on long-horizon, complex task benchmarks show SAIGE achieves favorable trade-off between context efficiency and task performance. Scaling the agent pool or deepening recursion levels doesn't consistently improve outcomes. Multi-agent collaboration only benefits specific task structures.

Conclusion: Multi-agent superiority is bounded by task structure rather than universal - more agents don't necessarily make a system more intelligent. Collaboration systematically benefits long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies, while single-agent systems remain superior for tightly coupled, sequential workflows.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of large language models and single-agent harnesses has reshaped the landscape of autonomous systems, raising a critical question of when multi-agent collaboration offers genuine value. As individual agent capabilities continue to scale, multi-agent collaboration faces diminishing returns while incurring growing context overhead. Through systematic analysis, we delineate the capability boundaries of multi-agent collaboration relative to single-agent alternatives, showing that it confers systematic benefits specifically in long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies, while single-agent harnesses remain superior in tightly coupled, sequential workflows. Building on these insights, we propose SAIGE, a lightweight multi-agent collaboration mechanism based on Semantic-Aware Incremental Graph Evolution. SAIGE models collaboration as a dynamically evolving graph, where nodes are agent instances spawned on demand and edges encode semantic dependencies established through content-based information retrieval. Experiments on long-horizon, complex task benchmarks show that SAIGE achieves a favorable trade-off between context efficiency and task performance, and that scaling the agent pool or deepening the recursion level does not consistently improve outcomes. Our findings suggest that multi-agent superiority is bounded by task structure rather than universal, and that more agents do not necessarily make a system more intelligent.

</details>


### [34] [TorchCraft: Unified binder design by inverting an all-atom structure predictor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19770)
*TorchCraft Team,Yu Liu,Zhouhanyu Shen,Zhengyi Li,Xikun Huang,Jiaqi Liu,Shuxian Gao,Qilin Yu,Xiayan Qin,Yucheng Zhang,Mingchen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TorchCraft is a unified binder-design framework that optimizes sequences through frozen all-atom predictors like AlphaFold 3, enabling design of various binder formats without post hoc sequence redesign.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While all-atom structure predictors like AlphaFold can model molecular interactions, leveraging their learned structural priors for practical binder design remains challenging. Current methods struggle to effectively reuse these powerful predictive models for designing functional binders across different molecular formats.

Method: TorchCraft uses a frozen all-atom predictor (AlphaFold 3) and optimizes sequence logits through gradient-based optimization. It combines confidence, contact, geometric, and sequence-prior objectives within a unified optimization procedure implemented in TorchFold, supporting multiple binder formats including minibinders, VHHs, cyclic peptides, and ligand-binding proteins.

Result: TorchCraft successfully generated minibinders and VHHs that showed experimentally measured binding across four targets in each format, without needing post hoc sequence redesign. Computational benchmarks also demonstrated applicability to cyclic peptides and ligand-conditioned pocket design.

Conclusion: TorchCraft extends predictor inversion to multiple binder formats and molecular contexts, providing a common framework for reusing all-atom structural priors in design, enabling practical application of powerful structure predictors for diverse binder development.

Abstract: All-atom structure predictors model diverse molecular interactions, but using their learned structural priors for binder design remains challenging. Here we present TorchCraft, a unified binder-design framework that optimizes sequence logits through a frozen all-atom predictor. Implemented in TorchFold, TorchCraft combines confidence, contact, geometric, and sequence-prior objectives within a shared optimization procedure for minibinders, framework-conditioned VHHs, cyclic peptides, and ligand-binding proteins. Using pretrained AlphaFold 3 weights, TorchCraft generated representative minibinders and VHHs with experimentally measured binding across four targets in each format, without post hoc sequence redesign. Computational benchmarks further demonstrated the framework's applicability to cyclic peptides and ligand-conditioned pocket design. TorchCraft extends predictor inversion to multiple binder formats and molecular contexts, providing a common framework for reusing all-atom structural priors in design.

</details>


### [35] [Integrating knowledge from case reports: a medical ontology based multimodal information system with structured summary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19775)
*Shuyu Guo,Lan Huang,Yichen Liu,Hanbin Ma,Tian Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal information system that structures and organizes medical case reports with clinical summaries, medical images, and biomedical entities to improve rare disease diagnosis through better search and retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional keyword-based retrieval tools are inefficient for accessing relevant information from millions of unstructured medical case reports in PubMed, despite their crucial role in documenting rare diseases, diagnostics, and treatments.

Method: Developed a comprehensive multimodal information system integrating structured clinical summaries, medical images, and biomedical named entities from 52,949 open-access case reports (2000-2021), organized using a well-structured medical ontology.

Result: Created a powerful search and browsing interface that assists junior clinicians in effectively retrieving relevant cases, potentially improving identification and diagnosis of rare diseases.

Conclusion: The multimodal system addresses the limitations of traditional keyword-based approaches by providing structured access to case report information, enhancing clinical decision support for rare disease diagnosis.

Abstract: Published medical case reports serve as a crucial medical information carrier, documenting discoveries in rare diseases, diagnostic methods, and innovative treatments. Despite the wealth of clinical knowledge in millions of case reports in the public medicine literature database (PubMed), accessing relevant information efficiently is hindered by the limitations of traditional keyword-based retrieval tools on unstructured and diverse case reports. To address the above issues, we introduce a comprehensive multimodal information system for case reports integrating structured clinical summaries of patients including medical images and biomedical named entities from 52949 open-access case reports published from 2000 to 2021. The multimodal essential information is organized in a well-structured medical ontology. Also, a powerful interface for searching and browsing case reports is designed to assist junior clinicians in retrieving cases effectively and improving the identification and diagnosis of rare diseases.

</details>


### [36] [Contagion on the Trading Floor: How Adversarial Signals Spread in Multi-Agent Trading Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19789)
*Qi Rong Sua,Junhao Dong,Nguyen Duc Thai,Yuqing Wen,Cheston Tan,Yew-Soon Ong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based multi-agent trading systems are vulnerable to black-box poisoning attacks through social media feeds, but proper system design can improve robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to investigate the security vulnerabilities of LLM-based multi-agent trading systems, which are increasingly used in quantitative finance but whose robustness to adversarial attacks remains largely unstudied.

Method: The authors introduce GMATS framework to model multi-agent trading systems, create black-box poisoning attacks that inject adversarial content into social media feeds, and develop contagion metrics to track attack propagation through the system.

Result: Experiments show that simple input-only attacks can significantly degrade trading performance (reducing Sharpe ratios), but properly designed multi-agent topologies and coordinator prompts can mitigate these attacks and improve system robustness.

Conclusion: LLM trading systems are vulnerable to adversarial attacks through social media inputs, but architectural choices and prompt engineering can enhance their security and resilience against such threats.

Abstract: Multi-agent trading systems built on large language models (LLMs) are beginning to appear in quantitative finance, yet their robustness to adversarial inputs is largely unknown. We study the vulnerability of LLM trading stacks to black-box, input-only attacks that enter solely via admissible social-media feeds. We introduce the Generic Multi-Agent Trading System (GMATS), a framework that captures modern multiagent trading architectures and instantiate a class of black-box poisoning attackers that treat an LLM as a post generator and inject budget-constrained, plausibly benign social-media content into the analyst's evidence stream. We define contagion metrics that trace how adversarial content propagates through the stack, including belief-shift scores at analyst and coordinator layers and attack-clean deltas on standard backtest metrics. Experiments on a safe offline benchmark with historical market and social data show that even simple input-only attackers can materially degrade risk-return profiles, sharply reducing Sharpe ratios. At the same time, we find that suitably designed multi-agent topologies and coordinator prompts can dampen adversarial shocks and improve average robustness under identical poisoning budgets.

</details>


### [37] [Steering Equilibrium Selection in Regularized Self-Play via the Reference Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19820)
*Luis Leal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers show that in regularized self-play for two-player zero-sum games, the reference policy can be used to intentionally steer convergence toward specific Nash equilibria in the polytope of value-equivalent equilibria, enabling targeted equilibrium selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regularized self-play (used in DeepNash's Stratego) converges to Nash equilibria through entropy regularization, but when there are multiple value-equivalent equilibria, the regularizer silently selects the maximum-entropy member by default. The key question is whether the reference policy can be deliberately used to choose specific equilibria rather than accepting the default selection.

Method: The method involves anchoring the reference policy at a target equilibrium member and refining through self-play. Experiments were conducted on five exactly solvable games plus a 2-D polytope using exact best responses and equivalence tests across independent seeds. The approach follows the reach-weighted I-projection principle and includes analysis of limitations like off-manifold references, boundary targets, and curvature effects.

Result: Steering successfully converges to target equilibria with mean coordinate error 0.007 at median exploitability 5×10⁻⁵, TOST-equivalent to the request within ±0.05. The selection follows reach-weighted I-projection with slope 0.969 [0.950, 0.987]. Limitations include: fixed off-manifold references cost 0.08-0.25 exploitability; boundary targets undershoot; and steering only matters against fixed, non-equilibrium opponents.

Conclusion: The reference policy in regularized self-play can serve as a deliberate "selection knob" for targeting specific Nash equilibria, not just as a stability mechanism. The recipe—anchor reference at desired member and refine—reinterprets RLHF-style KL anchoring as an equilibrium selection tool. However, the approach has specific limitations regarding boundary conditions and opponent types.

Abstract: Regularized self-play -- the family behind DeepNash's Stratego play -- drives a two-player zero-sum policy to a Nash equilibrium by best-responding to a slowly moving, entropy-regularized reference policy $ρ$. When the game has a polytope of value-equivalent equilibria, the regularizer silently breaks the tie: with a uniform reference it selects the maximum-entropy member, the I-projection of $ρ$ onto the Nash set. Can the reference be used to choose the equilibrium on purpose? On five exactly solvable games plus a 2-D polytope, with exact best responses and equivalence tests over independent seeds, anchoring the reference at a target member and refining steers self-play to that member with mean coordinate error 0.007 at median exploitability $5\times10^{-5}$, TOST-equivalent to the request within $\pm0.05$; the anchoring persists through refinement and follows the reference, not the initialization. Selection follows the reach-weighted I-projection (slope 0.969 [0.950, 0.987]). We report with equal emphasis where the story breaks: fixed off-manifold references cost 0.08-0.25 exploitability; stiff or flat families require a smaller mirror step, set by a pre-registered rule; boundary targets undershoot; curvature predicts where boundary saturation bites (rank correlation 0.90, p=0.037) while interior precision is curvature-independent. Table and MLP steering maps are equivalent within $\pm0.03$ at every target (30 seeds); matched control arms show attention's robust signature is excess seed variance, any systematic shift bounded at 0.018 and not significant. Against a best response the selection-robustness trade-off is degenerate: steering matters only against fixed, non-equilibrium opponents. The recipe -- anchor the reference at the desired member and refine -- reinterprets the KL anchor of RLHF-style RL as a selection knob, not only a stability leash.

</details>


### [38] [Dual-Axis Policy Optimization for LLM Agents: Bayesian Feedback Attribution and Trajectory Mass Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19830)
*Yingxuan Zhuang,Binhe Yu,Jingxiao Yang,Ruopei Sun,Ziting Li,Cheng Tan,Xuhong Zhang,Jianwei Yin,Jintao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BATON is a dual-axis RL framework for LLM agents that separates intra-trajectory feedback attribution (Bayesian Feedback Attribution) and inter-trajectory objective aggregation (Trajectory Mass Normalization), achieving consistent performance gains across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for LLM agents involves two distinct optimization dimensions that are typically conflated: how environment feedback is exploited within a single trajectory (intra-trajectory feedback attribution) and how complete trajectories are aggregated across a batch (inter-trajectory objective aggregation).

Method: BATON introduces a dual-axis framework: (1) Bayesian Feedback Attribution constructs a feedback-conditioned posterior over sampled actions within trajectories, and (2) Trajectory Mass Normalization assigns equal optimization mass to complete trajectories across batches.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA benchmarks using GRPO and GiGPO show that both axes provide independent performance gains, and their combination consistently achieves the strongest overall performance across different model scales.

Conclusion: Separating intra-trajectory feedback attribution and inter-trajectory objective aggregation into distinct optimization axes provides complementary benefits, with BATON's dual approach delivering robust and scalable improvements for LLM agent reinforcement learning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for LLM agents involves two distinct optimization di- mensions: how environment feedback is exploited within a trajectory, and how complete trajectories are aggregated across a batch. We formulate these dimen- sions as Intra-Trajectory Feedback Attribution and Inter-Trajectory Objec- tive Aggregation, and introduce BATON (Bayesian Attribution and Trajectory Objective Normalization), a dual-axis policy optimization framework. BATON instantiates the first axis with Bayesian Feedback Attribution, which constructs a feedback-conditioned posterior over sampled actions, and the second with Trajec- tory Mass Normalization (TMN), which assigns equal optimization mass to com- plete trajectories. Experiments with GRPO and GiGPO on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that both axes provide independent gains and that their combi- nation consistently achieves the strongest overall performance across model scales.

</details>


### [39] [MetaRTL: Meta-path Attention Enhanced Relational Table Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19832)
*Ken Zhong,Weichen Li,Zheng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaRTL is a two-stage framework for relational table learning that uses lightweight pre-training and efficient meta-path aggregation instead of deep GNN stacks, achieving both scalability and expressive power on large real-world databases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing relational table learning methods rely on deep GNN or HGNN stacks, which leads to high computational costs and limited performance when applied to large real-world databases. There's a need for more scalable and efficient approaches that can handle real-world scale while maintaining expressive power.

Method: MetaRTL is a two-stage framework: 1) Obtain initial table embeddings via lightweight pre-training, and 2) Perform non-parametric message passing to derive meta-path features, which are then aggregated by an attention module called MetaAttn. This approach shifts computation from deep message passing to efficient meta-path aggregation.

Result: The method was evaluated on 10 real-world datasets across 24 tasks, demonstrating effectiveness in terms of both performance and efficiency compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: MetaRTL provides a scalable and expressive solution for relational table learning by efficiently capturing rich relational semantics through meta-path aggregation while avoiding the computational overhead of deep GNN/HGNN stacks, making it suitable for large real-world databases.

Abstract: Relational table learning has gained increasing attention with the widespread use of relational databases. Existing methods typically rely on deep GNN or HGNN stacks, leading to high computational costs and limited performance on large real-world databases. We propose MetaRTL, a two-stage framework for scalable and expressive relational table learning. In the first stage, MetaRTL obtains initial table embeddings via lightweight pre-training. In the second stage, it performs non-parametric message passing to derive meta-path features, which are then aggregated by an attention module, MetaAttn. By shifting computation from deep message passing to efficient meta-path aggregation, MetaRTL captures rich relational semantics while maintaining high efficiency. Experiments on 10 real-world datasets across 24 tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [40] [A Dual-Process Perspective on Nudge Susceptibility in LLM-Based GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19843)
*Haya Halimeh,Sascha Kaltenpoth,Kevin Bösch,Oliver Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based GUI agents are susceptible to both automatic and reflective digital nudges, with reasoning configurations moderating these effects in opposite directions - reducing automatic default nudge susceptibility while increasing reflective social influence nudge susceptibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-based GUI agents increasingly act on behalf of users in digital environments designed for humans, there's a need to understand how these agents are influenced by the same interface design elements (digital nudges) that guide human behavior, particularly given their reasoning capabilities.

Method: The study used Dual-Process Theory to investigate susceptibility to Type 1 (automatic) and Type 2 (reflective) digital nudges. A randomized online shopping experiment was conducted with 3,600 agents across 21,600 simulations using six frontier models from three providers, examining how reasoning configuration moderates nudge susceptibility.

Result: Agents were vulnerable to both types of nudges, with reasoning configuration having opposing moderating effects: it reduced susceptibility to automatic default nudges but increased susceptibility to reflective social influence nudges. Extensive reasoning didn't make agents more robust overall but redirected the pathway of influence. Exploratory analysis showed this redirection was systematically structured by model scale.

Conclusion: Nudge susceptibility is a behavioral property of agentic AI, and interface design should be considered a governance concern for organizations delegating decisions to autonomous agents, as reasoning capabilities don't provide blanket protection but rather redirect how influence operates.

Abstract: LLM-based GUI agents increasingly act on behalf of users in digital environments that were designed with human users in mind. These graphical user interfaces were designed to support, but also deliberately steer, the behaviour and decisions of users. While behavioural biases in the textual outputs of LLMs are well-documented, far less is known about how such influence operates when models act as agents that perceive interfaces and execute decisions---and, in particular, whether the reasoning capabilities increasingly built into these agents make them more robust to it. Drawing on Dual-Process Theory, we empirically investigate whether LLM-based GUI agents are susceptible to automatic (Type 1) and reflective (Type 2) digital nudges, and how their reasoning configuration moderates this susceptibility. In a randomized online shopping experiment with 3,600 agents and a total of 21,600 simulations across six frontier models from three providers, we found that agents were vulnerable to both nudge types. Crucially, the reasoning configuration moderated these effects in opposing directions, reducing susceptibility to automatic default nudges while heightening it to reflective social influence nudges. Extensive reasoning therefore did not make agents more robust but redirected the route through which choice architecture takes effect. Exploratory analysis further showed this redirection to be systematically structured by model scale. Beyond establishing nudge susceptibility as a behavioural property of agentic AI, the study positions interface design as a governance concern for organizations that delegate decisions to autonomous agents.

</details>


### [41] [Constraint-Safe Graph-Context Scoring for Stable Point-Feature Labels Under Text-Width and Accessibility-Inspired Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19848)
*Taimoor Ahmad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LABELSENSE-Pilot is a prototype system for point-feature label placement on interactive maps that uses ML scoring and mixed-integer optimization to achieve high display yield with low flicker and zero collisions, while handling accessibility and multilingual requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional label placement algorithms often oversimplify by focusing only on overlap counts, ignoring important concerns like geometric validity, display yield, placement utility, stability across camera motion, and accessibility/multilingual requirements that change label dimensions.

Method: The system generates eight compass candidates per feature, scores them with a multilayer perceptron using graph-context summaries, adds a previous-placement bonus, and selects layouts through mixed-integer optimization. Layouts are checked for viewport containment, per-feature uniqueness, and pairwise clearance.

Result: On 2,500 airport coordinates spanning 155 countries with various stressors, LABELSENSE-Pilot displayed 85.62% of labels with only 2.09% flicker and zero collisions. It sacrificed 1.43 percentage points of display versus a handcrafted utility baseline while reducing flicker by 12.04 points. Enlarged-box-aware layouts achieved zero proxy violations.

Conclusion: The approach establishes an auditable engineering trade-off for label placement, demonstrating effectiveness for geometric and stability concerns. However, further work is needed with official baselines and participant evidence before submission, as human accessibility, multilingual usability, and preference aspects remain unaddressed.

Abstract: Point-feature label placement on interactive maps must reconcile geometric validity, display yield, local placement utility, and stability across camera motion. Accessibility and multilingual requirements further change label dimensions, yet algorithmic evaluations often collapse these concerns into overlap counts. We present LABELSENSE-Pilot, a reproducible prototype that generates eight compass candidates per feature, scores candidates with a multilayer perceptron over graph-context summaries, adds a previous-placement bonus, and selects a layout through mixed-integer optimization. The executed scorer is deliberately not described as a graph transformer. Every returned layout is checked for viewport containment, per-feature uniqueness, and pairwise clearance. Experiments use 2,500 airport coordinates and names spanning 155 countries, with country-grouped splits and generated density, camera, text-suffix, preference, and enlarged-font stressors. Across five seeds, LABELSENSE-Pilot displayed 85.62 percent of labels with 2.09 percent flicker and zero collisions. Versus a handcrafted-utility integer program, LABELSENSE-Pilot sacrificed 1.43 percentage points of display while reducing flicker by 12.04 points. Enlarged-box-aware layouts produced zero proxy violations, whereas standard geometry reevaluated at 1.5x violated 52.57 percent of selected placements. These results establish an auditable engineering trade-off, not human accessibility, multilingual usability, or preference. Official recent baselines and participant evidence remain required before submission.

</details>


### [42] [Reproducibility is not construct validity: LLM measurement of institutionally situated communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19866)
*Veronika Batzdorfer,Carlo Romano Marcello Alessandro Santagiustina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM annotations show high reproducibility but poor construct validity when measuring AI risk concerns, with significant divergence from survey responses that varies systematically across stakeholder groups and geographic regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine the distinction between LLM annotation reproducibility and construct validity - testing whether highly reproducible LLM inferences actually capture the intended constructs they're supposed to measure, using AI policy consultation data.

Method: Used European Commission's AI Act consultation data, linking structured survey responses to free-text consultation submissions from the same stakeholders. Applied LLM annotations to consultation submissions and compared them to survey-reported measures of the same constructs, analyzing divergence patterns across stakeholder groups and geographic regions.

Result: LLM annotations showed extremely high reproducibility (intraclass correlations > 0.99) but limited convergence with survey measures. Divergence varied systematically: business associations expressed greater AI risk concerns in text vs surveys (effect size +1.0), while public authorities showed negative divergence. Geographic autocorrelation existed (Moran's I = 0.347, p = 0.036). Despite divergence, survey-reported concerns remained strongly associated with support for explainability.

Conclusion: LLM annotation reproducibility can coexist with poor construct validity. Researchers must distinguish between reproducibility, construct validity, and communication context variation when using LLMs as measurement instruments, developing validation procedures that address all three aspects.

Abstract: High annotation reproducibility does not necessarily imply that an LLM-inferred measure captures the construct it is intended to measure. We test this distinction using a dataset from the European Commission's AI Act consultation, linking structured survey responses to free-text consultation submissions from the same stakeholders. LLM annotations of consultation submissions are highly reproducible (intraclass correlations > 0.99), yet show limited convergence with survey-reported measures of the nominal construct they were intended to approximate. Divergence between survey-and LLM-inferred text-based measures varies systematically across stakeholder groups: business associations express greater concern about AI risks in text-based consultations than in survey responses ({g} = +1.0), whereas public authorities and several nonbusiness groups show smaller or negative divergences. Divergences between scores suggest positive spatial autocorrelation across European countries (Moran's I = 0.347, p = 0.036), indicating that stakeholders from neighboring countries tend toward more similar text-based stances towards AI safety concerns. Despite divergence, survey-reported concerns remain strongly associated with support for explainability across all divergence levels. These results demonstrate that LLM annotation reproducibility can coexist with poor construct correspondence and motivate validation procedures that distinguish reproducibility, construct validity, and communication context variation when LLMs are used as measurement instruments.

</details>


### [43] [Physical knowledge on historical data matters more than enforcing physical constraints on the forecast](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19871)
*Etienne Lehembre,Pascal Audigane,Vincent Nguyen,Christel Vrain,Thi-Bich-Hanh Dao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PIRNN - a Physics Informed Recurrent Neural Network that forecasts time series while also predicting unobservable physical variables, outperforming existing models on groundwater level prediction tasks while maintaining physical coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deep learning models for time series forecasting in physical processes don't estimate unobservable intermediate physical variables that are crucial for domain experts to understand system behavior. There's a need for models that incorporate domain knowledge while maintaining interpretability.

Method: Proposed Physics Informed Recurrent Neural Network (PIRNN) that simultaneously predicts target variables and unobservable physical variables across both historical data and forecast periods. The method incorporates physical equations (using Gardenia groundwater model as case study) and is adaptable to various physical models with multiple equations and unobservable variables.

Result: PIRNN outperforms other neural network models and the Gardenia physical model on 5 out of 12 real-world datasets. Ablation study shows the importance of physical background, and domain experts validated the physical coherence of predicted variables.

Conclusion: The PIRNN approach successfully integrates physical knowledge into time series forecasting, enhancing model robustness and interpretability while maintaining predictive accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art methods. The method demonstrates the value of incorporating domain expertise in forecasting physical processes.

Abstract: Time series forecasting has seen signicant advancements with the emergence of new deep learning models. However, forecasting time series in applications involving physical processes remains a major challenge. Despite the apparition of Physics Informed Neural Networks (PINN), recent models do not estimate unobservable intermediate physical variables, which are important for domain experts to understand the target behavior. To this end, we propose a Physics Informed Recurrent Neural Network (PIRNN) which predicts, along the target, unobservable variables on both historic data and forecast target. This approach enhances the model robustness and results interpretation using domain knowledge. Our method is easily adaptable to any physical model using several equations, each having its own set of unobservable variables, to describe it-self. As a case study, we incorporate physical equations used for groundwater levels predictions by the physical model called Gardenia. This model uses transfers equations between reservoirs, optimized with data assimilation, to simulate the evolution of groundwater levels. Evaluation includes several well known neural network models and the Gardenia model compared on twelve real world datasets. In addition, we study the impact of each component through an ablation study. Our model outperforms other models on ve out of the twelve datasets and our ablation study underlines the importance of having a physical background in our time series forecasting task. Finally, the coherence of the physical variables predicted by our neural network is assessed by a domain expert.

</details>


### [44] [TRACE: Accountable Agentic Retrieval for Source Discovery in Digital Archives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19897)
*Donghan Bian,Marie Puren,Florian Cafiero*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a training-free agentic retrieval framework for accountable source discovery in historical archives that outperforms existing RAG methods while remaining cost-effective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Historical archives present unique retrieval challenges: OCR-degraded documents, heterogeneous sources across genres, and need for strong source traceability for scholarly and institutional use.

Method: TRACE is a training-free agentic retrieval framework designed for accountable source discovery over historical corpora. It was developed for the DECIDON project studying political discourse circulation during the French Third Republic.

Result: TRACE achieves R@10 = 0.856 and MRR = 0.653 on HistoriQA-ThirdRepublic benchmark (1,752 French historical questions), outperforming sparse, dense, graph-based, and agentic RAG baselines. It costs approximately $0.02 per question and shows largest gains on multi-hop and cross-corpus questions.

Conclusion: For large digital libraries and archives, retrieval accountability and corpus-aware agent design can provide a practical alternative to heavier training-based or graph-construction approaches, being economically feasible for heritage institutions with limited resources.

Abstract: Historical archives pose a difficult retrieval problem for retrievalaugmented generation systems: documents are OCR-degraded, heterogeneous across genres and sources, and require strong source traceability for scholarly and institutional use. We introduce TRACE, a training-free agentic retrieval framework designed for accountable source discovery over historical corpora. The system was developed in the context of DECIDON, an interdisciplinary project on the circulation of political discourse between parliamentary debates and the press during the French Third Republic, involving digitised historical collections and institutional use cases. The prototype is currently deployed internally within the project and accessible to 24 researchers across six partner institutions. We evaluate TRACE on HistoriQA-ThirdRepublic, a benchmark of 1,752 French historical questions over parliamentary debates and newspapers from 1887, with documents derived from Biblioth{è}que nationale de France digitised collections. TRACE achieves R@10 = 0.856 and MRR = 0.653, outperforming sparse, dense, graph-based, and agentic RAG baselines, with the largest gains on multi-hop and cross-corpus questions. At approximately $0.02 per question under the default hosted inference configuration, TRACE also remains economically feasible for heritage institutions, laboratories or companies that cannot rely on costly local GPU infrastructure. These results suggest that, for large digital libraries and archives, retrieval accountability and corpus-aware agent design can provide a practical alternative to heavier training-based or graph-construction approaches.

</details>


### [45] [From "Who Is This User?" to "What Does This Purchase Mean?": A Deployed Pipeline for Semantic User Profiling at Bank Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19928)
*Ryota Mitsuhashi,Tetsuro Morimura,Hirotake Ito*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a scalable alternative to per-user LLM inference for transaction analysis by shifting to per-transaction-pattern inference, reducing computational costs by orders of magnitude while maintaining statistical quality comparable to direct per-user analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional per-user LLM inference on transaction histories becomes prohibitively expensive at scale since the computational budget grows linearly with the number of users, making it impractical for large-scale applications in banking and e-commerce.

Method: The method employs a three-phase pipeline: 1) Resolve phase abstracts item names with optional web grounding, 2) Profile phase infers attributes for each frequent transaction pattern using a single LLM call per pattern, generating categorical labels, free-text attributes, and prevalence estimates, and 3) Tag phase clusters free-text attributes into a queryable database. The key innovation is shifting inference from per-user to per-pattern basis.

Result: On the Open e-commerce corpus, the method achieves statistical performance indistinguishable from direct per-user LLM analysis (comparable AUC across attributes). Prevalence estimates provide discriminative signal between positive and negative users. The pipeline has been deployed at a major Japanese bank, profiling tens of millions of users with nearly three orders of magnitude reduction in LLM inference targets compared to per-user approaches.

Conclusion: The per-pattern inference approach provides a scalable, cost-effective solution for large-scale transaction analysis that maintains statistical quality while dramatically reducing computational requirements, enabling practical deployment in enterprise settings like banking.

Abstract: Per-user LLM inference on transaction histories binds the inference budget linearly to user count, which becomes prohibitive at applied scale. We re-cast attribute inference from per-user to per-transaction-pattern. The pipeline runs in three phases: Resolve abstracts item names with optional web grounding, Profile infers attributes for each frequent pattern, and Tag clusters free-text attributes into a queryable database. In Profile, a single LLM call per pattern emits predefined categorical labels, free-text attributes, and per-attribute prevalence estimates. Because inference runs over patterns rather than users, the budget grows with the pattern count rather than the user count. On the public Open e-commerce corpus, the database is statistically indistinguishable from an LLM that reads each user's raw history directly in AUC across the evaluated attributes, and the prevalence estimates carry discriminative signal between positive and negative users. The pipeline is deployed at a major Japanese bank profiling on the order of tens of millions of users, with close to a three-order-of-magnitude reduction in LLM inference targets versus a per-user pipeline. The code is publicly available on https://github.com/CyberAgentAILab/profiling-agent-open-ecommerce.

</details>


### [46] [Beyond Depth Truncation: Controlled Evaluation of Depth Utilization in Recursive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19934)
*Ha Van Dau,Thanh Tung Khuat,Nguyen Thanh Dung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Depth-recurrent language models' depth-utilization metrics conflate multiple factors. The Depth Control Protocol (DCP) disentangles these factors with systematic controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics for depth-recurrent language models are flawed - depth truncation at inference time alters multiple model properties simultaneously (number of block applications, volume of distinct computation, and residual stream distribution) but the resulting slope is interpreted as reflecting only computation volume.

Method: Proposes Depth Control Protocol (DCP): three positive controls isolating each factor while varying others, negative control applying same interventions to dense transformers to rule out measurement artifacts, and controlled training intervention for causality verification.

Result: DCP enables proper isolation of factors affecting depth-utilization assessment. The key control (full block applications with single distinct iteration) is uniquely realizable in depth-wise weight-sharing architectures.

Conclusion: Existing depth-truncation evaluation conflates multiple factors; DCP provides a more rigorous diagnostic suite for properly assessing whether depth-recurrent models genuinely utilize their depth.

Abstract: Depth-recurrent language models iteratively apply a small layer stack, decoupling per-token compute from distinct parameter count. To determine whether such a model genuinely utilizes its depth, both recurrence and layer-pruning literatures rely on a shared evaluation: truncating depth at inference time, plotting quality against retained depth fraction, and reading off the slope. While cheap and training-free, this metric suffers from an unexamined flaw: it extracts a single scalar from an intervention that alters multiple model properties simultaneously. Depth truncation concurrently reduces the number of block applications, decreases the volume of distinct computation performed, and pushes the readout head onto an out-of-distribution residual stream. The observed slope conflates all three factors, yet is conventionally interpreted as reflecting solely the second.
  We propose the Depth Control Protocol (DCP), a diagnostic suite that disentangles these three quantities. DCP comprises three positive controls that isolate each factor while varying the others, a negative control applying the identical interventions to dense transformers to ensure the effect is not an artifact of the measurement protocol, and a controlled training intervention to verify causality. The linchpin control, running the full budget of block applications while executing only a single distinct iteration, is strictly realizable only in depth-wise weight-sharing architectures, since in a dense network repeating a layer yields an entirely different model rather than the same model in an alternative configuration.

</details>


### [47] [MaSCoD: A Multi-Agent Framework for Structural-Context-Guided Candidate Causal Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19944)
*Yudai Nakada,Yuichiro Nishiura,Jin Michael Splichal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MaSCoD is a multi-agent framework for causal discovery that pre-organizes candidate third variables and structural patterns before direct-edge judgment to reduce premature omission of causal relations, showing dataset- and backbone-dependent performance rather than uniform superiority.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based causal discovery approaches rarely treat premature omission of potentially relevant causal relations as an explicit design objective, creating a need for frameworks that better control omission errors.

Method: MaSCoD organizes candidate third variables and local structural patterns in Phase 1 before direct-edge judgment, using multi-agent organization. Evaluated with GPT-5.4 as primary backbone and GPT-4o for replication on Auto-MPG, DWD, and Sachs datasets.

Result: MaSCoD shows dataset- and backbone-dependent retention-selectivity profile. Full configuration (with Phase 1) achieved higher mean Recall and F1 than No Phase 1 across all settings, but increased false-positive rates. Reference-edge retention outperformed baselines on specific dataset-backbone combinations (DWD with GPT-5.4, Sachs with GPT-4o), not uniformly.

Conclusion: Structural pre-organization should be an explicit design target for omission control in causal discovery, and context construction should be evaluated jointly with its utilization in judgment procedures.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been applied to causal discovery, but candidate-graph generation rarely treats premature omission of potentially relevant causal relations as an explicit design objective. We propose MaSCoD, a multi-agent framework that organizes candidate third variables and local structural patterns before direct-edge judgment. We evaluate MaSCoD on Auto-MPG, DWD, and Sachs using GPT-5.4 as the primary backbone and GPT-4o for replication. MaSCoD exhibits a dataset- and backbone-dependent retention-selectivity profile rather than uniform superiority. Across all six dataset-backbone settings, Full, which supplies structural hypotheses before direct-edge judgment, achieved higher mean Recall and F1 than No Phase 1, which instead constructs them within the judgment procedure, while also increasing false-positive rates. Additional reference-edge retention over all evaluated baselines was observed on DWD with GPT-5.4 and on Sachs with GPT-4o, rather than uniformly across settings. Partial ablations showed that supplying both information components did not always outperform supplying only one. For GPT-5.4, stage-wise analysis showed that the Full-No Phase 1 retention gap was already present after direct-edge judgment, while reconciliation introduced additional reference-edge loss for Full on Sachs. These findings support structural pre-organization as an explicit design and evaluation target for omission control and motivate evaluating context construction jointly with its utilization in judgment.

</details>


### [48] [Not All AI Agents Are Equal: Characterizing Resource and Performance Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19947)
*Wonmi Choi,Minuk Park,Zhixiong Niu,Yongqiang Xiong,Chuck Yoo,Gyeongsik Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based AI agents waste resources due to unoptimized execution models; this paper analyzes resource dynamics across different agent tasks and proposes CPU-aware optimizations that improve latency by 5.4× for CPU-sensitive tasks and reduce average latency by 32%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based AI agents use iterative reasoning and tool execution with remote LLM APIs and local tool containers, creating complex resource dynamics. Current systems don't consider these dynamics, leading to significant resource waste and suboptimal performance. The authors aim to understand and optimize these resource interactions.

Method: The paper analyzes resource inter-mix for three representative AI agent tasks: retrieval-augmented question answering, web search, and software coding. They characterize latency with respect to resource dynamics when processing multiple requests and tasks concurrently. Through measurements, they identify task-dependent bottlenecks and resource behaviors.

Result: Measurements reveal wide-ranging behaviors across tasks, with the same tools showing substantially different resource dynamics. Concurrent execution exposes task-dependent bottlenecks in CPU, disk I/O, and memory. Surprisingly, faster LLM responses or more CPU cores don't always accelerate agents. The authors demonstrate CPU-aware tool admission and task-aware CPU allocation as optimization opportunities.

Conclusion: The paper successfully identifies and exploits task-specific resource dynamics in AI agent serving. Their optimization approach improves CPU-sensitive agent tasks by ~5.4× in latency and reduces average latency across multiple tasks by ~32% compared to native agents, demonstrating significant potential for resource-efficient AI agent systems.

Abstract: LLM-based AI agents process user requests through iterative reasoning and tool execution, often involving the invocation of remote LLM APIs with local tool containers. This execution model can make the optimization of agent serving difficult because latency, local resource demand, and container bottlenecks inter-mix across requests. However, the current agent ecosystem runs without much consideration of resource dynamics, which results in significant waste of the precious resources. This paper analyzes the resource inter-mix of AI agents for three representative tasks: retrieval-augmented question answering, web search, and software coding. To this end, we characterize the latency with respect to the resource dynamics of processing multiple requests and tasks concurrently. Our measurements show that agents have a wide range of behaviors depending on tasks, so that even the same tool can differ substantially in resource dynamics. We also find that running multiple requests concurrently exposes task-dependent bottlenecks in resource dynamics such as CPU, disk I/O, and memory. Furthermore, we uncover that faster LLM responses or more CPU cores do not always accelerate agents. Based on these observations, we demonstrate new optimization opportunities that exploit the resource dynamics of tasks: CPU-aware tool admission and task-aware CPU allocation. Our results show that the latency of CPU-sensitive agent tasks improves $\sim$5.4$\times$, and the average latency across multiple tasks is reduced $\sim$32% compared to native agents.

</details>


### [49] [Neuro-Symbolic Agentic AI for Networked Low-Altitude UAVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19961)
*Yuqi Ping,Tianhao Liang,Nanchi Su,Guangyu Lei,Junwei Wu,Qinyu Zhang,Tingting Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Neuro-symbolic agentic AI combines neural grounding, symbolic reasoning, and agentic interaction to enable more reliable and adaptive autonomy for networked UAVs, demonstrated through urban fire inspection with improved data efficiency, generalization, and evidence-grounded decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Networked UAVs face challenges with uncertain observations, dynamic environments, and intermittent connectivity, while existing agentic systems suffer from hallucination risks, data dependence, and weak generalization. There's a need for more reliable and adaptive decision-making capabilities for UAV autonomy.

Method: The paper proposes neuro-symbolic agentic AI (NSAAI) framework combining neural grounding, symbolic reasoning, and closed-loop agentic interaction. It develops a reference architecture with components for task/goal management, neuro-symbolic planning, verification/metacognition, skill execution/network interaction, and shared knowledge/memory. An urban fire-inspection case study is implemented in LAESim to demonstrate the approach.

Result: The urban fire-inspection case shows that NSAAI enables UAVs to coordinate sensing and cloud access under intermittent connectivity, reuse verified image-delivery skills, and satisfy explicit evidence conditions before mission completion. The approach demonstrates potential for reusable skills, evidence-grounded decision-making, and adaptive mission execution.

Conclusion: NSAAI offers promising foundations for more reliable and adaptive UAV autonomy through its combination of neural grounding and symbolic reasoning. The framework supports data efficiency, compositional generalization, continual learning, and zero-shot transfer. Future research directions include uncertainty-aware reasoning, knowledge/skill expansion, adaptive self-monitoring, and standardized evaluation.

Abstract: Networked low-altitude unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) need reliable and adaptive decision-making capabilities to operate under uncertain observations, dynamic environments, and intermittent connectivity, while many existing agentic systems remain limited by hallucination risks, data dependence, and weak generalization. This article investigates neuro-symbolic agentic AI (NSAAI) as a framework for combining neural grounding, symbolic reasoning, and closed-loop agentic interaction to support more reliable and adaptive UAV autonomy. We first examine its capability foundations in data efficiency, compositional generalization, continual learning, and zero-shot transfer, and then develop a reference architecture integrating task and goal management, neuro-symbolic planning, verification and metacognition, skill execution and network interaction, and shared knowledge and memory. An urban fire-inspection case implemented in LAESim illustrates how a UAV can coordinate sensing and cloud access under intermittent connectivity, reuse a verified image-delivery skill, and satisfy explicit evidence conditions before completing the mission. The results illustrate the potential of NSAAI to support reusable skills, evidence-grounded decision-making, and adaptive mission execution in networked UAV systems. We further discuss key research directions in uncertainty-aware reasoning, knowledge and skill expansion, adaptive self-monitoring, and standardized evaluation.

</details>


### [50] [Customizable and Jointly Optimized Route Planning: A Deep Architecture Enabling Differentiable Shortest-Path Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19996)
*Rui Zhao,Chao Chen,Longfei Xu,Chenguang Ji,Hengbin Cui,Kaikui Liu,Xiaolong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A deep learning architecture that jointly optimizes cost functions and route-ranking models for personalized route planning, addressing limitations of traditional graph algorithms and data-driven approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 1. Traditional graph algorithms use heuristic cost functions without optimality guarantees for route quality.
2. Data-driven approaches suffer from feedback loop problems when using user trajectories as ground truth (influenced by navigation services).
3. Need for route planning that accommodates diverse user preferences with customizable optimization.

Method: 1. Offline collection of Pareto optimal routes using multi-objective Dijkstra algorithm as candidate set.
2. Neural network structure that emulates shortest-path search and route ranking in end-to-end differentiable manner.
3. Constrained optimization formulation for route preferences with novel loss function that optimizes single-objective variable while keeping others under strict constraints.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world datasets show the architecture significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both route quality and customizability.
