Qualitative analysis further shows that S4L captures implicit causal relations, deontic modality, and exception structure more reliably than the baselines. These results demonstrate that structured reasoning prompts can substantially improve the reliability of natural-language-to-logic translation for legal and safety-critical applications.

</details>


### [75] [A Qualitative Model for Reasoning about Path and Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20349)
*Abhishek Jaiswal,Zoe Falomir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid qualitative reasoning model for Camelot Jr. block-puzzle game that integrates mathematical physics with symbolic reasoning to provide human-like tutoring for spatial skill training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial reasoning skills correlate strongly with STEM performance, and games can effectively train these skills in children. However, human-like tutoring requires AI agents capable of commonsense inferences about spatial events, which qualitative reasoning models can provide through interpretable symbolic representations.

Method: The paper introduces a hybrid qualitative model for Camelot Jr. that combines qualitative reasoning with mathematical center-of-mass stability logic to handle the precise physics required by the block-puzzle domain.

Result: The developed model can translate game states into interpretable feedback for human-like player guidance, facilitating spatial skill training while contributing to explainable game-playing agents.

Conclusion: The hybrid qualitative reasoning approach successfully enables spatial skill training in Camelot Jr. and advances the development of human-centric, explainable AI agents for educational games.

Abstract: Spatial reasoning abilities correlate strongly with performance in STEM fields. Games offer a compelling medium for training these critical skills in developing children who have a natural proclivity for play. However, to facilitate human-like tutoring and player guidance, these games require an AI agent capable of making commonsense inferences from spatial events. Qualitative reasoning (QR) models appear to be a suitable framework for these application domains. As these models reason in symbolic representations, they can seamlessly translate game states into interpretable feedback for human-like player guidance. This paper introduces a hybrid qualitative model designed for Camelot Jr., a block-puzzle game that requires constructing multi-level bridges to connect two avatars stationed on separate towers. The game poses a challenge for the player, who must make platforms stable, plan their path, and ensure they use all the provided blocks. To handle the precise physics required by the domain, we integrate a mathematical center-of-mass stability logic to guide our qualitative solver. Our work facilitates spatial skill training in Camelot Jr. and contributes to the development of human-centric, explainable game-playing agents.

</details>


### [76] [Generating Heterogeneous 3D Geological Microstructures from 2D Images via a Stable Diffusion-Adversarial Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20358)
*Ali Aouf,Eric Laloy,Bart Rogiers,Christophe De Vleeschouwer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid denoising diffusion model with adversarial loss generates high-quality 3D microstructures from 2D images, outperforming previous GAN methods for complex heterogeneous materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D imaging for clay and cementitious materials is expensive and technically limited, while existing GAN-based methods (like SliceGAN) struggle with finer details in complex heterogeneous microstructures, motivating alternative generative frameworks.

Method: A hybrid approach combining denoising diffusion models with adversarial loss, replacing standard denoising loss with adversarial loss to enable stable training without 3D ground truth data.

Result: The model generates microstructures of varying complexity with minimal slice artefacts and close agreement with ground-truth phase fractions and structural descriptors.

Conclusion: The proposed hybrid diffusion-adversarial approach effectively reconstructs 3D volumes from 2D images, offering a practical solution for materials characterization where 3D imaging is challenging.

Abstract: Characterizing the physical properties of clay and cementitious materials matters across many fields, from materials science to geological waste disposal. Property simulation typically calls for 3D imaging, which is expensive, not always accessible, and technically limited for certain materials. Recent progress in deep generative models offers a way around this, reconstructing 3D volumes from the more easily acquired 2D images.
  Among GAN-based methods for 3D microstructure generation, SliceGAN has shown strong results for homogeneous isotropic and anisotropic systems. It struggles, however, to capture the finer detail of more complex heterogeneous microstructures, which motivates alternative generative frameworks.
  We introduce a hybrid approach that draws on the stability and generation quality of denoising diffusion models. Since no 3D ground truth is available, we replace the standard denoising loss with an adversarial loss, which yields a stable training process in our experiments. We show that the resulting model generates microstructures of varying complexity with minimal slice artefacts and close agreement with ground-truth phase fractions and structural descriptors.

</details>


### [77] [The Organization of Inference: Information, Resource Constraints, and AI Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20449)
*Yukun Zhang,Kemu Xu,Yishen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The study examines how organizational factors like workflow design and information access affect AI productivity on software engineering tasks, finding that planning workflows with proper information access can outperform direct execution at higher capacity levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the distribution of capacity and task information across different stages of AI production affects economic value and productivity outcomes in software-engineering workflows.

Method: Controlled workflow experiments on externally verified software-engineering tasks using two matched resource panels with different token ceilings (12,000 and 24,000 tokens), comparing direct execution versus information-constrained planning approaches, including a strict read-only planning campaign.

Result: Direct execution achieved 59.6% success rate at both token ceilings, while planning success improved from 36.2% to 51.2% as capacity increased. Planning with task information access improved success by 16 percentage points over hidden-task planning at lower capacity. At higher capacity (24,000 tokens), task-informed planning showed a 29.6-point advantage over direct execution. Planning workflows shifted from being rate-limited (46.2% binding) to capacity-driven (0.8% binding) with downstream execution accounting for most usage increases.

Conclusion: While scale determines available capacity, workflow design and information structure crucially shape productive value. Planning workflows with proper task information access can outperform direct execution when sufficient capacity is available, highlighting the importance of organizational factors in AI production systems.

Abstract: The economic value of inference depends on how capacity and task information are distributed across stages of AI production. We study these organizational margins using controlled workflow experiments on externally verified software-engineering tasks. In two matched resource panels, direct execution records the same success rate of 59.6 percent at logical-token ceilings of 12,000 and 24,000, while success under information-constrained planning rises from 36.2 to 51.2 percent. The planning disadvantage narrows by 15.0 percentage points (95 percent task-cluster bootstrap interval: 4.2 to 25.8). A strict read-only planning campaign varies whether the planner sees the task issue. At 12,000 tokens, issue access raises success by about 16 percentage points over issue-hidden planning. Compared with direct execution, task-informed planning is about 10 points lower at 12,000 tokens; at 24,000 tokens, it shows a 29.6-point advantage. In the resource panels, direct execution uses substantially less than either ceiling, while the planning workflow's binding rate falls from 46.2 to 0.8 percent and downstream execution accounts for 89.9 percent of the increase in total use. Scale determines the capacity available to a system; workflow and information structure shape the productive value

</details>


### [78] [SkillAA: Attribution-Guided Skill-Graph Updating with Targeted Validation and Rollback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20455)
*Ziqiao Shang,Ling-Yue Ge,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillAA is a structured skill-optimization framework that uses graph-based representation and attribution-guided editing to improve frozen language models' skill execution without direct parameter updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for external skills have limitations: they edit skills directly from failed rollouts without structured routing, and existing skill graphs underuse semantic boundaries, object addresses, and topological dependencies for skill retrieval, targeted updating, and scoped validation.

Method: SkillAA represents skill applicability, execution, and composition in a unified graph. It contrasts successful and failed executions to route candidate repairs to specific graph objects, updates only selected local structure, and uses Local and Big Gates to screen candidate changes before commitment.

Result: With gpt-5.6-sol, SkillAA achieves 81.5% on SearchQA, 66.7% on LiveMath, and 91.2% on DocVQA, attaining the highest observed mean in every main setting.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the utility of attribution-guided graph editing and graph-scoped validation for improving skill execution in frozen language models.

Abstract: External skills provide domain procedures without parameter updates, but existing methods often edit skills directly from failed rollouts without structured routing from an observed failure to an editable location; existing skill graphs also underuse semantic boundaries, object addresses, and topological dependencies for skill retrieval, targeted updating, and scoped validation. We introduce SkillAA (Skill Abductive Attribution), a structured skill-optimization framework for frozen language models. It represents skill applicability, execution, and composition in a unified graph, allowing the same structure to support skill selection, attribution-guided repair, and update validation. SkillAA contrasts successful and failed executions to route candidate repairs to specific graph objects, updates only the selected local structure, and uses Local and Big Gates to screen candidate changes before commitment. With gpt-5.6-sol, SkillAA reaches 81.5%, 66.7%, and 91.2% on SearchQA, LiveMath, and DocVQA, respectively, and attains the highest observed mean in every main setting. These results support the utility of attribution-guided graph editing and graph-scoped validation.

</details>


### [79] [How Do Agent Harnesses Create Value? Planning Information and Release Control in Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20474)
*Yukun Zhang,Kemu Xu,Yishen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The study shows that prewritten task-specific plans significantly improve agent success rates compared to random text, especially for complex tasks. Adding a verification system catches most errors with minimal cost, and verification alone provides most error-reduction benefits of a full planning+verification system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand how different components of agent systems - supply planning guidance, execution organization, and completion checking - affect operational success, error rates, and costs in real-world applications.

Method: The researchers conducted two Retail experiments and an Airline pilot using a τ²-bench framework. They compared prewritten task-specific plans (Fixed) against randomly shuffled policy text matched in word count (Sham) across 265 matched cells. They also implemented a read-only terminal verifier to assess error detection.

Result: Fixed plans improved success rates by 7.17 percentage points, especially for complex tasks. The verifier rejected 61% of invalid episodes while withholding only 17% of correct ones, costing less than one cent per episode. The importance of planning versus verification depends on liability costs.

Conclusion: Planning guidance significantly boosts agent performance, particularly for complex tasks, while verification systems efficiently catch errors. For high-liability scenarios, verification alone provides most error-reduction benefits of full planning+verification systems at lower cost.

Abstract: Agent harnesses supply planning guidance, organize execution, and check completion. We study how these components affect success, erroneous acceptance, and cost in two Retail experiments and an Airline pilot in $τ^2$-bench. The primary comparison pairs prewritten task-specific plans (Fixed) with shuffled policy text matched in word count (Sham), isolating the contribution of guidance content. Across 265 matched cells, Fixed improves oracle-verified success by 7.17 percentage points (90\% task-clustered bootstrap interval, 1.15--13.36 points), with gains concentrated in higher-complexity tasks. A read-only terminal verifier rejects 61\% of Retail oracle-invalid episodes while withholding 17\% of correct ones, at less than one cent of additional cost per episode. Which component matters more depends on the loss assigned to erroneous acceptance: at low liability the planning gain dominates; at high liability the verifier's avoided false passes dominate---and a standalone verifier captures nearly all the false-pass benefit of the full planning-plus-verification stack at a fraction of its cost.

</details>


### [80] [SoL-Pi: Recursively Scaling Auto-Research Loops for Efficient Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20519)
*Haozhe Liu,Tian Ye,Sensen Gao,Qihang Cao,Yitong Li,Mingchen Zhuge,Duomin Wang,Ruihua Zhang,Ping Luo,Jiawang Bian,Lei Zhu,Ligeng Zhu,Enze Xie,Song Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SoL-Pi is a token-efficient coding agent system that uses RSI-inspired auto-research loops to develop reusable improvements, reducing API costs by ~1/3 while maintaining comparable performance to existing systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As coding agents operate unattended for longer reasoning trajectories, token efficiency becomes crucial for scaling recursive self-improvement (RSI) and reducing API costs.

Method: The authors take an RSI-inspired approach at the harness layer, scaling auto-research loops across diverse environments. They develop four selected mechanisms forming SoL-Pi: action execution, context compaction, observation handling, and delegated reading.

Result: On the 51-task EdgeBench evaluation, SoL-Pi achieves performance comparable to Pi across GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 while reducing token traffic by 44.7-49.0% and API costs by about one third. Estimated hourly savings are $8.75-$13.50 relative to native Codex/Claude Code harnesses and $4.36-$5.71 relative to Pi.

Conclusion: The SoL-Pi system demonstrates that automated harness discovery can achieve production-level outcomes through reusable improvements that transfer beyond their development setting, enabling significant cost savings while maintaining performance.

Abstract: As coding agents move from supervised code completion to unattended, around-the-clock exploration, their work expands from isolated predictions into long trajectories of reasoning, tool use, and feedback. Token efficiency therefore becomes important for scaling recursive self-improvement. We take an RSI-inspired approach at the harness layer, scaling auto-research loops across increasingly numerous and diverse environments for harness rollouts. At this scale, the process yields reusable improvements that transfer beyond their development setting, moving automated harness discovery toward production-level outcomes. Four mechanisms survive selection and form SoL-Pi, spanning action execution, context compaction, observation handling, and delegated reading. On the 51-task EdgeBench evaluation, SoL-Pi achieves performance comparable to Pi across GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 while reducing recorded token traffic by 44.7-49.0% and API cost by about one third. In other words, estimated hourly savings are \$8.75-\$13.50 relative to native Codex and Claude Code harnesses, and \$4.36-\$5.71 relative to Pi.

</details>


### [81] [FreqCondNorm: Towards Cross-domain Predictive Maintenance through a Frequency-Conditioned Transformer Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20535)
*Zaynab Raounak,Camille LHermine,Zhiguo Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FreqCondNorm is a Transformer-based architecture with frequency-conditioned normalization that unifies heterogeneous time-series signals across 5 orders of magnitude in sampling frequency for predictive maintenance, achieving strong fault diagnosis transfer but not improving remaining useful life prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning predictive maintenance models suffer from poor transferability across machines and operating conditions, especially when labeled data are scarce and signals span from 1 Hz to ~100 kHz sampling frequency. This heterogeneity in data makes it challenging to build models that can generalize across different equipment and operating conditions.

Method: The authors propose FreqCondNorm, a Transformer-based architecture that introduces a FiLM-style frequency-conditioned normalization layer to unify heterogeneous time-series within a single model. The architecture is pretrained on five public predictive maintenance datasets (CWRU, MFPT, UOC18, PRONOSTIA, CMAPSS) using masked auto-encoding and contrastive learning with balanced domain sampling.

Result: On fault diagnosis, the model achieves 99.2% accuracy on CWRU (+6.4 percentage points over CNN baseline) and 82.1% zero-shot accuracy on MFPT, demonstrating strong transfer across different sampling frequencies. However, the approach does not improve remaining useful life (RUL) prediction, suggesting a mismatch between pretraining objectives and RUL prediction tasks.

Conclusion: FreqCondNorm successfully addresses the challenge of transferring across heterogeneous sampling frequencies for fault diagnosis in predictive maintenance, but reveals a limitation in applying the same approach to remaining useful life prediction, highlighting the need for future investigation into better aligning pretraining objectives with RUL prediction tasks.

Abstract: Deep learning predictive maintenance models suffer from poor transferability across machines and operating conditions, especially when labelled data are scarce and signals span five orders of magnitude in sampling frequency (1 Hz to ~100 kHz). We propose FreqCondNorm, a Transformer-based architecture that introduces a FiLM-style frequency-conditioned normalization layer to unify heterogeneous time-series within a single model. The architecture is pretrained on five public predictive maintenance datasets (CWRU, MFPT, UOC18, PRONOSTIA, CMAPSS) using masked auto-encoding and contrastive learning with balanced domain sampling. On fault diagnosis, the model achieves 99.2% accuracy on CWRU (+6.4 pp over CNN) and 82.1% zero-shot accuracy on MFPT, demonstrating strong transfer across sampling frequencies. However, the approach does not improve remaining useful life prediction, suggesting a mismatch between pretraining and RUL objectives that warrants future investigation.

</details>


### [82] [Refuse, Decompose, Refresh: A Claim-Safe Protocol for Closed-Loop AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20538)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a "claim-safe" evaluation protocol for AI systems that addresses the problem of reproducible but misleading evaluations by requiring three actions: refuse (abstain when evidence is insufficient), decompose (separate execution details from pass/fail labels), and refresh (treat distribution shifts as opportunities to update references rather than fault evidence).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies a critical flaw in current AI evaluation practices: evaluations can be perfectly reproducible yet still support wrong claims, especially in closed-loop systems where policy determines which states are visited, what components are observable, and which failures leave measurable traces. This creates a risk of misleading conclusions from technically sound but contextually limited evaluations.

Method: The authors propose a three-action protocol: 1) Refuse - abstain from claims when clean reference streams or matched runtime comparisons lack sufficient support, 2) Decompose - report protocol execution details, operational false admission, and structural hypotheses separately rather than as a single PASS/FAIL label, and 3) Refresh - treat distribution-shift alarms as requests to invalidate and recompute reference maps rather than as evidence of faults. They instantiate this protocol in an aggregate-only simulator with 24 policy components, three demand regimes, two fault-mask families, and independent development/heldout seeds.

Result: In the preregistered heldout dataset containing 1,440 cases and 21,600 partition rows, only 55/72 regime-component units were reference-admitted, and 54/55 remained runtime-admitted, demonstrating abstention as part of the result. Stable false admission was 0/20 represented components, with a one-sided exact 95% upper bound of 0.1391. Within admitted units, affected clean traffic outperformed nominal fault-cell fraction: the negative-log-likelihood difference was 0.1264 nats per row with 95% component-cluster interval [0.0593, 0.1918]. Drift analysis showed "null" must be reference-relative, as clean fault-null streams triggered different alarm patterns across regimes.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that rather than relying on universal thresholds, they contribute an executable contract linking observable support, statistical calibration, and justified claims. This approach addresses the fundamental problem that even perfectly reproducible evaluations can support wrong claims by ensuring evaluations are both technically sound and contextually meaningful.

Abstract: An AI evaluation can be perfectly reproducible and still support the wrong claim. This risk is acute in closed-loop systems: policy determines visited states, observable components, and which failures leave a measurable trace. We propose a claim-safe protocol with three actions. Refuse: abstain when a clean reference stream or matched runtime comparison lacks support. Decompose: report protocol execution, operational false admission, and structural hypotheses separately rather than as one PASS/FAIL label. Refresh: treat distribution-shift alarms as requests to invalidate and recompute a reference map, not as fault evidence. We instantiate the protocol in an aggregate-only simulator with 24 policy components, three demand regimes, two fault-mask families, and independent development and heldout seeds. The preregistered heldout contains 1,440 cases and 21,600 partition rows. Only 55/72 regime-component units were reference-admitted and 54/55 remained runtime-admitted, making abstention part of the result. Stable false admission was 0/20 represented components, with a one-sided exact 95% upper bound of 0.1391 under a frozen 0.20 rule. Within admitted units, affected clean traffic outpredicted nominal fault-cell fraction: across 540 unit-arm rows nested in 20 component clusters, the cell-minus-traffic negative-log-likelihood difference was 0.1264 nats per row, with a 95% component-cluster interval of [0.0593, 0.1918]. A drift log shows why "null" must be reference-relative: clean fault-null streams triggered 15/15, 0/15, and 14/15 alarms across three regimes, while only the middle regime matched the frozen detector reference. Rather than a universal threshold, we contribute an executable contract linking observable support, statistical calibration, and justified claims.

</details>


### [83] [Limits of Confidence in Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20581)
*Russ Webb,Amitis Shidani,Alice Bizeul,Dan Busbridge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reveals that discrete diffusion methods like remasking and uniform-state samplers fail to properly handle dependencies between tokens in complex domains (pixels, phonemes, words), showing these methods can only match training distributions when writing conditionally independent positions and that per-position distributions cannot capture dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why discrete diffusion methods produce poor results in domains with inherent token dependencies (images, speech, language), and to demonstrate mathematically that these methods fundamentally cannot capture joint distributions when dependencies exist between tokens.

Method: Theoretical analysis of discrete diffusion sampling processes, demonstrating that per-position distributions cannot match dependent groups, with empirical validation on the synthetic ScanAndAdd task where the true joint distribution is known in closed form.

Result: Theoretical proof that discrete diffusion can only match training distributions when writing conditionally independent positions, and empirical results showing generated distribution has 29× higher total variation than sampling-noise floor on ScanAndAdd task, while per-sample metrics appear perfect (1.0).

Conclusion: Current discrete diffusion methods are fundamentally limited in handling token dependencies, producing samples that appear good individually but collectively diverge significantly from the true distribution, necessitating new approaches for dependent token generation.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion, including remasking and uniform-state samplers, generate a sequence by writing multiple token positions per step, drawing each from a per-position distribution and choosing which positions to write from those same distributions. For domains of general interest (pixels, phonemes, or words) there are inherent dependencies between tokens. We show that a step matches the training distribution only when the positions it writes are conditionally independent given the tokens already fixed, that no product of per-position distributions can match a dependent group, and that per-position distributions do not determine whether a group is dependent: two joint distributions can have identical per-position marginals while differing in which combinations of values occur. On ScanAndAdd, a synthetic task whose joint distribution is available in closed form, we verify that every group of two or more undetermined positions a confidence ranking writes is dependent, and measure the generated distribution to be $29\times$ the sampling-noise floor total variation while per-sample metrics are $1.0$.

</details>


### [84] [PAA: The Probabilistic Allen Algebra: A Generative and Complete Probabilistic Extension of Allen's Interval Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20634)
*Julian Eggert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAA extends crisp Allen interval algebra to handle uncertainty via probabilistic relations derived from Gaussian distributions over interval boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Allen algebra's crisp relations are inadequate for real-world temporal data where times, durations, and boundaries are uncertain (e.g., from language, perception, databases, uncertain histories). Expressions like "just before" or "roughly during" have graded meaning not captured by crisp predicates.

Method: Develops probabilistic Allen algebra using Gaussian distributions: time points as Gaussian, intervals as Gaussian midpoints with truncated-Gaussian durations. Relation probabilities are derived from distributions over interval boundaries via multivariate Gaussian orthant probabilities from linear inequality constraints. Introduces tolerance band for contact relations.

Result: Provides complete probabilistic extension that: 1) Derives Allen's taxonomy rather than positing it, 2) Preserves hierarchy as intervals collapse, 3) Separates graded expressions from contact relations, 4) Is scale-invariant, 5) Validated via Monte Carlo simulations, 6) Available as open Python package.

Conclusion: PAA provides a principled, generative probabilistic framework for handling uncertain temporal relations that generalizes crisp Allen algebra while maintaining its structural properties and taxonomy.

Abstract: Allen's interval algebra is a qualitative calculus for temporal relations, but its thirteen base relations are crisp predicates over exact interval boundaries. This is inadequate for temporal information from language, perception, databases, or uncertain histories, where times, durations, and boundaries are uncertain and expressions such as "just before" or "roughly during" have graded meaning. We develop the probabilistic Allen algebra (PAA): a generative and complete extension in which relation probabilities are derived from distributions over interval boundaries rather than assigned as scores. Time points are Gaussian; intervals have Gaussian midpoints and truncated-Gaussian durations. Every relation is a boundary-ordering predicate in one common probability space: point-point relations reduce to error functions, and point-interval and interval-interval relations to multivariate Gaussian orthant probabilities induced by linear inequalities. Contact relations (meets, starts, finishes, equals) receive positive measure through a tolerance band, and under a single tolerance the thirteen relations form a true partition that recovers crisp Allen as the tolerance vanishes. The construction derives Allen's taxonomy rather than positing it: coarse predicates such as precedence, overlap, and containment are unions of leaves whose probabilities are leaf sums, and this hierarchy is preserved as intervals collapse to points and thirteen relations reduce to five and then three. Each relation further decomposes into correlation-aware temporal primitives in the spirit of CIDOC CRM. The algebra is scale-invariant and separates graded expressions such as "shortly before" from contact relations. All results are Monte-Carlo validated and shipped as an open, tested Python package.

</details>


### [85] [Ownership in AI-Assisted Everyday Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20658)
*Megan Wei,Melanie Subbiah,Audrey Lee,Annya Dahmani,Dave Edwards,Helen Edwards,Ellie Pavlick*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: People feel ownership of AI-assisted work when they lead, iterate, or rewrite, but lose ownership when they merely approve AI suggestions. Ownership can be maintained even when AI executes the vision, and willingness to disclose AI use is shaped by social norms rather than pride.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI becomes integrated into everyday tasks, there's a need to understand how people perceive ownership and contribution when collaborating with machines to produce work. The paper explores when work done with AI still feels like our own.

Method: Exploratory qualitative survey where participants described two recent, self-selected tasks completed with AI: one that felt like their own and one that did not. The study examines factors affecting felt ownership in AI collaborations.

Result: Felt ownership depends on collaboration process: people disown work when merely approving AI suggestions but retain ownership when leading, iterating, or rewriting. Ownership can extend to tasks where people own the vision but not execution. Loss of personal voice and lack of output comprehension erode ownership. Willingness to disclose AI use is decoupled from pride/ownership and shaped by community norms and fear of credit erasure.

Conclusion: The paper proposes research directions to promote AI development that supports people's sense of authorship over their own lives, emphasizing the importance of designing AI systems that maintain human agency and ownership in collaborative work.

Abstract: When does work done with AI still feel like ours? As AI becomes woven into everyday tasks, we must examine what happens to our sense of ownership and contribution when a machine shares in producing what we make. We report an exploratory qualitative survey in which participants were asked to describe two recent, self-selected tasks completed with AI: one that felt like their own and one that did not. We find that felt ownership depends on the process of collaboration: people disown work when they merely approve AI's suggestions, but retain ownership when they lead, iterate, or rewrite. Ownership can also extend to settings where people own the vision for a project but not the execution; respondents reported high ownership on tasks they could not have completed without AI. Loss of personal voice and a lack of comprehension of the output both erode ownership. Finally, willingness to disclose AI use is often decoupled from actual pride or ownership, and instead shaped by community norms and fear of credit erasure. We propose several research directions as a result of these findings to promote AI development that supports people's sense of authorship over their own lives.

</details>


### [86] [Deep Noir: Autonomous Steering Discovery via Architectural Chronometry in Transformer Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20722)
*Frank E. Bobe,Gregory D. Vetaw,Darshan W. Bryner,Matthew G. Cook,Jose L. Salas-Vernis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deep Noir is an automated framework that uses Logit Lens convergence and causal attribution to discover optimal activation steering parameters for modifying LLM behavior, achieving significant improvements across multiple tasks and model sizes while identifying security vulnerabilities in steered systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current activation steering techniques for modifying LLM behavior require manual identification of where and how strongly to steer, which is time-consuming and limits scalability. There's a need for an automated approach to discover optimal steering parameters across different tasks and architectures.

Method: Deep Noir combines two key techniques: Logit Lens convergence analysis (to identify where to steer) and causal head-level attribution (to determine how strongly to steer). The framework autonomously discovers optimal steering parameters by analyzing these convergence patterns and causal effects, without requiring manual parameter tuning.

Result: The framework achieves substantial improvements across multiple scales: 16.7 percentage-point improvement on spam detection at 1B models (std dev 4.7), with gains increasing to 21-42 percentage points at 7-9B across four architectures. On SST-2 sentiment analysis, it achieves 13.1 percentage-point improvement without code changes. The automated approach outperforms baseline methods like RepE without head masking (which fails to improve over baseline).

Conclusion: Deep Noir enables automated, effective activation steering that generalizes across tasks and architectures, but also reveals that steering creates a predictable prompt-injection attack surface whose vulnerability increases with steering magnitude - an important security consideration for agent systems deploying steered classifiers.

Abstract: Activation steering modifies LLM behavior at inference time, but identifying where and how strongly to steer remains manual. We introduce Deep Noir, a framework that uses Logit Lens convergence and causal head-level attribution to autonomously discover optimal steering parameters. Across three scales (1B x 3, 2-3B x 2, and 7-9B x 4), our engine achieves 16.7 percentage-point improvement on spam at 1B (standard deviation 4.7; 39 runs), with gains increasing to 21 to 42 percentage points at 7-9B across four architectures. On SST-2 sentiment, it achieves a 13.1 percentage-point improvement with zero code changes. Mechanistic grounding enables automated discovery of intervention points that generalize across tasks and architectures. On sentiment, RepE without head masking fails to improve over baseline, while Deep Noir improves all models (p less than 0.01). We further show that steering creates a predictable prompt-injection attack surface whose vulnerability increases monotonically with steering magnitude. This finding is relevant to agent systems deploying steered classifiers.

</details>


### [87] [Q&A on Any Spreadsheet Requires Interpreting Its Grid Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20732)
*Zofia Smoleń*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes semantic cell annotation to improve spreadsheet chunking for LLM-driven RAG systems, but identifies a fundamental limitation of discrete classification approaches for inherently 2D unstructured spreadsheet data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spreadsheets are challenging for LLM-driven RAG systems because they're fundamentally two-dimensional unstructured data with continuous relationships and infinite potential cell roles. Current discrete classification approaches can't perfectly capture spreadsheet structural nuance.

Method: Proposes a novel framework that splits spreadsheets into interpretable chunks using semantic cell role annotation, creating enriched context for answer generation in RAG systems.

Result: The framework beats state-of-the-art methods but faces a hard ceiling due to the limitations of discrete classification. The research shows that classification models restricted to finite, pre-defined classes cannot perfectly capture spreadsheet structural nuance.

Conclusion: The spreadsheet-to-LLM bottleneck requires moving beyond discrete cell classification. The field must develop dimensionality-reduction techniques to directly flatten 2D unstructured spreadsheets into 1D unstructured text for better RAG interpretability and generation.

Abstract: Semantic cell annotation improves chunking interpretability for spreadsheets in LLM-driven RAG systems, aiding answer generation through enriched context rather than improved retrieval accuracy. We propose a novel framework of splitting any spreadsheet into interpretable chunks using cell role annotation. Our framework beats the state of the art, yet it faces a hard ceiling. Spreadsheets are fundamentally two-dimensional unstructured data with continuous relationships and infinite potential cell roles. Because classification models are restricted to finite, pre-defined classes, they cannot perfectly capture this structural nuance, even with human-level annotation. We show that addressing the spreadsheet-to-LLM bottleneck requires moving beyond discrete cell classification. Instead, the field must develop dimensionality-reduction techniques to directly flatten 2D unstructured spreadsheets into 1D unstructured text. Text chunks would be easier for downstream RAG to interpret and generate from.

</details>


### [88] [RAFT: A Stateful Retrieval-Augmented Framework for Troubleshooting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20754)
*Mingxuan Zhang,Xiaowen Wang,Anupma Sharan,Zhengyi Chen,Chenyu Diana Zhang,Shanshan Yang,Chittibabu Pacharu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAFT is a stateful RAG framework for troubleshooting agents that retrieves historical cases at the timeline entry level rather than treating cases as static documents, improving retrieval accuracy across all stages of case progress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems for troubleshooting agents treat support cases as static documents, overlooking their multi-stage, stateful nature and failing to retrieve relevant historical cases whose intermediate states match active troubleshooting situations.

Method: RAFT abstracts closed historical cases into directed chains of timeline entries, retrieves at the entry level to surface cases with matching intermediate states, and returns parent-case trajectories anchored at matched states. It optionally links cases through configurable similarity representations in a case-level graph.

Result: RAFT improves Case Hit over vanilla RAG and GraphRAG baselines at every stage of case progress, with statistically significant gains over the strongest baseline. Jira results provide directional evidence that the advantage transfers to real case histories.

Conclusion: Stateful retrieval at the timeline entry level is effective for troubleshooting agents, and the approach shows promise for real-world application. The authors release their benchmark, implementation, and evaluation dataset to support further research.

Abstract: Effective troubleshooting agents in enterprise customer support depend on retrieving actionable guidance from similar historical cases, yet existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat support cases as static documents and overlook their multi-stage, stateful nature. We introduce RAFT (Retrieval-Augmented Framework for Troubleshooting Agents), a stateful RAG framework that abstracts each closed historical case into a directed chain of timeline entries and retrieves at the entry level, surfacing cases whose intermediate states match the active case and returning the parent-case trajectory anchored at the matched state; an optional case-level graph links cases through a configurable similarity representation. We evaluate this retrieval layer directly, which, unlike evaluating a full agent system, requires no production deployment. Because public multi-stage troubleshooting data is extremely rare, we pair a synthetic benchmark built from Microsoft Learn Windows Server documentation with real Apache Jira issues carrying human-created duplicate labels. RAFT improves Case Hit over vanilla RAG and GraphRAG baselines at every stage of case progress, with statistically significant gains over the strongest baseline; the Jira results provide directional evidence that the advantage transfers to real case histories. We release our benchmark, implementation, and the Apache Jira evaluation set.

</details>


### [89] [An Empirical Study of Harness Design for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20804)
*Run-Ze Fan,Zihao Zhang,Simin Ma,Yebowen Hu,Shouju Wang,Kaiqiang Song,Fei Liu,Hamed Zamani,Xiaoyang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A modular analysis of coding harness components shows that context management prevents failures under tight budgets, planning shifts from accuracy scaffold to cost saver, and action space choice depends on model bash proficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding harness evaluations treat harnesses as monolithic systems, making it unclear which individual components are effective. This prevents component-level comparisons and informed harness design.

Method: Used a lightweight coding harness with fixed execution loop while varying three components: planning, action space, and context management. Evaluated 176 matched settings across four models on SWE-Bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, with five context-management strategies, four context-window budgets, and targeted ablations.

Result: (1) Context management prevents context-overflow failures and becomes valuable with tight budgets; (2) Rule-based elision before LLM summarization is most efficient; (3) Planning shifts from accuracy scaffold for weak models to cost saver for strong models; (4) Tool choice depends on bash proficiency - predefined tools help weak models, bash-only is cheaper for capable models.

Conclusion: Coding harness components have distinct effects: context management extends trajectories, planning changes stopping points, action space changes code granularity. Findings inform model- and budget-aware harness design and provide a modular evaluation framework.

Abstract: Coding harnesses shape how autonomous coding agents translate model capabilities into long-horizon software-engineering performance, yet existing work typically evaluates harnesses as monolithic systems, leaving the effectiveness of individual components unclear. To enable component-level comparisons, we study this question with a lightweight coding harness whose execution loop is fixed while three components are varied: planning, action space, and context management. Across four models evaluated on SWE-Bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, we evaluate 176 matched settings spanning five context-management strategies, four context-window budgets, and targeted ablations of planning and action space. We find that: (1) Context management becomes increasingly valuable as the context-window budget tightens, with most of its benefit coming from preventing context-overflow failures. (2) Staging rule-based elision before LLM-based summarization provides the strongest overall efficiency among the context-management strategies, whereas making elided content recoverable adds machinery that models rarely use and yields no accuracy gain. (3) Planning shifts from an accuracy scaffold for weaker models to a cost saver for stronger models, with little change in accuracy. (4) Predefined tools improve performance for models with weaker bash proficiency, whereas bash-capable models can operate effectively with a bash-only interface and achieve substantially lower cost, especially on command-line-centric tasks. Trajectory-level analysis explains these effects: context management extends execution trajectories without substantially altering agent behavior, planning changes where trajectories stop, and the action space changes the granularity at which code is written. These findings inform model- and budget-aware harness design and provide a modular framework for evaluating future harness components.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [90] [Generative Query Suggestion via Intent Coverage and Query-Level Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19209)
*Xinpeng Liu,Lu Ma,Jiayi Qiao,Mengyu Zhou,Linglong Li,Xiaofeng Bian,Haonan Chen,Xiaoxi Jiang,Guanjun Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dual-stage optimization framework for generative query suggestion that improves query quality and intent coverage through intent-aware diversity modeling and query-level credit assignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance user engagement by generating query slates that are both individually useful and collectively diverse, covering distinct user intents while overcoming the challenge of balancing query quality with intent coverage.

Method: Intent-Driven Query Suggestion Framework with dual-stage optimization: 1) Intent-aware diversity modeling using intent-aligned supervised fine-tuning data and Intent-Aware Diversity Reward to optimize intent coverage; 2) Query-level credit assignment that routes individual quality signals to corresponding query tokens while sharing a slate-level diversity signal across the slate.

Result: Experiments on large-scale production dataset show improvements in click-through rate, query quality, and intent coverage through online A/B testing and offline evaluation.

Conclusion: The proposed intent-driven framework with dual-stage optimization effectively addresses the challenge of generating query suggestions that are both high-quality and diverse, leading to improved user engagement metrics in real-world applications.

Abstract: Generative query suggestion aims to enhance user engagement by anticipating user intents and recommending relevant follow-up queries. A central challenge is to generate slates whose individual queries are useful while the slate covers distinct intents. We propose an Intent-Driven Query Suggestion Framework with dual-stage optimization. First, intent-aware diversity modeling constructs intent-aligned supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data and uses an Intent-Aware Diversity Reward to optimize intent coverage. Second, query-level credit assignment routes individual quality signals to the corresponding query tokens while sharing a slate-level diversity signal across the slate. Experiments on a large-scale production dataset, including online A/B testing and offline evaluation, show improvements in click-through rate, query quality, and intent coverage.

</details>


### [91] [Layer-wise Curriculum Learning for Efficient LLM Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19213)
*Donggeon Lee,Dooyeon Na,Seungmin Oh,Jongbin Ryu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Layer-wise curriculum learning method for LLM compression that partitions models into segments, uses progressive optimization, and includes feature caching to reduce GPU memory and training time by 50% while achieving SOTA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM compression methods face computational inefficiency and knowledge transfer challenges when transferring from teacher to student models, particularly with large language models where memory usage and training time are prohibitive.

Method: Layer-wise curriculum learning that partitions the model into layer segments, starts with easier optimization tasks and progressively tackles harder ones. Includes feature caching with multi-threading to address feature misalignment and maximize GPU utilization.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple datasets while reducing GPU memory usage and training hours by more than 50% on BERT and GPT-2. Outperforms other pruning methods on LLaMA-family and Qwen models under same training hours with lower GPU memory footprint.

Conclusion: Layer-wise curriculum learning provides an efficient approach to LLM compression that accelerates convergence, stabilizes knowledge transfer, and significantly reduces computational resources while maintaining or improving performance compared to existing methods.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce layer-wise curriculum learning for efficient LLM compression. The proposed method facilitates the knowledge transfer from the teacher model to the student model, utilizing a curriculum learning approach that begins with easier optimization tasks and progressively tackles harder ones. In order to adopt the layer-wise learning in LLM compression, we partition the whole model into multiple segments consisting of layers, thereby enabling more computationally efficient knowledge transfer for LLMs. Based on our theoretical analysis of cumulative error phenomenon, layer-wise curriculum learning accelerates convergence while stabilizing the knowledge transfer process. In addition, we present a feature caching method with a multi-threading strategy to efficiently address feature misalignment across layers, maximizing GPU utilization. Consequently, our method exhibits advanced model compression performance, as well as high computational efficiency in terms of minimized memory usage and short training hours. Experiments on multiple datasets show that the proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing GPU memory usage and training hours by more than 50\% on BERT and GPT-2. Moreover, it outperforms the other pruning methods on LLaMA-family and Qwen models under the same training hours, with a lower GPU memory footprint.

</details>


### [92] [Block Parallelism For Efficient Distributed Long-Context Diffusion Language Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19242)
*Tarun Suresh,Pranshu Chaturvedi,Hangoo Kang,Parth Shroff,Ishan S. Khare,Hermann Kumbong,Azalia Mirhoseini*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Block Parallelism (BP) and Context-Sharded Block Parallelism (CSBP) are new distributed training methods that significantly accelerate Block Diffusion Language Models (BDLMs) for long-context training by optimizing attention communication and memory usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Block Diffusion Language Models face scaling challenges for long-context training due to distributed attention communication overhead and activation memory constraints. Conventional context parallelism (CP) has inefficiencies in handling clean and corrupted key/value pairs and their gradients.

Method: Introduced Block Parallelism (BP) which assigns each corrupted-block computation to one rank, and Context-Sharded Block Parallelism (CSBP) which additionally shards the shared clean sequence across ranks. This keeps corrupted K/V and gradients local, avoids replicated clean prefixes, and preserves BDLM training semantics.

Result: On 16 H200 GPUs at 256K context, CSBP improves throughput over best baselines by 1.18-1.45x for supervised fine-tuning and 1.27-1.33x for conversion tasks, with full-model speedup reaching 1.61x at 512K. On eight H100 GPUs, accelerates DFlash2 training by 2.48x at 512K and 7.59x at 1M context. Achieves higher pass rates on benchmark tasks in matched training runs.

Conclusion: CSBP is an effective distributed training strategy that addresses key bottlenecks in BDLM training, enabling more efficient long-context training with significant performance improvements across various tasks and hardware configurations.

Abstract: Block diffusion language models (BDLMs) combine autoregressive dependencies across blocks with parallel denoising within blocks, but long-context training is constrained by distributed attention communication and activation memory. Conventional context parallelism (CP) shards the combined clean-plus-corrupted sequence by position, communicating shared clean K/V together with block-specific corrupted K/V and their gradients. We observe that the BDLM objective separates over target blocks. We introduce block parallelism (BP), a new distributed parallelism dimension that assigns each corrupted-block computation to one rank. To scale BP to long contexts, we introduce context-sharded block parallelism (CSBP), which also shards the shared clean sequence across those ranks. CSBP keeps corrupted K/V and gradients local, avoids replicated clean prefixes, and preserves BDLM training semantics. On 16 H200 GPUs at 256K context, CSBP improves throughput over the best baseline by 1.18-1.45x for supervised fine-tuning and 1.27-1.33x for conversion of autoregressive models to BDLMs, while matching or reducing peak HBM. Full-model speedup reaches 1.61x at 512K. On eight H100 GPUs, CSBP accelerates DFlash2 speculative-decoder training by 2.48x at 512K and 7.59x at 1M. In matched 12-hour DiffusionGemma 26B-A4B SFT runs, CSBP achieves higher pass rates at every trained checkpoint on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench Lite. Code: https://github.com/ScalingIntelligence/Turbo-dLLM

</details>


### [93] [Randomized SVD Approximations for Spectral Co-Clustering of Word-Document Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19243)
*Fateme Mazdarani,Carlos Toxtli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Randomized approximations for spectral co-clustering that reduce computational costs by using either random projection or element-wise sampling, with performance depending on matrix sparsity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral co-clustering is computationally expensive on high-dimensional data due to reliance on SVD, creating a need for faster approximations.

Method: Two randomized approaches: 1) Randomized SVD via random projection, and 2) Partial SVD combined with element-wise random sampling, both designed for bipartite text data with potentially different numbers of document and word clusters.

Result: Both methods reduce runtime compared to full SVD baseline, but performance varies with matrix sparsity. Random projection is more reliable across settings, while sampling-based method works best on denser matrices and provides limited benefit on sparse data.

Conclusion: Randomized approximations for spectral co-clustering should be selected based on the underlying data structure, particularly matrix sparsity.

Abstract: Spectral co-clustering is a useful tool for discovering latent structure in word-document matrices, but its reliance on singular value decomposition (SVD) can make standard formulations expensive on high-dimensional data. This paper presents two randomized approximations for normalized spectral co-clustering of bipartite text data when the numbers of document and word clusters may differ. The first method uses randomized SVD through random projection, while the second combines partial SVD with element-wise random sampling. Across real-world and synthetic datasets, both methods reduce runtime relative to the full-SVD baseline, but their behavior depends on matrix sparsity. The random projection method is the more reliable approximation across the tested settings, whereas the sampling-based method is most useful on denser matrices and provides limited benefit on already sparse text data. These results show that randomized approximations for spectral co-clustering should be selected according to the underlying structure of the data.

</details>


### [94] [Radio-Frequency Convolutional Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19279)
*Zhihui Gao,Shi-Yuan Ma,Yiran Chen,Dirk Englund,Tingjun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RF-CNNs repurpose existing radio frequency mixers in wireless devices to perform neural network convolutions, eliminating need for separate AI accelerators and achieving ultra-low energy efficiency for edge AI inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge devices need AI inference but lack computing power; adding separate accelerators increases SWaP-C constraints; wireless radios already have frequency mixers that naturally perform convolution operations.

Method: Map multi-channel CNN convolutions onto frequency tones for execution by passive mixer hardware; weights transmitted over air; analog hardware shared with communication functions; uses existing radio frequency infrastructure.

Result: Demonstrated RF-CNNs running deep CNNs up to 26.4M parameters and 9 layers for wireless signal/image classification and image generation; achieves 0.72 femtojoules per multiply-accumulate (two orders of magnitude better than digital processors); close to full-precision performance.

Conclusion: Deployed wireless infrastructure can enable efficient, state-of-the-art AI inference on billions of existing edge devices without additional hardware, leveraging existing radio frequency mixers to perform neural computations.

Abstract: Running artificial intelligence (AI) models directly on edge devices such as smartphones, wearables, and drones offers low latency, pervasive scalability, and data privacy, but these devices rarely carry the computing capability that modern neural networks demand. Edge accelerators have been developed in response, yet each adds computing hardware to devices already constrained in size, weight, power, and cost (SWaP-C). An alternative lies in what these devices already carry: the frequency mixer in every wireless radio multiplies signals in time, natively performing convolution in the frequency domain. Here we introduce radio-frequency convolutional neural networks (RF-CNNs), which repurpose existing communication hardware for CNN inference. Multi-channel convolutions are mapped onto frequency tones for a passive mixer to execute in a single pass. We experimentally demonstrate that RF-CNN runs deep CNNs up to 26.4 million parameters and nine layers from classification of wireless signals and images to controllable image generation, close to full-precision performance. Because the weights arrive over the air and the analog hardware is shared with communication, the edge device spends energy only on data preparation and readout-down to 0.72 femtojoules per multiply-accumulate, two orders of magnitude less than it would cost on an added digital processor. These results suggest that deployed wireless infrastructure can bring efficient, state-of-the-art AI inference to the billions of devices it already connects.

</details>


### [95] [Learning-Induced Dynamical Transition in Recurrent Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19288)
*Varun Vaidya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recurrent neural networks transition from chaotic to stable dynamics during learning, described by a non-equilibrium dynamical mean-field theory that predicts critical learning transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how learning fundamentally reshapes the dynamics of recurrent neural networks, particularly the transition from initially chaotic activity to stable task-dependent behavior, which remains a theoretical challenge.

Method: Developed a non-equilibrium dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) to describe learning transitions, derived two-time correlation functions throughout learning, and identified critical feedback strengths and learning rate dependent critical times separating regimes.

Result: The theory shows that slow feedback-driven learning generates evolving effective feedback strength that drives networks through a transition from chaotic to stable dynamics via a bifurcation of the DMFT solution, with quantitative agreement with numerical simulations.

Conclusion: Learning progressively deforms an effective dynamical landscape through growing learned feedback structure, enabling precise prediction of network output evolution during training and explaining the fundamental dynamics reshaping in recurrent neural networks.

Abstract: Learning in recurrent neural networks can fundamentally reshape their underlying dynamics, transforming initially chaotic activity into stable task-dependent behavior. We develop a non-equilibrium dynamical mean-field theory(DMFT) to describe this transition during learning. We show that a slow feedback-driven learning process generates an evolving effective feedback strength that drives the network through a transition from chaotic to stable dynamics defined by a bifurcation of the DMFT solution. By deriving the two-time correlation function throughout learning, we identify a critical feedback strength and a corresponding learning rate dependent critical time separating these regimes. The transition arises from the progressive deformation of an effective dynamical landscape by the growing learned feedback structure. Starting from the untrained state, the theory predicts the time evolution of the network output during training and shows quantitative agreement with numerical simulations.

</details>


### [96] [Personalized Federated Hierarchical Gaussian Processes for Privacy-Preserving Modeling of Heterogeneous Distributed Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19337)
*Xianjian Xie,Hao Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: pFedHGP is a personalized federated learning method using hierarchical Gaussian processes that decomposes each client's function into shared global, client-specific, and local residual components, enabling probabilistic regression/classification with uncertainty quantification while keeping data decentralized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address probabilistic regression and classification in federated learning settings with heterogeneous clients, where data cannot be centralized due to privacy/communication constraints, and uncertainty quantification is needed for reliable decision-making.

Method: The method uses hierarchical Gaussian processes where each client's latent function decomposes into: 1) a shared global component, 2) a client-specific deviation sharing the global kernel structure, and 3) a flexible local residual. It employs sparse inducing-variable approximations and federated variational inference, with the server synchronizing only low-dimensional statistics for the shared component while raw data stays local.

Result: The method achieves perfect fault classification in press tonnage monitoring using only 13.77% of labeled cycles, and successfully recovers geographic zones in federated air-quality modeling without centralizing station-level time series. It also establishes connections to multi-output Gaussian processes through an Instantaneous Linear Mixing Model viewpoint.

Conclusion: pFedHGP provides an effective federated probabilistic learning framework that balances shared knowledge transfer with personalization, maintains data privacy through local computation, offers uncertainty quantification, and demonstrates strong performance in real-world applications with heterogeneous client data distributions.

Abstract: We present Personalized Federated Hierarchical Gaussian Processes (pFedHGP) for probabilistic regression and classification when data are distributed across heterogeneous clients. Each client's latent function decomposes into (i) a shared global component, (ii) a client-specific deviation that shares the global kernel structure, and (iii) a flexible local residual. Sparse inducing-variable approximations and federated variational inference keep raw data local while the server synchronizes only low-dimensional statistics for the shared component. Full predictive distributions support uncertainty-aware decisions. In application studies, pFedHGP attains perfect fault classification in press tonnage monitoring using 13.77% of labeled cycles and recovers geographic zones in federated air-quality modeling without centralizing station-level time series. An Instantaneous Linear Mixing Model viewpoint links the hierarchy to multi-output Gaussian processes for correlated sensors.

</details>


### [97] [COIN-GP: Cooperative Online Learning in Networked Distributed Systems with Partial Measurements via Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20598)
*Zewen Yang,Xiaobing Dai,Zhenxiao Yin,Hang Zhao,Zhijun Li,C. C. Chan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A distributed observer-based framework combining state estimation and dynamics learning using Gaussian Processes for sensor networks with partial observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of jointly estimating system states and partially unknown dynamics in distributed sensor networks where only partial state observations are available, requiring a solution for accurate estimation despite incomplete measurements and deficient models.

Method: Proposes an observer-based dynamic cooperative learning framework with online distributed Gaussian Process (GP) regression, including a novel data collection strategy with theoretical feasibility conditions, and derives error bounds for both state and model estimation.

Result: Empirical simulations demonstrate the superiority of the proposed approach over existing distributed GP-based methods, with theoretical conditions ensuring feasible data acquisition and derived error upper bounds for state and model estimation.

Conclusion: The paper presents an effective distributed framework for simultaneous state estimation and dynamics learning in sensor networks with partial observations, achieving improved performance through cooperative GP learning and provable error bounds.

Abstract: In this paper, we tackle the problem of jointly estimating the system states and partially unknown dynamics within distributed sensor-equipped networks, particularly in scenarios where only partial state observations are available. To address this issue, we propose an observer-based dynamic cooperative learning framework incorporating online distributed Gaussian Process (GP) regression, which enables accurate estimation despite incomplete in measurements and deficient GP models. In addition, a novel data collection strategy is introduced, with theoretical conditions ensuring feasible data acquisition. Moreover, we also derive an error upper bound encompassing state estimation and model estimation, leveraging the deterministic error bounds of GPs. Empirical simulations demonstrate the superiority of our approach compared to existing distributed GP-based methods.

</details>


### [98] [How to Guide Your Language Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19356)
*Rohit Dilip,Tianrong Chen,Yuyang Wang,David Van Valen,Joshua Susskind,Miguel Angel Bautista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Probe guidance uses frozen diffusion model states to guide flow matching models without extra inference cost, achieving SOTA in unconditional generation and improving QA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autoguidance methods for diffusion models require additional forward passes at inference time and lack understanding of the actual guidance mechanism. The authors aim to create a more efficient guidance method that eliminates inference overhead while improving understanding of how guidance works.

Method: Probe guidance extracts guidance signals from frozen internal states of an existing diffusion model. Unlike autoguidance, it doesn't need extra forward passes at inference and ensures weak and strong models share similar dynamics. The method is applied to continuous diffusion language models.

Result: Probe guidance achieves state-of-the-art performance on unconditional generation for diffusion language models. When applied to a 1.7B diffusion language model, it consistently improves performance on multiple choice question answering benchmarks. The study also reveals that in autoguidance, weak models must come from low-entropy training regions.

Conclusion: Probe guidance provides both a practical improvement method for diffusion language models and new insights into the poorly understood mechanisms behind autoguidance, offering better efficiency and performance while advancing theoretical understanding.

Abstract: We introduce a new method to guide flow matching models. Our approach, which we call probe guidance, uses the frozen internal states of an existing diffusion model to construct a guidance signal. This works using a similar principle as autoguidance, but eliminates the need for an additional forward pass at inference time and provides a reliable path to ensure that the weak and strong model share similar dynamics. We apply and benchmark this method on continuous diffusion language models, where probe guidance sets a new state-of-the-art performance on unconditional generation. When applied to a 1.7B diffusion language model, probe guidance consistently improves on multiple choice question answering benchmarks. Using our probes, we study the traditional autoguidance setting where the strong model is a weak checkpoint, and find that the weak model must come from a low-entropy region of training. These findings both provide a practical way to improve diffusion language models and shed light on the actual mechanism behind autoguidance, which is currently poorly understood.

</details>


### [99] [Smart Insole Human Activity Recognition for Continuous Monitoring in Elderly Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19359)
*Edwin Rios,Antony Garcia,Fengpei Yuan,Xinming Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Wireless smart insoles with pressure sensors and IMUs can accurately detect sitting, standing, walking, and unstable walking in unseen participants using machine learning, enabling elderly fall prevention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Falls in older adults are often preceded by changes in mobility, balance, and postural transitions, creating a need for continuous activity monitoring and early fall risk detection.

Method: Developed wireless smart insoles with 16 pressure sensors and 6D IMU (accelerometer + gyroscope) to collect plantar-pressure and inertial data at 80Hz from 15 healthy adults. Used overlapping window segmentation and evaluated multiple models with participant-independent cross-validation to ensure generalization to unseen individuals.

Result: Histogram-Based Gradient Boosting achieved macro-F1 scores of 0.954 (left foot), 0.959 (right foot), and 0.980 with bilateral sensing. A 1D-CNN did not significantly outperform HGB (p=0.0625). The system accurately recognizes sitting, standing, walking, and unstable walking in participants unseen during training.

Conclusion: Low-profile footwear sensing with pressure and IMU measurements can effectively infer activity states for unseen participants, providing a foundation for activity monitoring and fall prevention in elderly care applications.

Abstract: Falls in older adults are often preceded by changes in mobility, balance, and postural transitions. This paper presents a wireless smart insole platform and machine-learning workflow for recognizing sitting, standing, walking, and unstable walking from plantar-pressure and inertial signals. Each insole integrates 16 active pressure-sensing locations and a six-dimensional IMU stream consisting of tri-axial acceleration and angular velocity. Data were collected from 15 healthy adults at 80~Hz and segmented into overlapping windows. Window length and candidate model families were first screened with stratified 10-fold cross-validation; the primary performance estimate was then obtained with participant-independent 5-fold Stratified Group cross-validation, ensuring that all windows from a participant remained in a single fold. Under this protocol, Histogram-Based Gradient Boosting (HGB) achieved macro-F1 scores of 0.954 and 0.959 for the left and right feet, respectively, and 0.980 with bilateral sensing. A compact 1D-CNN evaluated with the same participant-independent folds did not significantly outperform HGB ($p=0.0625$). The results show that low-profile footwear sensing can infer activity state from pressure and IMU measurements for participants unseen during training, establishing a basis for activity monitoring and fall prevention in elderly care.

</details>


### [100] [Stiefel Attention: When the Geometry of Transformer Projection Matrices Dominates Optimizer Choice---and When It Does Not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19363)
*Rubén Darío Guerrero*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Constraining query/key projections to Stiefel manifold with Riemannian Adam prevents weight decay collapse and improves training stability and performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention uses Euclidean optimizers without constraints on query/key geometry, allowing weight decay to collapse learned attention geometry during training cycles.

Method: Constrain query and key projections to Stiefel manifold, optimize with Riemannian Adam that has scalar second moments per frame, trust region step capping, and polar retraction.

Result: Achieves 97.0% validation accuracy vs 61.1% baseline on modular arithmetic grokking; +8.98pp improvement on CIFAR-10 patches across 12 paired runs; gains widen with more data.

Conclusion: Stiefel-constrained optimization prevents weight decay from destroying learned attention geometry; step scale-free property is key factor, not projector or equivariance; method provides stability against collapse cycles.

Abstract: The query and key projections $\WQ,\WK$ in attention are almost always trained by Euclidean optimizers with no constraint on their geometry. We constrain them to the Stiefel manifold and optimize them there with a Riemannian Adam that carries one scalar second moment per frame, caps its step by a trust region, and retracts polarly. Four propositions prove this update is steepest descent in the embedded metric, independent of gradient scale, well conditioned, and exactly $\mathrm{O}(d)$-equivariant, each certified numerically in \texttt{float64}. A fifth supplies the mechanism: weight decay has \emph{identically zero} Riemannian gradient on $\St(d,r)$, since $W = W I_r$ lies in the normal space, so the learned attention geometry survives the collapse cycles that decay drives through the rest of the model. On modular arithmetic grokking, a single run holds $97.0\%$ validation accuracy at epoch 20\,000 against the baseline's $61.1\%$---an unstable endpoint we report as evidence for the mechanism rather than as an effect size. On CIFAR-10 patches the same rule gains $\mathbf{+8.98}$\,pp over 12 paired starts ($t{=}60.6$, $12/12$), and the gap widens with data rather than eroding. The step rule earns this: a fixed-step Riemannian update is degree one in the gradient, so it moves $24$--$40\times$ less per step than an identically shaped AdamW matrix---its frames barely leave their initialization, and freezing them outright costs only $0.28$\,pp. An ablation credits the whole gain to making the step scale free, and nothing measurable to the projector or to equivariance. A negative result sharpens the account: gauge removal cannot motivate the method, because a direction along which the loss is invariant carries no gradient at all.

</details>


### [101] [Machine-Learning Assessment of the Predictive Value of Inflammatory Biomarkers for Cognitive Impairment in an Older Hispanic Adult Cohort](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19374)
*Antony Garcia,Gabrielle Britton,Alcibiades Villarreal,Diana Oviedo,Giselle Rangel,Xinming Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: I-309 (CCL1) chemokine emerged as the most significant incremental predictive biomarker for cognitive impairment using a leakage-safe interpretable Naive Bayes classifier on a small clinical dataset, achieving improved ROC-AUC with strong statistical significance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small clinical tabular datasets require interpretable machine learning because deep learning is often impractical and ensemble models can be difficult to inspect. Statistical significance doesn't necessarily imply predictive utility, necessitating robust methods for feature selection.

Method: Implemented a leakage-safe threshold-likelihood Bernoulli/Categorical Naive Bayes classifier using data from the PARI-HD cohort (n=165). Continuous predictors were reduced to supervised chi-square-derived states within training folds, with all data-dependent steps performed within repeated stratified 10-fold cross-validation (30 repeats). Statistical robustness was assessed across 200 random partitions.

Result: Demographic baseline achieved ROC-AUC of 0.630 ± 0.017. I-309 (CCL1) increased AUC by 0.110, with 100% of DeLong tests showing p<0.05. Primary analysis: fixed-partition DeLong p=0.0018, median p-value across 200 partitions was 0.0011. Among 18 candidate markers, only I-309 achieved Benjamini-Hochberg-adjusted q=0.032 and satisfied q<0.05 in 85% of random partitions.

Conclusion: I-309/CCL1 is identified as an interpretable candidate feature for tabular prediction of cognitive impairment, with the fitted model being an inspectable table of thresholds and class-conditional probabilities. Results are pending external validation.

Abstract: Small clinical tabular datasets require interpretable machine learning because deep learning is often impractical and ensemble models can be difficult to inspect. A key pitfall is that statistical significance does not necessarily imply predictive utility. Using data from the Panama Aging Research Initiative--Health Disparities (PARI-HD) cohort (n=165), we implemented a leakage-safe threshold-likelihood Bernoulli/Categorical Naive Bayes (BNB/CNB) classifier. Within every training fold, each continuous predictor was reduced to a supervised chi-square-derived state, while income entered the model through a categorical likelihood.
