All data-dependent steps were performed within repeated stratified 10-fold cross-validation with 30 repeats. The demographic baseline achieved a ROC-AUC of 0.630 +/- 0.017. I-309 (CCL1) was the dominant incremental feature, increasing AUC by 0.110, with paired DeLong tests yielding p<0.05 in 100% of repeats. In the pre-specified primary analysis, I-309 produced a fixed-partition DeLong p=0.0018, with robustness assessed across 200 random partitions, where the median p-value was 0.0011. Within the exploratory family of 18 candidate markers, I-309 achieved a Benjamini-Hochberg-adjusted q=0.032 on the frozen partition and satisfied q<0.05 in 85% of random partitions, whereas no other marker demonstrated reliable incremental predictive value. Because the fitted model is an inspectable table of thresholds and class-conditional probabilities, these results identify I-309/CCL1 as an interpretable candidate feature for tabular prediction of cognitive impairment, pending external validation.

</details>


### [102] [FCx: An algorithm for finding Feasible Counterfactual Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19383)
*Kleopatra Markou,Vana Kalogeraki,Dimitrios Gunopulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FCx is a novel counterfactual explanation method that generates realistic, low-cost explanations while explicitly enforcing feasibility constraints through a modified VAE with multi-factor loss, outperforming existing methods in feasibility guarantee.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing counterfactual explanation methods often fail to ensure feasibility, producing unrealistic suggestions like changing immutable attributes (e.g., race) or suggesting non-constructive modifications that don't align with real-world constraints, limiting their practical usefulness.

Method: FCx uses a modified Variational Autoencoder (VAE) optimized with a multi-factor loss function that incorporates both hard constraints (user-specified domain knowledge) and soft constraints (inferred via causal inference from data). It measures cost via proximity (absolute value changes) and sparsity (number of features changed), while realism is ensured using Local Outlier Factor (LOF) for density estimation to guarantee CFs reside in densely populated regions.

Result: Extensive experiments on four public datasets show FCx matches state-of-the-art performance across multiple metrics while uniquely guaranteeing feasibility, making it the first method to efficiently generate CFs that are simultaneously realistic, low-cost, and feasible.

Conclusion: FCx successfully addresses the feasibility gap in counterfactual explanations, providing a practical solution that balances realism, cost, and feasibility constraints through a novel VAE-based approach with comprehensive constraint handling.

Abstract: Counterfactual (CF) explanations identify changes that alter an input's classification. While existing methods produce realistic and low-cost CFs, they often fail to ensure feasibility, by suggesting non-constructive modifications or incompatible with future changes (e.g., changing an individual's race to secure a job offer). We introduce a refinement of CF explanations that explicitly enforces feasibility. Our approach is the first to efficiently generate CFs that are realistic, low-cost and feasible. We accommodate both hard feasible constraints, specified by domain knowledge users, and soft feasible constraints, inferred automatically via causal inference from the dataset. Our method, Feasible Counterfactual Explanations (FCx), is based on a modified Variational Autoencoder (VAE) optimized with a multi-factor loss function. We measure the cost of a change based on the absolute change in values (proximity) as well as the number of features changed (sparsity) while realism is measured based on the LOF for density estimation, guaranteeing that CFs reside in densely populated regions. Extensive experiments on four public datasets show that our approach matches state-of-the-art performance across multiple metrics while guaranteeing feasibility.

</details>


### [103] [Improving Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning via Selective Reward Stimulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19414)
*Jing Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RSIQL is a simple non-hierarchical offline goal-conditioned RL method that adds reward signals at intermediate progress-making states to address the challenge of delayed goal completion information in sparse reward settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Goal-conditioned RL faces challenges in offline settings with sparse rewards and long-horizon dependencies, where goal-completion information is temporally distant from early decisions, and offline value estimation introduces additional errors. The paper identifies that goal-directed value separation can become small relative to local estimation error in delayed-goal settings.

Method: RSIQL (Reward Stimulation Implicit Q-Learning) introduces additional reward signals at progress-making intermediate states in offline trajectories. It uses an auxiliary goal-conditioned value function to identify intermediate states estimated to make progress toward the goal and applies reward stimulation to provide less-delayed training supervision. Unlike hierarchical methods, RSIQL does not learn a separate high-level subgoal policy.

Result: Experiments on D4RL goal-reaching benchmarks and OGBench show that RSIQL improves over goal-conditioned IQL on average and achieves performance competitive with hierarchical offline goal-conditioned methods, while retaining a simple flat policy structure.

Conclusion: RSIQL successfully addresses the challenge of delayed goal information in offline goal-conditioned RL through reward stimulation at intermediate progress-making states, achieving competitive performance with hierarchical methods while maintaining a simpler flat policy architecture.

Abstract: Goal-conditioned reinforcement learning aims to learn policies that reach specified goals, but remains challenging in offline settings with sparse rewards and long-horizon dependencies. In such settings, goal-completion information can be temporally distant from the early decisions that enable success, while offline value estimation introduces additional error. We study this issue from a reward-propagation perspective and show, in a stylized delayed-goal setting, how goal-directed value separation can become small relative to local estimation error. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Reward Stimulation Implicit Q-Learning (RSIQL), a simple non-hierarchical method that introduces additional reward signals at progress-making intermediate states in offline trajectories. RSIQL uses an auxiliary goal-conditioned value function to identify intermediate states estimated to make progress toward the goal and applies reward stimulation to provide less-delayed training supervision. Unlike hierarchical methods, RSIQL does not learn a separate high-level subgoal policy. Experiments on D4RL goal-reaching benchmarks and OGBench show that RSIQL improves over goal-conditioned IQL on average and achieves performance competitive with hierarchical offline goal-conditioned methods, while retaining a simple flat policy structure.

</details>


### [104] [Bayesian Optimization with Rich Auxiliary Information via LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19437)
*Tejus Gupta,Efe Mert Karagözlü,Rohit Sonker,Barnabás Póczos,Jeff Schnieder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-enhanced Bayesian Optimization that leverages rich auxiliary information (training curves, expert notes, images, prior knowledge) to outperform standard BO and existing LLM-based optimization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world optimization problems contain richer information beyond simple function evaluations (training curves in hyperparameter optimization, expert notes and images in scientific experimentation, prior knowledge about optima locations), but standard Bayesian Optimization doesn't effectively utilize this auxiliary information.

Method: Developed three methods for incorporating auxiliary information into Bayesian Optimization using Large Language Models (LLMs) to interpret and leverage rich contextual information.

Result: Across hyperparameter optimization benchmarks and a real-world nuclear fusion optimization task, the LLM-enhanced methods consistently outperform both standard BO and existing LLM-based optimization approaches.

Conclusion: LLMs are effective for leveraging rich auxiliary information in Bayesian Optimization, demonstrating their utility in optimization tasks where traditional methods only use limited function evaluations.

Abstract: Bayesian Optimization (BO) is widely used for optimizing expensive black-box functions, yet many real-world optimization problems contain substantially richer information than function evaluations alone. Examples include training curves in hyperparameter optimization, expert notes and images in scientific experimentation, and prior knowledge about where optima may lie. We show that large language models (LLMs) can effectively leverage such rich auxiliary information to guide optimization. Motivated by these findings, we develop three methods for incorporating auxiliary information into BO using LLMs. Across hyperparameter optimization benchmarks and a real-world nuclear fusion optimization task, our methods consistently outperform both standard BO and existing LLM-based optimization approaches. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of LLMs for leveraging rich auxiliary information in BO.

</details>


### [105] [Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) Improves Classification Accuracy of Bacterial Raman Spectral Data Enabling Portable Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19453)
*Kaitlin Zareno,Jarett Dewbury,Siamak K. Sorooshyari,Hossein Mobahi,Loza F. Tadesse*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) improves AI model generalization for Raman spectroscopy-based antibiotic resistance testing, achieving up to 10.5% accuracy gains over traditional optimizers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Antimicrobial resistance is projected to cause 10 million deaths annually by 2050, with resource-limited regions disproportionately affected. Raman spectroscopy offers rapid, portable antibiotic resistance testing but current AI algorithms suffer from poor generalization on limited datasets and require complex pre-processing steps.

Method: The researchers apply Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) to enhance model generalization across diverse hyperparameters for clinical bacterial isolate classification using Raman spectroscopy data. SAM helps models find flatter minima in the loss landscape, improving generalization.

Result: SAM achieves accuracy improvements of up to 10.5% on a single split and an average accuracy increase of 2.7% across all splits in spectral classification tasks compared to the traditional Adam optimizer.

Conclusion: SAM demonstrates significant potential to advance clinical application of AI-powered Raman spectroscopy tools for rapid antibiotic resistance testing, addressing current limitations in model generalization and reducing dependency on complex pre-processing.

Abstract: Antimicrobial resistance is expected to claim 10 million lives per year by 2050, and resource-limited regions are most affected. Raman spectroscopy is a novel pathogen diagnostic approach promising rapid and portable antibiotic resistance testing within a few hours, compared to days when using gold standard methods. However, current algorithms for Raman spectra analysis 1) are unable to generalize well on limited datasets across diverse patient populations and 2) require increased complexity due to the necessity of non-trivial pre-processing steps, such as feature extraction, which are essential to mitigate the low-quality nature of Raman spectral data. In this work, we address these limitations using Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) to enhance model generalization across a diverse array of hyperparameters in clinical bacterial isolate classification tasks. We demonstrate that SAM achieves accuracy improvements of up to 10.5% on a single split, and an increase in average accuracy of 2.7% across all splits in spectral classification tasks over the traditional optimizer, Adam. These results display the capability of SAM to advance the clinical application of AI-powered Raman spectroscopy tools.

</details>


### [106] [Enhanced Agriculture-informed Neural Network by Domain Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19466)
*Ci Lin,Futong Li,Rose Chong-Wu,Tet Yeap,Iluju Kiringa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid neural-mechanistic framework (KAINN) that incorporates agricultural domain knowledge improves N2O emission prediction accuracy and interpretability compared to purely data-driven models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate prediction of agricultural N2O emissions is important for environmental impact assessment but remains challenging due to complex interactions between soil, climate, biochemical processes, and management practices, combined with limited high-quality observations. Deep learning models can capture nonlinear relationships but often lack physical interpretability and may generalize poorly across environmental conditions.

Method: Proposes the Knowledge-enhanced Agriculture-informed Neural Network (KAINN), a hybrid neural-mechanistic framework that extends the Agriculture-informed Neural Network by incorporating domain knowledge about fertilizer diffusion, soil respiration, and water-filled porosity. Evaluated using CNN, LSTM, and Transformer architectures across multiple growing seasons and input-feature configurations.

Result: KAINN generally provides lower root mean square error and mean absolute error, and higher R-squared values than purely data-driven models and the original AINN. Analysis of learned interfaces shows smoother and more physically consistent parameter trajectories with reduced uncertainty.

Conclusion: Incorporating environmental knowledge into neural networks can improve the reliability, interpretability, and generalization of agricultural N2O-emission predictions, demonstrating the value of hybrid neural-mechanistic approaches for complex environmental modeling tasks.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions from agriculture is important for assessing environmental impacts and supporting sustainable farming. However, prediction remains difficult because N2O emissions result from complex interactions among soil properties, climate, biochemical processes, and management practices, while high-quality observations are limited. Deep learning models can capture nonlinear relationships but often lack physical interpretability and may generalize poorly across environmental conditions. We propose the Knowledge-enhanced Agriculture-informed Neural Network (KAINN), a hybrid neural-mechanistic framework that extends the Agriculture-informed Neural Network by incorporating domain knowledge about fertilizer diffusion, soil respiration, and water-filled porosity. We evaluate KAINN using CNN, LSTM, and Transformer architectures across multiple growing seasons and input-feature configurations. The results show that KAINN generally provides lower root mean square error and mean absolute error and higher R-squared values than purely data-driven models and the original AINN. Analysis of the learned interfaces also shows smoother and more physically consistent parameter trajectories with reduced uncertainty. These findings demonstrate that incorporating environmental knowledge into neural networks can improve the reliability, interpretability, and generalization of agricultural N2O-emission predictions.

</details>


### [107] [Search at the Cost of Sampling: Nearly-Instant Latent Space Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19476)
*Donney Fan,Colin Doumont,Aleksandra Kalisz,Paul Duckworth,Jacob R. Gardner,Henry Moss,Geoff Pleiss*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel Bayesian optimization method with 100x speedup for de novo discovery pipelines by exploiting linear models on spherical domains, making BO practical for previously too-slow applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bayesian optimization (BO) is well-suited for de novo discovery pipelines where designs are generated and filtered through virtual screens, but its computational overhead becomes a bottleneck when virtual screens are relatively cheap, making BO impractical despite its sequential decision-making advantages.

Method: The authors develop a fast BO method by exploiting the unique combination of a linear model constrained to a spherical domain where high-dimensional latents concentrate. They build on recent work justifying linear surrogates and derive nearly closed-form solutions for surrogate modeling and acquisition problems that leverage spherical symmetry.

Result: The method achieves at least 100x speedup over state-of-the-art baselines while maintaining matching or improved performance across molecular and image generation benchmarks.

Conclusion: This work makes BO a practical drop-in solution for de novo discovery pipelines where it was previously too slow to consider, enabling more efficient optimization in generative model applications.

Abstract: Generative models are increasingly central to many de novo discovery pipelines, in which designs are generated at scale and filtered through virtual screens to determine a set of candidates to experimentally validate. While Bayesian optimization (BO) is a natural fit for this setting, as it uses past evaluations to guide future proposals, the computational overhead required for its sequential decision-making becomes a bottleneck when virtual screens are relatively cheap. We make BO practical in this regime by exploiting the unique combination of a linear model constrained to a spherical domain where high-dimensional latents concentrate. We build off recent work justifying the use of linear surrogates, while deriving nearly closed-form solutions to the surrogate modelling and acquisition problems that exploit spherical symmetry. The result is at least a 100x speedup over state-of-the art baselines, with matching or improved performance across molecular and image generation benchmarks. Altogether, our method makes BO a practical drop-in for de novo pipelines where it was previously too slow to consider.

</details>


### [108] [Sample Count Is Not Enough: Candidate-Generation Strategy Shapes the Energy and Performance of LLM Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19499)
*Mobina Kashaniyan,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Test-time scaling with multiple candidate responses improves LLM reasoning accuracy, but the generation schedule (batch size vs number of calls) significantly impacts systems costs like latency, throughput, and energy consumption, not just candidate count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While sampling-based methods for LLM reasoning improvement typically focus on candidate count N, this doesn't capture the systems costs of different generation schedules. The same N can be achieved with different batching strategies, which may have vastly different efficiency implications.

Method: Experimental study comparing different generation schedules (1x8, 2x4, 4x2, 8x1) while fixing total candidate count N=8. Evaluated on Phi-3-mini and Qwen2.5-1.5B models using 500 GSM8K prompts, measuring latency, throughput, GPU-hours, and gross GPU-device energy on A100 GPUs, with validation across multiple A100 nodes and SciQ/V100 experiments.

Result: Increasing N from 1 to 8 improved accuracy by 8.4pp for Phi-3-mini and 18.4pp for Qwen2.5-1.5B. However, comparing generation schedules revealed dramatic efficiency differences: eight serial calls (8x1) used 4.64-4.86x more energy and had 5.77-6.12x higher P95 latency than one batched call with eight candidates (1x8), with consistent patterns across different hardware setups.

Conclusion: Candidate count alone is insufficient to describe the systems cost of multi-candidate test-time scaling. When candidates are independent and memory permits, fewer generation calls with larger batch sizes are more efficient. Evaluations should report generation schedules and GPU-level systems metrics alongside candidate count and accuracy.

Abstract: Test-time scaling can improve large language model reasoning by generating and combining multiple candidate responses. In sampling-based methods, the inference budget is often described by the number of generated candidates, N. However, N tells us how many candidates are generated, not how they are executed. The same candidate budget can be produced in one batched generation call or split across several sequential calls with smaller batch sizes. We first study the effect of increasing N on reasoning accuracy using Phi-3-mini and Qwen2.5-1.5B on 500 GSM8K prompts. As expected, increasing N from 1 to 8 improves accuracy by 8.4 percentage points for Phi-3-mini and 18.4 points for Qwen2.5-1.5B. However, accuracy alone does not show the systems cost of using a larger candidate budget. We therefore fix N = 8 and compare four generation schedules: 1x8, 2x4, 4x2, and 8x1, where axb denotes a generation calls with b candidates per call. We measure latency, throughput, GPU-hours, and gross GPU-device energy while keeping the total candidate count fixed. On A100 GPUs, eight serial calls use 4.64-4.86x as much gross GPU-device energy and have 5.77-6.12x the P95 latency of one batched call with eight candidates. The same pattern appears across three independently scheduled A100 nodes per model and in short-output SciQ/V100 experiments. These results show that candidate count alone is not enough to describe the systems cost of multi-candidate test-time scaling. When candidates are independent and memory allows it, fewer generation calls with larger batch sizes are more efficient. Evaluations should therefore report not only candidate count and accuracy, but also generation schedule and GPU-level systems metrics.

</details>


### [109] [LSTM-UT and Recurrent-Depth Transformers on Cellular Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19521)
*Aras Kavuncu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Universal Transformers with bounded gated memory (LSTM-UT) outperform other recurrent-depth architectures like Block Universal Transformer and CoTFormer on tasks requiring repeated computation and information retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To compare different recurrent-depth transformer architectures that apply shared computation repeatedly but differ in how they retain information across steps, particularly examining their extrapolation capabilities and ability to retrieve information from past states.

Method: Comparison of three architectures: 1) Block Universal Transformer (BUT) with only current hidden state, 2) CoTFormer with expanding attention cache, and 3) new LSTM Universal Transformer (LSTM-UT) with bounded gated memory. Experiments include Rule 30 cellular automata for depth extrapolation and delayed-recall tasks. Also conducted state and cache interventions to analyze failure modes.

Result: BUT extrapolated to unseen recurrent depths more reliably than CoTFormer on Rule 30, though its accuracy eventually degraded. CoTFormer's failures depended on interaction between current state and retained history - correcting state temporarily restored accuracy while retained history undermined corrections. In delayed-recall, BUT outperformed CoTFormer despite lacking direct past state access. LSTM-UT improved both depth extrapolation and delayed recall over both baselines.

Conclusion: Bounded gated memory (as in LSTM-UT) provides an effective inductive bias for repeated computation and later retrieval tasks, outperforming architectures with only current state retention or expanding attention caches.

Abstract: Recurrent-depth Transformers apply shared computation repeatedly, but differ in how they retain information across steps. We compare a Block Universal Transformer (BUT), which carries only its current hidden state; CoTFormer, which also retains an expanding attention cache; and a new LSTM Universal Transformer (LSTM-UT) with bounded gated memory. On Rule 30 cellular automata, BUT extrapolates to unseen recurrent depths more reliably than CoTFormer, although its accuracy eventually degrades. State and cache interventions show that CoTFormer's failure depends on their interaction: correcting the current state can temporarily restore accuracy, while retained history can undermine that correction. In a delayed-recall task, BUT also outperforms CoTFormer despite lacking direct access to past states; CoTFormer does not reliably select the requested cached representation. LSTM-UT improves both depth extrapolation and delayed recall over these baselines. The results support bounded gated memory as an effective inductive bias for repeated computation and later retrieval in these tasks.

</details>


### [110] [Compressed Active Subspaces for Scalable Bayesian Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19539)
*Thomas Flynn,Sanket Jantre,Byung-Jun Yoon,Kibaek Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAS enables scalable active subspace methods for high-dimensional models by using compressed parameter embeddings, reducing memory requirements while maintaining accurate uncertainty estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard active subspace methods require storing many full-dimensional model gradients, which becomes prohibitive for large-scale models, limiting their practical application to high-dimensional problems.

Method: Compressed Active Subspaces (CAS) first maps model parameters to a compressed space using structured isometric embedding, then constructs the active subspace within this reduced parameterization, dramatically lowering memory requirements.

Result: CAS demonstrates scalability on neural networks of increasing size while maintaining predictive performance and robust uncertainty estimates, making Bayesian inference practical for large models where standard active subspace methods fail.

Conclusion: CAS provides a scalable approach to active subspace methods that enables uncertainty quantification for high-dimensional models previously considered impractical, bridging the gap between theoretical active subspace methods and real-world large-scale applications.

Abstract: Active subspace methods provide a framework for quantifying predictive uncertainty in high-dimensional models by identifying and performing inference along parameter directions that have the greatest influence on the model output. However, the construction of active subspaces requires storing many full-dimensional model gradients, which becomes prohibitive as model size increases. We address this limitation by proposing Compressed Active Subspaces (CAS), a scalable approach that first maps the model parameters to a compressed space using a structured isometric embedding and then constructs the active subspace within this reduced parameterization. Our approach substantially reduces the memory required for active subspace construction and enables Bayesian inference for large models where standard active subspace methods become impractical. We demonstrate the scalability of CAS on neural networks of increasing size while maintaining predictive performance and robust uncertainty estimates.

</details>


### [111] [FedFIbOS: Fisher Importance based Optimal Submodelling for Heterogeneous Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19559)
*Yasmeen Afzal,Jeremiah D. Deng,Haibo Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedFIbOS is a new heterogeneous federated learning method that uses Fisher Information for principled parameter selection in capacity-constrained submodels, achieving better convergence and ~10% higher accuracy than state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing heterogeneous federated learning methods use heuristic importance measures (like parameter magnitude) for submodel parameter selection without theoretical justification. There's a fundamental gap in convergence theory, especially with partial client participation introducing estimation effects in Fisher scores.

Method: Proposes FedFIbOS: Fisher Importance-based Optimal Submodelling. Uses Fisher Information derived from minimizing submodel masking error. Formulates submodel selection through Fisher-weighted quadratic masking surrogate, showing raw Fisher top-k rule solves this surrogate under Fisher-dominant ranking condition. Fisher scores are efficiently estimated from empirical diagonal Fisher information using squared gradients.

Result: Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and AGNews under pathological and Dirichlet non-IID settings show FedFIbOS achieves approximately 10% higher accuracy than state-of-the-art methods, with improvements becoming more pronounced under stronger heterogeneity.

Conclusion: FedFIbOS provides a theoretically grounded approach to parameter selection in heterogeneous federated learning using Fisher Information, addressing the limitations of heuristic methods and demonstrating superior performance, especially under challenging heterogeneous conditions.

Abstract: Heterogeneous federated learning requires clients with diverse computational capacities to collaboratively train a global model, where each client trains a capacity-constrained submodel. Existing methods select submodel parameters using heuristic importance measures---most prominently parameter magnitude---without theoretical justification for why these measures support convergence. We identify a fundamental gap: existing parameter selection criteria lack theoretical grounding in the convergence framework, partial client participation introduces additional estimation effects in the Fisher scores. We propose \textbf{FedFIbOS}: Fisher Importance-based Optimal Submodelling for heterogeneous federated learning, using Fisher Information in a principled criterion derived from minimizing submodel masking error. %We formally establish when magnitude selection is equivalent to Fisher selection fail under non-IID heterogeneous federated learning. We theoretically formulate submodel selection through a Fisher-weighted quadratic masking surrogate and show that the raw Fisher top-$k$ rule implemented by FedFIbOS solves this surrogate under a Fisher-dominant ranking condition. The resulting method retains the convergence structure of the underlying masked federated optimization bound. Fisher scores are efficiently estimated from empirical diagonal Fisher information using squared gradients, enabling stable and adaptive parameter selection without additional optimization overhead. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and AGNews under pathological and Dirichlet non-IID settings show FedFIbOS achieves ${\approx}10\%$ higher accuracy than the state of the art, with improvements becoming more pronounced under stronger heterogeneity.

</details>


### [112] [The Complexity Kink: A Prompt-Side Structural Complexity Index for Code-Generation Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19616)
*Michael Hernandez,Tian Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers developed a six-dimensional prompt complexity index measured before code generation, showing nonmonotonic relationship between prompt complexity and LLM pass rates, with task types and construction frames influencing failure patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing complexity metrics based on generated code are failure-dependent (difficult prompts can yield short failing programs with low complexity scores). Need pre-generation structural complexity assessment separate from correctness to better understand LLM performance patterns.

Method: Created six-dimension prompt-side structural-complexity index scored before generation. Collected 5,000 Python prompts across six complexity bands. Used 4 LLM raters for scoring (19,997 score rows). Evaluated 21 models per prompt (105,000 generations). Conducted fixed-effects analysis with task-type and construction-frame controls.

Result: Unadjusted analysis shows pass rate has nonmonotonic breakpoint at composite score 13.75 (79.9% pass below vs 87.6% above). Task-type fixed effects shift breakpoint to 10.75 and reduce gap to 2.1 points. Construction-frame control shifts to 8.50 with -3.5 point gap. 28.5% of zero-pass generations pair high prompt complexity (above 8) with low output complexity (≤10).

Conclusion: The paper contributes a pre-generation measurement framework for prompt complexity and bounded observational analysis showing reliability regimes in LLM performance. The six-dimension index shows predictive value but overidentification tests reject joint restrictions, suggesting treating composite as index rather than making causal claims.

Abstract: Complexity measured from generated code is failure-dependent: a difficult prompt can yield a short failing program and be assigned low output complexity. We introduce a six-dimension prompt-side structural-complexity index scored before generation and kept separate from correctness. We select 5,000 Python prompts across six bands of a preliminary single-rater rubric. Four out-of-panel LLM raters rescore the locked prompts, giving 19,997 score rows; composite inter-rater reliability is ICC = 0.872 on the 4,998 prompts with all four ratings. We evaluate 21 models per prompt, yielding 105,000 generations. In the unadjusted mean-pooled analysis, pass rate has a nonmonotone breakpoint at composite 13.75, with 79.9% at or below and 87.6% above. This is not a universal failure cutoff. Task-type fixed effects shift the breakpoint to 10.75 and cut the regime gap from 7.6 to 2.1 points. A construction-frame control shifts it to 8.50 with a raw gap of -3.5 points, and neither frame alone reproduces the pooled +7.6-point change. Model-specific fits include 16 upward and five downward changes. A 365-prompt audit-clean extension matches the original five-model estimates at bins 15 and 16 but adds only 14 prompts above bin 16. Among zero-pass generations with computable Lizard complexity, 28.5% pair a prompt composite above 8 with output complexity at most 10. Human agreement is moderate and rater-dependent on a disagreement-enriched calibration set; paraphrase and cross-language rescoring preserve score ordering. Overidentification tests reject the joint restrictions on the six dimensions, so we treat the composite as an index and make no causal interpretation of the 2SLS estimates. The contribution is a pre-generation measurement framework and a bounded observational analysis of reliability regimes.

</details>


### [113] [A Policy Profile for Croissant: Refusal as a Property of the Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19640)
*Alexander Chernov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents an evaluation framework for Croissant ML dataset descriptors, addressing the missing decision procedures and evaluation semantics for data use conditions by proposing an additive profile with explicit operators and guaranteed decision procedures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Croissant (machine-readable descriptor for ML datasets) versions don't specify how to evaluate data use conditions - no decision procedures, cost bounds, outcomes for unevaluable conditions, records of what was checked, or composition with caller-side authority.

Method: Proposes an additive profile allowing datasets to declare operations they admit and conditions for admitting them, using a closed set of five operators with fully specified decision procedures. This enables gates to make decisions from descriptors alone and record what was checked.

Result: Evaluation shows: 1) Decisions from profile documents match native descriptor records (3 real nf-core pipeline descriptors), 2) Profile layer removal leaves valid Croissant documents, 3) Added evaluation cost is 11.7μs vs 119μs base decision time, 4) Generated corpus covers all operators, refusal classes and conformance clauses with 552 decision records agreeing across three validation methods (native descriptor, profile terms, and ODRL usageInfo).

Conclusion: The core contribution is not the carrier mechanism but the evaluation semantics. Caller-bound and data-bound policies operate over non-overlapping state spaces, meaning neither's permission set contains the other, establishing clear boundaries for authorization decisions.

Abstract: Croissant is the de facto machine-readable descriptor for ML datasets: JSON-LD over schema.org. Since version 1.1 it also carries data use conditions, recommending DUO and ODRL for them. What no version specifies is how any of them is evaluated: no decision procedure, no bound on evaluation cost, no outcome for a condition an implementation cannot evaluate, no record of what was checked, and nothing on composition with caller-side authority. We supply that half. An additive profile lets a dataset declare the operations it admits and the conditions under which it admits them, over a closed set of five operators whose decision procedure is given in full, so a gate decides from the descriptor alone and records what it checked. Two corpora evaluate it and their evidence is kept apart. Three descriptors that gated a real nf-core pipeline give the deployment result: decisions from a profile document match the gate's native descriptor record for record, stripping the layer leaves a valid Croissant document, and the added cost is 11.7 $μ$s against a 119 $μ$s decision. A corpus generated from the profile's grammar gives the breadth, covering every operator, refusal class and conformance clause. Across its valid cases, 552 complete decision records agree three ways -- native descriptor, profile terms, and the same policy as ODRL in usageInfo. The carrier is therefore not the contribution; the evaluation semantics is. Finally, caller-bound and data-bound policies range over non-overlapping state spaces, so neither permit set contains the other.

</details>


### [114] [CoRe: Coherence and Relational Alignment for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19670)
*Xiaoyu Lin,Huiran Duan,Yining Liu,Zhixiang Wu,Chu Lin,Lin Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoRe is a model-agnostic learning objective for direct multivariate time-series forecasting that replaces pointwise supervision with frequency coherence and relational graph losses to preserve temporal and cross-variable structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current direct forecasting methods use pointwise error objectives like MSE that don't explicitly preserve the structure of future trajectories, weakening temporal coherence within variables and relational consistency across variables.

Method: CoRe introduces two output-space constraints: 1) frequency coherence loss aligning predicted and target spectra, and 2) low-rank relational graph loss matching sampled pairwise differences in a target-derived PCA subspace. The approach is model-agnostic with no trainable parameters.

Result: Experiments on standard benchmarks show CoRe improves strong baselines, compares favorably with recent forecasting objectives, and remains effective across different backbones, datasets, and hyperparameter settings.

Conclusion: CoRe provides an effective model-agnostic alternative to pointwise supervision for multivariate forecasting that better preserves trajectory structure through frequency and relational constraints.

Abstract: Direct forecasting has become a standard paradigm for multivariate time-series forecasting because it predicts the full future horizon in a single pass. However, its training objective is often still decomposed into pointwise errors such as MSE. Such objectives provide stable supervision, but they do not explicitly preserve the structure of the future trajectory: temporal coherence within each variable and relational consistency across variables can both be weakened. We propose CoRe, a model-agnostic learning objective for direct multivariate forecasting. CoRe replaces pointwise supervision with two output-space constraints: a frequency coherence loss that aligns predicted and target spectra, and a low-rank relational graph loss that matches sampled pairwise differences in a target-derived PCA subspace. The resulting objective introduces no trainable parameters and can be applied to existing forecasting backbones by changing only the loss. Experiments on standard benchmarks show that CoRe improves strong baselines, compares favorably with recent forecasting objectives, and remains effective across different backbones, datasets, and hyperparameter settings overall consistently.

</details>


### [115] [Conservation Buys Stability and Factoring Buys Counterfactuals in Physical World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19674)
*Yufeng Wang,Parivesh Priye,Lu Wei,Haibin Ling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learned physics simulator needs different structural mechanisms for long-horizon stability versus counterfactual generalization to unseen physical laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned simulators can fail in two distinct ways: accumulating small errors leading to divergence over long rollouts, and failing to adapt to interventions on physical parameters due to being locked to training laws.

Method: Use symplectic integrator with learned energy for long-horizon stability (preserves conservative dynamics geometry), and explicit linear factorization of physical coupling for counterfactual transfer to unseen laws. Conduct double dissociation experiments with matched controls.

Result: Symplectic integration enables 100× longer stable rollouts than baseline models, while factorization enables generalization to never-seen coupling signs. These mechanisms are separable - each can work independently of the other.

Conclusion: Long-horizon stability and changed-law generalization require distinct structural commitments in physical world models, and can be imposed deliberately without requiring each other, providing concrete design principles.

Abstract: A learned simulator can reproduce its training conditions accurately yet fail in two distinct ways once those conditions change. Over long rollouts, small errors accumulate until the trajectory drifts away from physically plausible behavior; under an intervention on a physical parameter, the model may continue to follow the law seen during training rather than the intervened one. We show that these two failures require different structural remedies. Evolving a learned energy with a symplectic integrator preserves the geometry of the conservative dynamics and keeps rollouts bounded and physically meaningful for up to $100\times$ the training horizon, while equal-capacity predictors, an energy-regularized predictor, and a tuned neural ODE diverge. By contrast, encoding the physical coupling through an explicit linear factorization enables the model to follow a never-seen sign of that coupling, whereas an unrestricted parameterization remains locked to the training law. Crucially, the two mechanisms are separable: removing the structure responsible for long-horizon stability leaves counterfactual transfer intact, while removing the factorized coupling destroys counterfactual transfer without eliminating stability. This double dissociation, established with matched controls that remove or replace one structural component at a time, persists beyond the headline three-body system and remains visible when the physical state must be inferred from pixels rather than provided directly. The result is a concrete design principle for physical world models: long-horizon stability and changed-law generalization arise from distinct structural commitments, and each can be imposed deliberately without requiring the other.

</details>


### [116] [Opinion Dynamics-based Coalition Formation for Federated Learning in Heterogeneous IoT Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19695)
*Mohammed El Hanjri,Anas Abouaomar,Hamidou Tembine,Abdellatif Kobbane*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated learning with Hegselmann-Krause opinion dynamics for coalition formation improves forecasting accuracy on heterogeneous IoT data by grouping similar clients in weight space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard FedAvg fails to capture client-specific patterns in statistically heterogeneous IoT deployments like smart-water-metering networks, where each household has unique consumption patterns. Averaging dissimilar local models into a single global model leads to poor performance.

Method: Proposes forming client coalitions directly in local-weight space using Hegselmann-Krause (HK) bounded-confidence opinion dynamics. Develops HK variants based on Euclidean-distance and cosine-similarity confidence criteria to group similar models. Applied to water-consumption forecasting with local LSTM models.

Result: HK-based coalition formation produces stable coalition structures within 10 iterations with no extra client-side computation/communication vs FedAvg. Reduces average MAE by up to 54% vs FedAvg, 39% vs FedProx, and 24% vs Per-FedAvg, while achieving 83-85% global accuracy on real smart-metering data.

Conclusion: The HK opinion-dynamics approach effectively handles statistical heterogeneity in FL by forming endogenous coalitions, significantly improving forecasting accuracy without additional computational or communication overhead compared to standard FL methods.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables privacy-preserving, on-device training across heterogeneous Internet-of-Things (IoT) deployments such as smart-city water-metering networks, where each smart meter observes a household-specific consumption time series. Under such statistical heterogeneity, the standard Federated Averaging (FedAvg) aggregation averages dissimilar local models into a single global model that may fail to capture client-specific patterns. We address this by forming client coalitions directly in the local-weight space and aggregating at the coalition level. Extending a prior weight-driven coalition-formation scheme, we model coalition formation as a Hegselmann-Krause (HK) bounded-confidence opinion-dynamics process acting on the local weights, and develop variants of the HK interaction based on Euclidean-distance and cosine-similarity confidence criteria. The framework is applied to short-term water-consumption forecasting with local Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) models and evaluated against FedAvg, Per-FedAvg, FedProx, and FedAvg with Euclidean-distance or cosine-similarity coalition formation. Experiments on a real smart-metering dataset of water consumption show that the proposed HK-based coalition formation produces stable, endogenous coalition structures within at most ten inner iterations, incurs no additional client-side computation or communication compared to FedAvg, and reduces the average MAE by up to 54% relative to FedAvg, 39% relative to FedProx, and 24% relative to Per-FedAvg, while achieving the highest global accuracy (83-85%).

</details>


### [117] [Odds-Ratio Thompson Sampling: A Specification and Design Guide for Contrast-Based Multi-Armed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19709)
*Sulgi Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OR-TS (Odds-Ratio Thompson Sampling) improves batched multi-armed bandits by tracking relative arm contrasts rather than absolute reward rates, better handling changing baseline levels between batches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional batched bandits maintain absolute reward rates between updates, but these become stale when the baseline level changes between batches, even if relative arm performance remains stable.

Method: OR-TS maintains joint posterior over log-odds contrasts between arms and fits the common baseline level afresh in each batch, marginalizing it out. The approach includes decay (for evidence survival) and aggressiveness (for belief-to-allocation sharpness) controls.

Result: Across 86 public A/B tests, baseline level varied ~25× more than contrasts. In synthetic environments, moving baselines cost absolute-rate memory 5× more regret and failed to give best arm majority traffic in 7/20 runs (vs. 0 for OR-TS). In real experiments, OR-TS identified better arm >2× more often despite small performance differences.

Conclusion: OR-TS outperforms absolute-rate memory in batched bandits when baseline levels change, though it still fails when arm contrasts themselves change substantially.

Abstract: Batched multi-armed bandits update on a service's own schedule, and the usual implementation carries each arm's absolute reward rate from one update to the next. When the shared level moves between batches, that memory goes stale even though the comparisons between arms may not have. Odds-Ratio Thompson Sampling (OR-TS) instead carries the joint posterior over log-odds contrasts and fits the common level afresh in every batch, marginalizing it out. This paper specifies that update, places it inside a Bayesian bandit agent with two controls, decay for how much past evidence survives an update and aggressiveness for how sharply belief becomes allocation, and evaluates it against absolute-rate memory. Across 86 public A/B series the level varies about twenty-five times more than the contrast. In prespecified synthetic environments a moving level costs absolute-rate memory five times the regret and leaves the best arm below a majority of traffic in 7 of 20 runs, against none for OR-TS. In a policy simulation built from 71 real experiments, where the contrasts are too small to resolve, expected-click differences stay within 0.1% for 58 of them, yet contrast memory still ends on the better arm more than twice as often. Where the contrasts themselves move, the bet fails, and that case is reported too.

</details>


### [118] [Learn Your Own Thoughts: Abstract Token Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19717)
*Khashayar Gatmiry,Avrajit Ghosh,Parsa Mirtaheri,Jason D. Lee,Nika Haghtalab,Emmanuel Abbe,Peter Bartlett*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ATC is a curriculum learning framework that trains LLMs to develop continuous intermediate representations (abstract thoughts) without explicit supervision, improving reasoning capabilities over chain-of-thought methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought techniques require explicit supervision on thinking tokens and task-specific data, limiting their applicability. There's a need for methods that can elicit effective intermediate representations without such direct supervision or manual scratchpad design.

Method: Abstract Token Curriculum (ATC) gradually increases problem complexity through a sequence of distributions, training models to develop internal abstract "thoughts" in continuous representation space without direct supervision or manual scratchpad design.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows ATC enables attention to focus on CoT tokens providing the "easiest path" for predictions. Experimental results demonstrate ATC's effectiveness on graph reachability and arithmetic learning tasks, showing advantages over previous continuous thought training methods.

Conclusion: ATC provides a novel curriculum learning approach that successfully trains LLMs to develop continuous intermediate representations for reasoning tasks without explicit supervision, offering both theoretical guarantees and practical benefits over existing methods.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable reasoning capabilities by utilizing chain-of-thought (CoT) as a scratchpad for intermediate stages of thinking. However, CoT techniques require explicit supervision on thinking tokens, which requires rich, task-specific data. In this work, we propose Abstract Token Curriculum (ATC), a novel curriculum learning framework that elicits effective continuous intermediate representations without direct supervision or manual scratchpad design. ATC gradually increases problem complexity through a sequence of distributions, training the model to develop internal abstract ``thoughts'' in the continuous representation space. This paper provides both theoretical and experimental evidence for the benefits of ATC and its advantages over previous methods for training continuous thoughts. Theoretically, we show that for learning parity functions with single-layer softmax attention using ATC, attention naturally focuses on the CoT tokens in the context that provide the ``easiest path'' to predicting the next token. Experimentally, we show ATC's effectiveness on graph reachability and arithmetic learning tasks.

</details>


### [119] [Alliance Beats Isolation: Unifying Heterogeneous Allied Datasets Improves Classifier Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19748)
*Girish Keshav Palshikar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method to merge feature spaces of allied heterogeneous datasets (same type objects, same labels, largely distinct features) using matrix completion to create a unified representation that improves classifier performance across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world domains have multiple labelled public datasets that cannot be directly combined for classification because they have heterogeneous feature spaces with only a few shared features, even though they describe the same type of objects with the same class labels. This prevents knowledge transfer between datasets.

Method: Propose a method to merge feature spaces of paired allied heterogeneous datasets into a single unified feature space, then use matrix completion to create a unified dataset based on this merged representation.

Result: Experiments on several pairs of allied heterogeneous datasets show that classifiers trained on the unified representation consistently outperform classifiers separately trained on the constituent allied datasets across various domains.

Conclusion: The proposed merging approach effectively facilitates classification knowledge transfer between allied datasets, providing an easy way to substantially improve classifier performance by unifying and using multiple allied datasets together.

Abstract: In many application domains, such as student dropout, insurance fraud, loan approval, and machine failures, several labelled public datasets are available where (i) data is about the same type of objects but the set of actual underlying objects are disjoint; and (ii) the class labels are same; and (iii) the feature spaces of the datasets are largely distinct (heterogeneous), with a few shared features. We call such datasets as allied. A single classifier cannot be trained on both datasets together, and one classifier trained on one dataset cannot be tested on the other. In this paper, we propose a method to merge the feature-spaces into a single feature-space for a pair of given allied heterogeneous datasets. We then use a matrix completion method to create a unified dataset based on the merged feature-space. The hypothesis is that the merged representation facilitates the transfer of classification knowledge from one dataset to another. We conduct experiments on several pairs of allied, heterogeneous datasets and several classifiers to demonstrate that any classifier trained on the unified representation always outperforms classifiers separately trained on the constituent allied datasets on several pairs of allied datasets. This work provides an easy way to substantially improve classifier performance by unifying and using multiple allied datasets together.

</details>


### [120] [OceanMoE: Structured Conditional Sparse Computation for Long-Horizon Multivariate Ocean Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19768)
*Yishun Zhu,Jian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OceanMoE: A structured sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework for multivariate ocean forecasting that balances shared context with adaptive specialization through conditional computation and spatial-aware routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multivariate ocean forecasting needs to exploit shared evolution in coupled ocean systems while adapting to heterogeneous statistical/dynamical characteristics across different variables and locations. Fully shared models lack flexibility for heterogeneity, while fully independent models discard common ocean context.

Method: Proposes OceanMoE, a structured conditional sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework that fuses cross-variable information to construct target-specific local representations, performs content-conditioned sparse routing at each spatial location with adaptive expert activation, and includes a decoder with learned geographic bias parameterized by spherical-harmonic bases plus shared residual/seasonal pathways.

Result: Experiments on long-horizon autoregressive ORAS5 forecasting show OceanMoE lowers aggregate forecasting error in both settings, maintains lower geometric-mean normalized RMSE than baselines over most later rollout months, and routing analysis shows expert allocation varies with prediction targets and spatial locations.

Conclusion: Structured conditional computation is an effective modeling strategy for balancing shared ocean context with adaptive specialization in multivariate ocean forecasting.

Abstract: Multivariate ocean forecasting must exploit shared evolution in a coupled ocean system while adapting to the heterogeneous statistical and dynamical characteristics of different prediction variables and locations. Fully shared models may lack the flexibility to handle this heterogeneity, whereas fully independent models discard the common ocean context shared across variables. The key question is how to retain shared context in a unified model while allowing computation to specialize according to the prediction target and local state. We propose OceanMoE, a structured conditional sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework that combines sharing and specialization for multivariate ocean forecasting. OceanMoE fuses cross-variable information to construct target-specific local representations and uses them to perform content-conditioned sparse routing at each spatial location, with the number of active experts adapted to router confidence. In the decoder, routing is augmented with a learned geographic bias parameterized by spherical-harmonic spatial bases, while shared residual and seasonal pathways provide common cross-variable and month-dependent context. Experiments on long-horizon autoregressive ORAS5 forecasting show that OceanMoE lowers aggregate forecasting error in both evaluated settings and maintains lower geometric-mean normalized RMSE than the corresponding baselines over most later rollout months. Routing analyses further show that expert allocation varies with prediction targets and spatial locations. These results support structured conditional computation as a modeling strategy for balancing shared ocean context with adaptive specialization.

</details>


### [121] [PhyRestore: Physics-Structured Latent-Factor Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19776)
*Ahmed Shafee,Chayan Lahiri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PhyRestore is a physics-structured framework that restores corrupted physical factors in soil-loss estimation instead of directly predicting change, showing improved recovery of high-magnitude soil-loss events when corrupted factors remain identifiable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating temporal soil-loss change is challenging when physically meaningful input factors are noisy or corrupted, especially because substantial changes are rare relative to the large number of locations with little change.

Method: PhyRestore uses a physics-structured latent-factor restoration framework that restores corrupted physical factors and reconstructs temporal change through known physical relationships, specifically applied to the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation (RUSLE). The method was evaluated in watershed-scale bitemporal raster settings with isolated and simultaneous corruption of rainfall erosivity and cover management factors.

Result: Factor restoration improves high-magnitude recovery when corrupted factors remain identifiable, but its advantage weakens under joint corruption, sparse positive extremes, and factor values outside the training support. The method was compared against degraded RUSLE estimates and Direct RF, XGBoost, MLP, and CNN models.

Conclusion: Physics-structured restoration of corrupted factors can enhance soil-loss change estimation, particularly for rare high-magnitude events, but performance is limited by factors like simultaneous corruption and values outside the training data distribution.

Abstract: Estimating temporal soil-loss change is challenging when physically meaningful input factors are noisy or corrupted, particularly because substantial changes are rare relative to the large number of locations exhibiting little change. We study this problem through the Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation (RUSLE) and introduce PhyRestore, a physics-structured latent-factor restoration framework. Rather than directly predicting soil-loss change or correcting a degraded physical estimate, PhyRestore restores corrupted physical factors and reconstructs temporal change through the known physical relationship. We evaluate PhyRestore in a watershed-scale bitemporal raster setting under isolated and simultaneous corruption of rainfall erosivity and cover management, comparing it with the degraded RUSLE estimate and Direct RF, XGBoost, MLP, and CNN models. Factor restoration improves high-magnitude recovery when the corrupted factors remain identifiable, but its advantage weakens under joint corruption, sparse positive extremes, and factor values outside the training support.

</details>


### [122] [Learning-Based Reconstruction of Optical Properties in Bilayered Media from Single-distance Time-Resolved Reflectance Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19786)
*Caterina Amendola,Giulia Maffeis,Lorenzo Buffoni,Lorenzo Chicchi,Francesco Coghi,Duccio Fanelli,Raffaele Marino,Fabrizio Martelli,Riccardo Paoli,Lorenzo Pattelli,Lorenzo Spinelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Machine learning approach outperforms traditional model-based methods for reconstructing optical properties in layered biological media from time-domain reflectance measurements, offering faster reconstruction and better accuracy without requiring prior knowledge of layer structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional analytical models and diffusion equation-based inverse solvers struggle with reconstructing optical properties in layered biological media, especially with structural heterogeneity. They have poor accuracy for superficial absorption and deep-layer scattering, creating a need for better approaches.

Method: Developed a machine learning framework trained on a robust synthetic dataset generated using exact Monte Carlo simulations of forward DTOF (Diffuse Time-of-Flight) at multiple source-detector distances. The pipeline was benchmarked against state-of-the-art model-based reconstruction methods.

Result: The machine learning approach achieves significant speed-up and higher accuracy than model-based inverse solvers. It can estimate parameter space dimensionality without requiring prior information about the number of layers in the geometry.

Conclusion: Machine learning provides a superior alternative to traditional model-based methods for optical property reconstruction in layered media, with potential for further accuracy improvements through multi-distance reconstruction approaches and training on multiple DTOF curves from the same medium.

Abstract: The inverse problem of reconstructing optical properties, specifically absorption and scattering coefficients, in layered biological media from time-domain reflectance measurements remains a significant challenge for traditional analytical models. Inverse solvers based on the diffusion equation often struggle with structural heterogeneity, frequently yielding poor accuracy for superficial absorption and deep-layers scattering. In this work, we propose a machine learning framework as an alternative approach to reconstruct the optical properties of a bilayered medium, benchmarking its efficiency and accuracy against model-based algorithms. To overcome the intrinsic approximations of diffusion theory and inverse reconstruction, we generated a robust synthetic dataset of forward DTOF using exact Monte Carlo simulations at multiple source-detector distances. A machine learning pipeline was then trained on this dataset and validated against state-of-the-art model-based reconstruction methods. Besides the significant reconstruction speed-up, the machine learning approach achieves higher accuracy than model-based inverse solvers, further providing an estimate of the parameter space dimensionality without requiring any a priori information about the number of layers in the investigated geometry. Further enhancements in the reconstruction accuracy can be expected in future extensions of this work, by training the pipeline over multiple DTOF curves from the same medium, in a joint multi-distance reconstruction approach.

</details>


### [123] [DeliveryGym: An RL Environment for Long-Horizon Embodied Agent Planning with Adaptive Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19801)
*Haoqiang Kang,Yiming Zhang,Yiyang Guo,Chuying Li,Jianzhi Shen,Tianruo Rose Xu,Xiaokang Ye,Lianhui Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeliveryGym is a 3D simulation environment that enables LLM agents to learn delivery planning across complete courier shifts by considering persistent resource constraints and allowing reinforcement learning from trajectory feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embodied AI environments focus on single-task completion but lack the ability to capture dependencies between actions across extended periods, particularly the consumption of resources (time, energy, money) that affects future performance. Current environments don't provide appropriate feedback for learning to plan sequences of deliveries while managing limited resources.

Method: DeliveryGym is a 3D environment that: 1) Couples multimodal tool interaction with persistent world dynamics, 2) Computes trajectory rewards from simulator events to make decision costs available for RL, 3) Adapts future training shifts to policy weaknesses while keeping evaluation fixed, 4) Provides continuous courier shift scenarios across 13 different city maps for comprehensive evaluation.

Result: Evaluation across 6 models and 13 city maps reveals a gap between executing single deliveries and planning complete shifts. RL improves Qwen3-VL-4B's net income by 54.3% on fixed tests, showing learning from complete shifts is effective. Environment adaptation during training improves test income by 16.5% over uniform sampling, demonstrating the importance of targeted practice.

Conclusion: DeliveryGym provides an executable environment for studying how agents learn to coordinate deliveries and manage resources across episodes, showing that learning from complete shifts with persistent constraints significantly improves planning performance, and that adapting training scenarios to policy weaknesses further enhances learning efficiency.

Abstract: Executable environments enable LLM agents to learn from the consequences of their actions. For embodied agents, those consequences extend beyond whether the current task succeeds: completing a delivery can consume the time, energy, or money needed for later work. Learning to plan therefore requires environments that preserve these dependencies and turn them into feedback across a complete trajectory. We introduce DeliveryGym, a 3D environment for evaluating and training agents on continuous courier shifts. It couples multimodal tool interaction with persistent world dynamics and computes trajectory rewards from simulator events, making the costs of an agent's decisions available for reinforcement learning (RL). The environment also adapts future training shifts to the policy's observed weaknesses while keeping evaluation fixed. Across six models and 13 city maps, evaluation exposes a gap between reliably executing assigned deliveries and choosing and sequencing work over a shift. On the fixed test suite, RL improves Qwen3-VL-4B's net income by 54.3%, showing that learning from complete shifts improves performance under these coupled constraints. Adapting the training environment improves test income by 16.5% over uniform sampling at the same rollout budget, indicating that which situations an agent practices also matters. DeliveryGym provides an executable setting for studying how agents learn to coordinate deliveries and preserve resources for later orders within an episode.

</details>


### [124] [Beyond Flattened Tokens: Structure-Preserving EEG Decoding with Reusable TriDim Blocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19842)
*Shiyue Su,Song Wang,Zekai Zhan,Junjie Zeng,Ziling Lu,Zongsheng Li,Xinyuan Ye,Zhiyuan Ma,Xinke Shen,Quanying Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TriDimEEG is a new EEG decoding architecture that preserves three distinct EEG axes (channel, intra-patch samples, inter-patch positions) in a reusable block, achieving state-of-the-art performance across multiple EEG tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EEG architectures struggle to effectively capture and coordinate three crucial EEG structures: channel organization (spatial), local waveform dynamics (short-term temporal), and long-range temporal context. Most approaches either use separate specialized modules or collapse these into a single token sequence, making it difficult to maintain their distinct roles and coordinate interactions throughout the backbone.

Method: Proposes TriDim, a reusable block that preserves EEG representation shape with three explicit axes: channel (spatial), sample position within each patch (short-term temporal), and patch position across the recording (long-term temporal). Each TriDim block applies feed-forward transformations along individual axes and cross-axis attention to coordinate information exchange among them. TriDimEEG is constructed by stacking TriDim blocks with multi-level tri-axis readout.

Result: Under strict cross-subject evaluation on eight datasets spanning clinical diagnosis, sleep staging, motor imagery, and emotion recognition, TriDimEEG achieves best overall performance among fifteen models, with 4.3% relative improvement in average accuracy over second-best model. When replacing Transformer blocks in three EEG foundation models with TriDim blocks, yields average relative improvement of 7.4% in downstream accuracy while reducing parameter counts by 17.0% to 47.3%.

Conclusion: TriDim is established as an effective and reusable building block for EEG processing, and TriDimEEG serves as a strong standalone EEG decoder. The approach successfully preserves and coordinates the three distinct EEG axes while achieving superior performance and parameter efficiency.

Abstract: Effective EEG decoding requires representations that preserve organization among channels, local waveform dynamics, and long-range temporal context. Existing EEG architectures often capture these structures using separate specialized modules or collapse them into a single token sequence, making it difficult to maintain their distinct roles and coordinate their interactions throughout the backbone. We propose TriDim, a reusable block that preserves the representation shape and keeps three EEG axes explicit: channel, sample position within each patch, and patch position across the recording. These axes correspond to spatial, short-term temporal, and long-term temporal information, respectively. Each TriDim block applies feed-forward transformations along individual axes and cross-axis attention to coordinate information exchange among them. By stacking TriDim blocks with a multi-level tri-axis readout, we construct TriDimEEG, a standalone EEG decoder. Under strict cross-subject evaluation on eight datasets spanning clinical diagnosis, sleep staging, motor imagery, and emotion recognition, TriDimEEG achieves the best overall performance among fifteen evaluated models, with a 4.3% relative improvement in average accuracy over the second-best model. Replacing Transformer blocks in three EEG foundation models with TriDim blocks yields an average relative improvement of 7.4% in downstream accuracy while reducing parameter counts by 17.0% to 47.3%. These results establish TriDim as an effective and reusable building block and TriDimEEG as a strong standalone EEG decoder. Code and parameters of TriDimEEG are available at https://github.com/ncclab-sustech/TriDim_model.

</details>


### [125] [Expected Hypervolume Maximization for Multiobjective Optimization under Uncertainties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19858)
*Victor Trappler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Bayesian decision approach to multiobjective optimization under uncertainty using expected hypervolume maximization with gradient-based stochastic optimization and Gaussian Process surrogates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional multiobjective optimization under uncertainty often uses expectation of objectives, but the authors propose a Bayesian decision formulation using expected hypervolume as a better approach for handling uncertainties.

Method: Formulate the problem as Bayesian decision making, maximize expected hypervolume using gradient-based stochastic optimization with careful handling of dominated points, and employ Gaussian Processes as differentiable surrogate models when original code is not differentiable.

Result: The method enables gradient-based optimization of expected hypervolume, with additional active learning strategies through acquisition functions for constructing surrogate models tailored to multiobjective problems.

Conclusion: The proposed Bayesian approach with expected hypervolume maximization provides an effective framework for multiobjective optimization under uncertainty, complemented by specialized active learning strategies for surrogate model construction.

Abstract: The problem of multiobjective optimization under uncertainties is often approached by taking the expectation of each objective. In this work, we propose instead to formulate this as a Bayesian decision problem and to rely on the expected value of the hypervolume, which is to be maximized with respect to a finite set of input points. We show that this can be performed using methods based on gradients in a stochastic optimization framework, provided that care is taken with respect to dominated points. Moreover, in the absence of readily available differentiable code, we propose to use Gaussian Processes as differentiable surrogate models, in order to perform the optimization. An additional contribution in this work are some active learning strategies, through acquisition functions which helps construct a surrogate model well-designed for the multiobjective optimization problem at stake. These strategies are compared on simple analytical problems to assess their performances.

</details>


### [126] [Pretrained Medical Representations for the Practical Screening of Drug Repositioning Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19865)
*Yuhei Fujioka,Daitaro Misawa,Shingo Fukuma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new unified pre-training framework for medical code representation learning that integrates hierarchical structure, partial masking, and cross-reference mechanisms, enabling effective hypothesis discovery for drug repositioning through task-adaptive representation control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing BERT-based models fail to adequately capture hierarchical medical code structure and complex diagnosis-treatment interactions, limiting their utility for scientific hypothesis discovery from electronic health records.

Method: Proposed unified pre-training framework with hierarchical sub-token aggregation, partial masking, and cross-reference mechanisms. Includes Task-Adaptive Representation Approach for hypothesis prioritization to reduce prescription information over-encoding in diagnostic vectors.

Result: Model outperforms existing methods on pre-training objectives and downstream clinical tasks (dementia onset, hospitalization). Successfully rediscovered known promising drugs for Alzheimer's in a data-driven drug repositioning case study without external knowledge.

Conclusion: Domain-informed representation learning with task-adaptive representation control enables practical hypothesis discovery workflow for exploratory screening, though not providing causal evidence but identifying candidates for subsequent causal inference.

Abstract: Representation learning from medical code sequences in electronic health records and medical claims data has been successful in various clinical applications, such as those regarding disease prediction. However, significant challenges remain in extending this approach to the discovery of scientific hypotheses. One reason is that many existing BERT-based models fail to adequately capture the hierarchical structure of medical codes and the complex interactions between diagnoses and treatments. To address these limitations, we propose a new unified pre-training framework that explicitly integrates hierarchical sub-token aggregation, partial masking, and cross-reference mechanisms. The proposed model consistently outperformed existing methods on both pre-training objectives and downstream clinical event prediction tasks, including the onset of dementia and hospitalization. We also conducted an in silico drug repositioning case study targeting Alzheimer's disease. In the hypothesis generation step, our approach successfully rediscovered known promising drugs in a data-driven manner without relying on such external knowledge sources as the literature. Subsequently, in the hypothesis prioritization step, we introduced a Task-Adaptive Representation Approach to alleviate the over-encoding of historical prescription information within diagnostic vectors, enabling the robust prioritization of generated hypotheses. This study establishes an exploratory screening workflow for hypothesis generation and prioritization based on observational associations. Importantly, this framework is not intended to provide causal evidence, but rather to identify promising candidates for subsequent rigorous causal inference. Overall, this study demonstrates that domain-informed representation learning combined with task-adaptive representation control can enable a practical hypothesis discovery workflow.

</details>


### [127] [AURA: Adaptive Uncertainty-Routed Analysis for Email Threat Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19873)
*Omran Berjawi,Walid fahs,Rida Khatoun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AURA is a multimodal email threat detection system that combines URL classification with transformer-based semantic analysis, using uncertainty routing to efficiently handle ambiguous cases, achieving strong generalization across diverse datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
