Result: At S=12, simple period repetition beats six standard backbones on 4/7 benchmarks by 8-44% MSE improvement. Benefit boundary tracks period (correlation +0.71), not horizon (-0.23). Zero-shot pretraining trails by 22-50% on periodic benchmarks. Two statistics predict benefit sign at 0.76 accuracy.

Conclusion: The benefit of retrieval plug-ins belongs to the operating point regime (window-length-to-period relation), not to sophisticated retrieval mechanisms. The paper contributes a regime map, revealing protocol, and screening statistics, showing that consulting historical records matters more than the machinery on top.

Abstract: Retrieval plug-ins supply a deep forecaster with information its lookback window cannot carry. Published evaluations report consistent gains, and each credits its own mechanism. We show that the benefit belongs instead to the operating point: the relation between window length $S$ and dominant seasonal period $L$, an axis the standard protocol never varies. Stratifying the evaluation by that relation exposes the regime. At $S{=}12$, a simple control that repeats the last observed period beats the six standard backbones, in aggregate, on four of seven benchmarks by $8\%$ to $44\%$ of MSE. It beats the strongest plug-in we run on ETTm1 and matches it on ECL. It is worse by up to $25\%$ on the three datasets whose training-split spectra lack a concentrated, shared period. A controlled synthetic sweep of horizon, period, and window shows the benefit boundary tracks the period (correlation $+0.71$), not the horizon ($-0.23$). A paired control with no phase to recover nearly erases the effect, consistent with phase starvation. Zero-shot pretraining does not escape it: a foundation model trails trained backbones by $22\%$ to $50\%$ on the periodic benchmarks. Within our instrument, exact lookup matches graph diffusion: the payoff is consulting the record, not the machinery on top. Two interpretable statistics, a trend test and a staleness rate, predict the sign of the per-cell benefit at $0.76$ accuracy under leave-one-dataset-out evaluation, a suggestive margin over the $0.69$ majority rule, where a 22-feature stack manages $0.57$. We propose no new plug-in. The contribution is the regime map, the protocol that reveals it, and two statistics that screen it before deployment. Code: https://github.com/KurbanIntelligenceLab/retrieval-regime.

</details>


### [153] [SoftTri: Smooth Triangular Membership Functions for Adaptive Fuzzy Inference Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20194)
*Babak Sarani,Rahman Ardakanian,Ali Mousavi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposed SoftTri, a differentiable triangular membership function using soft-hinge mechanism, enabling smooth gradient-based optimization while maintaining triangular MF interpretability in neuro-fuzzy systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Triangular MFs are widely used in fuzzy systems due to interpretability and locality, but their nondifferentiability at knot points limits gradient-based optimization in adaptive neuro-fuzzy architectures, often requiring approximations or smoothing techniques.

Method: Developed SoftTri, a differentiable triangular MF constructed using smooth soft-hinge mechanism inspired by Swish-type activations. Preserves triangular MF geometry and locality while providing C∞ smoothness. Derived closed-form analytical gradients for fully differentiable backpropagation. Integrated into Takagi-Sugeno fuzzy neural network with grid-partitioned rules.

Result: Experimental evaluation on 1D/2D nonlinear approximation benchmarks and Airfoil Self-Noise dataset shows SoftTri improves optimization stability and approximation accuracy vs classical triangular MFs, achieving comparable or better performance than Gaussian MFs under identical settings.

Conclusion: SoftTri provides an effective compromise between interpretability and differentiable optimization in neuro-fuzzy systems, offering a smooth alternative to classical triangular MFs that enables stable gradient-based learning without sacrificing interpretability.

Abstract: Triangular membership functions (MFs) are widely used in fuzzy systems because of their interpretability, low parameterization complexity, and strong locality properties. However, their inherent nondifferentiability at knot points limits the effectiveness of gradient-based optimization in adaptive neuro-fuzzy architectures, often necessitating subgradient approximations or heuristic smoothing techniques. In this paper, we propose \emph{SoftTri}, a differentiable triangular membership function constructed using a smooth soft-hinge mechanism inspired by Swish-type activations. The proposed formulation preserves the geometric structure and localized behavior of classical triangular MFs while providing $C^\infty$ smoothness with respect to both the input variable and the membership parameters $(a,b,c)$ for any finite sharpness parameter $β>0$. Closed-form analytical gradients are derived to enable efficient and fully differentiable backpropagation-based learning. SoftTri is integrated into a Takagi--Sugeno fuzzy neural network with grid-partitioned rules and evaluated on multiple one-dimensional and two-dimensional nonlinear approximation benchmarks as well as a real-world regression task using the Airfoil Self-Noise dataset. Experimental results demonstrate that SoftTri consistently improves optimization stability and approximation accuracy compared with classical triangular membership functions, while achieving performance comparable to or better than Gaussian MFs under identical rule structures and training settings. The proposed approach provides an effective compromise between interpretability and differentiable optimization in modern neuro-fuzzy learning systems.

</details>


### [154] [Evaluating Financial Sentiment in the Age of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20198)
*Arslan Bisharat,Oudom Hean*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: General-purpose LLMs achieve financial sentiment classification comparable to finance-specific models, but better classification doesn't lead to stronger economic relationships with stock returns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread use of financial sentiment measures, it's unclear whether general-purpose LLMs improve on existing finance-specific methods. The paper aims to evaluate various sentiment models to determine their effectiveness in financial contexts.

Method: The study evaluates twelve sentiment models including dictionary-based methods, finance-specific transformers, and open-source LLMs. Evaluation uses two criteria: linguistic validity (classification accuracy) and economic validity (relationship with financial outcomes).

Result: General-purpose LLMs achieve classification performance comparable to finance-specific transformer models without task-specific fine-tuning. Several models produce sentiment measures significantly associated with earnings surprises, but none significantly associated with next-day stock returns. Performance is strongest for announcements with large earnings beats/misses and weaker for moderate surprises.

Conclusion: Financial sentiment measures capture information about firms' economic performance but have limited ability to explain short-run market reactions. Higher classification accuracy doesn't translate to stronger economic relationships with stock returns.

Abstract: Financial sentiment measures are widely used in empirical finance, but it remains unclear whether general-purpose large language models (LLMs) improve on existing finance-specific methods. This paper evaluates twelve sentiment models, including dictionary-based methods, finance-specific transformers, and open-source LLMs, using two criteria: linguistic validity and economic validity. We find that general-purpose LLMs achieve classification performance comparable to finance-specific transformer models without task-specific fine-tuning. However, higher classification accuracy does not translate into stronger economic relationships. Several models produce sentiment measures that are significantly associated with earnings surprises, but none is significantly associated with next-day stock returns. Model performance is strongest for announcements with large earnings beats or misses and substantially weaker for announcements with more moderate earnings surprises. These findings suggest that financial sentiment captures information about firms' economic performance but has limited ability to explain short-run market reactions

</details>


### [155] [Towards a Unified Modality-Agnostic Multimodal Framework for Cognitive Workload Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20199)
*Stefanos Gkikas,Christian Arzate Cruz,Calvin Joseph,Giorgos Giannakakis,Raul Fernandez Rojas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified hierarchical Transformer architecture for cognitive workload assessment using multiple biosignal modalities shows EEG as strongest single modality, but adding more modalities doesn't consistently improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While biosignals have been widely studied for cognitive workload measurement, research on combining heterogeneous biosignal modalities remains limited. The study aims to investigate how different modality combinations affect cognitive workload assessment performance.

Method: Developed a unified, modality-agnostic, hierarchical Transformer-based architecture that can process five different biosignal modalities (ECG, EDA, RESP, SpO2, EEG) within a single model. Evaluated all 31 possible modality combinations across three cognitive tasks using leave-one-subject-out validation.

Result: EEG ranked as the strongest single modality. Adding more modalities did not consistently improve performance. The full five-modality combination achieved highest Average scores (73.02% on IQ task, 68.08% averaged across all settings). The method reduced model size by ~50% compared to late-fusion alternatives while maintaining lower inference time.

Conclusion: The proposed unified Transformer architecture effectively handles heterogeneous biosignals for cognitive workload assessment, demonstrating that while EEG is the most informative single modality, careful selection of modality combinations is important as more modalities don't always improve performance.

Abstract: Cognitive workload reflects the mental effort required during task performance and is central to the design of adaptive human-machine systems. The use of biosignals to measure cognitive workload has been extensively researched and documented; however, studies examining the effects of combining heterogeneous biosignal modalities for this purpose remain limited. To provide insight into this area, we developed a unified, modality-agnostic, hierarchical Transformer-based architecture to process heterogeneous biosignal modalities within a single model. We use this framework in a pilot study evaluating all $31$ possible combinations of five modalities: Electrocardiogram (ECG), Electrodermal Activity (EDA), Respiration (RESP), Peripheral Oxygen Saturation (SpO$_2$), and Electroencephalogram (EEG), under leave-one-subject-out validation across three cognitively distinct tasks: abstract reasoning (IQ), arithmetic problem solving (MATH), and a game task (GAME). In this pilot setting, the results suggest that: (i) EEG is the strongest single modality, ranking highest in IQ, GAME, and the pooled ALL setting, where samples from all three tasks are combined; (ii) adding more modalities does not consistently improve performance; (iii) the full five-modality combination achieves the highest \textit{Average} score of $73.02%$ on IQ and $68.08%$ when the \textit{Average} scores are averaged over the four evaluation settings: IQ, MATH, GAME, and ALL; and (iv) the proposed method reduces model size by approximately $50%$ compared with late-fusion alternatives while maintaining a lower inference time.

</details>


### [156] [Scene-Conditioned Relation Routing for urban cellular activity forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20209)
*Qingzhong Li,Jingye Lin,Hui Ma,Yajun Zhang,Xinjun Pei,Ming Yan,Fei Xing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCRR-Net: A novel framework that uses urban contextual information to dynamically control spatial dependency selection and cross-task knowledge transfer for forecasting multiple cellular activities in cities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for urban cellular activity forecasting separate temporal modeling, spatial relation learning, and multi-signal prediction, relying on fixed graph structures or static multi-task learning schemes, which limits adaptability to changing urban scenes.

Method: SCRR-Net is a scene-conditioned spatial relation routing framework with four components: context encoder, spatial graph expert routing module, temporal Transformer encoder, and task knowledge routing module. Urban context jointly controls spatial dependency selection and cross-task knowledge transfer.

Result: Experiments on Milano and Trento datasets show SCRR-Net consistently outperforms competing methods on SMS, network traffic, and call activity forecasting, while providing interpretable routing behaviors.

Conclusion: SCRR-Net effectively addresses the limitations of existing approaches by dynamically adapting to urban scenes through context-conditioned routing, achieving superior performance across multiple cellular activity forecasting tasks with interpretability.

Abstract: Urban cellular activity forecasting requires jointly modeling heterogeneous spatiotemporal signals, including SMS usage, mobile network traffic, and call activity. Existing methods often separate temporal modeling, spatial relation learning, and multi-signal prediction, relying on fixed graph structures or static multi-task learning schemes, which limits their adaptability to changing urban scenes. We propose SCRR-Net, a scene-conditioned spatial relation routing framework in which urban contextual information jointly controls spatial dependency selection and cross-task knowledge transfer. SCRR-Net includes a context encoder, a spatial graph expert routing module, a temporal Transformer encoder, and a task knowledge routing module. Experiments on the Milano and Trento datasets demonstrate that SCRR-Net consistently outperforms competing methods on SMS, network traffic, and call activity forecasting, while providing interpretable routing behaviors.

</details>


### [157] [Subdomain-aware representation compression for pretrained image embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20213)
*Poowanut Niamluang,Jittat Fakcharoenphol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Applying PCA and LDA dimensionality reduction to subdomain representations improves space efficiency and accuracy for edge ML applications, with benefits for transfer learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional dimensionality reduction is applied uniformly to entire datasets, but the authors want to explore applying these techniques specifically to subdomain representations to achieve better compression for edge-device ML applications while potentially improving accuracy.

Method: Using standard dimensionality reduction techniques like Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) applied to subdomain representations in image domains, rather than uniformly across entire datasets.

Result: The approach not only achieves expected improvements in space and computation complexity (crucial for edge devices) but also surprisingly shows accuracy improvements over using full embeddings directly. The compressed representations also demonstrate transfer learning capabilities.

Conclusion: Dimensionality reduction applied specifically to subdomain representations is an effective strategy for edge ML, offering both efficiency gains and potential accuracy improvements, possibly because it better extracts subdomain-specific features. The approach enables practical transfer learning with compressed representations.

Abstract: Dimensionality reduction is a well-known technique for improving space efficiency, typically applied uniformly across an entire dataset. This paper investigates the possibilities of using dimensionality reduction techniques for subdomain representation compression. We explore standard techniques such as Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Linear discriminant analysis (LDA) in image domains. The results not only demonstrate the expected improvements in space and computation complexity crucial for edge-device ML applications but also show improvements in accuracy over direct full-embedding procedure. One possible explanation is that dimensionality reduction effectively extracts subdomain features. We also performed experiments to demonstrate transfer learning capabilities using the compressed representations.

</details>


### [158] [Explaining spatial information flow in short-term traffic forecasting models using a gated graph attention network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20217)
*Yue Li,Shujuan Chen,Ying Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A gated Graph Attention Network (GAT) for traffic forecasting with a novel gating mechanism that quantifies neighbor information sharing, revealing spatial patterns and architectural redundancies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GAT models for traffic forecasting claim to capture spatial dependencies through attention weights, but these claims can't be verified since attention weights can't be compared to measurable quantities. This leaves uncertainty about how to explain the models and which components are truly necessary.

Method: Proposes a gated GAT layer that learns, for each sensor and time step, what share of a sensor's updated state comes from neighbors versus itself. The gate is regularized to provide graded ablation, progressively withdrawing neighbor information. Applied to ST-MetaNet architecture, with experiments on one year of data from 498 loop detectors on England's road network.

Result: The gate shows heavier traffic sensors draw more neighbor information, following daily/weekly travel cycles. Mild regularization slightly improves accuracy, while stronger regularization degrades it. Both GAT layers in encoder and decoder are largely redundant - removing either alone maintains accuracy, but removing both substantially degrades performance.

Conclusion: The gated GAT provides modest accuracy gains, offers interpretable explanations of spatial information flow patterns (when/where neighbors matter), and reveals architectural insights about layer redundancy in traffic forecasting models.

Abstract: Short-term traffic forecasting supports real-time monitoring and control of road networks, and graph attention networks (GAT) are the standard means of representing spatial dependence in these models. GAT layers are widely described as capturing the influence of neighbouring locations, but this is seldom verified, because the attention weights offered in support cannot be compared against any measured quantity. That leaves two questions open, how the model should be explained and which of its components are necessary. We address this by adding a gate to the GAT layer which learns, at every sensor and every time step, what share of a sensor's updated state is drawn from its neighbours rather than from itself. Regularising the gate withdraws neighbour information progressively and thereby provides a graded form of ablation. We apply the gated GAT to ST-MetaNet, whose encoder and decoder each place one GAT layer between two recurrent layers, and train it on one calendar year of records from 498 loop detectors on the strategic road network of England. The gate assigns a larger share of neighbour information to sensors carrying heavier traffic and follows the daily and weekly cycle of travel, consistent with adjacent locations being more strongly coupled when busy. Mild regularisation improves accuracy slightly, and accuracy declines at higher strengths as the penalty withdraws information the model needs. The encoder gate closes before the decoder gate, but direct ablation qualifies that ordering. Removing either GAT layer alone leaves accuracy at least as good as keeping both, whereas removing both degrades it substantially, so the two layers are largely redundant rather than either being indispensable. The gated GAT therefore yields a modest accuracy gain, an explanation of where and when spatial information flows, and evidence on which layers the architecture requires.

</details>


### [159] [Is It Still Worth Training a Classical Model in the Era of LLMs? A Crossover Benchmark on Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20218)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For business prediction tasks, training a classical model beats using frozen small LLMs with minimal labeled data (median crossover at ~6% of training set). Few-shot prompting doesn't follow learning curves, and feature-name semantics affect LLM performance. Recommendation: collect few hundred labels and train gradient-boosted models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can now label tabular data without training (available in tools like Microsoft Copilot and Claude for Excel), creating a practical question for businesses: should they use frozen LLMs or collect data to train classical models, and how much data is needed?

Method: Quantified the labeled-data crossover N* where trained classical models overtake frozen LLMs. Conducted 126 independent student evaluations of small GPT models across 8 prompting configurations and 18 tabular datasets, paired with power-law learning curves for 6 classical model families. Also tested in-context few-shot examples and controlled feature-name semantics probes.

Result: Training wins quickly: even with optimal prompting, trained classical models beat small frozen LLMs with available data in 86% of cases, and win with minimal labeled subsets in 40% of cases. Median crossover occurs at ~6% of training set. Few-shot examples don't follow power laws (unlike training), and performance varies across implementers (coefficient of variation: 0.148). LLMs depend on feature-name semantics.

Conclusion: For typical business tabular prediction problems, the evidence strongly favors collecting a few hundred labels and training gradient-boosted models rather than relying on frozen LLMs, even small ones.

Abstract: Large language models can label a tabular row from a plain-English description with no training - a capability now shipping in mainstream spreadsheet tools such as Microsoft Copilot in Excel and Anthropic's Claude for Excel - raising a practical question for the many business prediction problems where labels are expensive: should you prompt a frozen LLM, or collect data and train a model - and if so, how much data? We quantify the answer with the labeled-data crossover N*, the training-set size at which a trained classical model's learning curve overtakes a frozen LLM's training-free (and therefore flat) error. Aggregating 126 independent student evaluations of small GPT models under eight prompting configurations across 18 tabular datasets, paired with authoritative power-law learning curves for six classical model families, we find that training wins fast: even given an oracle choice of its best prompt configuration, a trained classical model beats the small frozen LLM using no more labeled data than is already on hand in 86% of cases, and wins by the smallest labeled subset we evaluate in 40%, with the observed crossover at a median of ~6% of the training set. In-context few-shot examples do not behave like training - error versus shot count does not follow a power law - and the same protocol re-run by independent implementers varies with a coefficient of variation of 0.148. A controlled probe indicates the LLM depends on recognizable feature-name semantics, which plausibly makes our crossover a conservative estimate (we do not claim memorization). For a typical business table, the evidence is clear: collect a few hundred labels and train a gradient-boosted model.

</details>


### [160] [Accuracy Is Not Enough: A Cross-Architecture Audit of Demographic Bias in Deep Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20249)
*Dang Quang Minh,Nguyen Dung Son,Nguyen Huu Loi,Truong Viet Vu,Nguyen Thai Anh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep knowledge tracing models show significant demographic bias that varies by context, with the most accurate models being the most biased, and standard debiasing techniques proving ineffective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a lack of comprehensive fairness audits for deep knowledge tracing models that power modern adaptive learning systems, despite evidence of demographic bias in simpler Bayesian knowledge tracing models.

Method: Evaluated four deep knowledge tracing architectures (DKT, DKVMN, SAKT, AKT) trained under three regimes (standard, reweighting, adversarial) on two datasets with demographic metadata (Eedi and OULAD). Used ABROCA, student-level bootstrap confidence intervals, and permutation tests to address fairness-metric instability concerns.

Result: 1) Significant socioeconomic bias on Eedi across all architectures; gender bias significant on OULAD but negligible on Eedi. 2) AKT (most accurate model) showed largest socioeconomic bias, with Rasch embeddings responsible for both accuracy gains and excess bias. 3) Standard debiasing methods (reweighting, adversarial) failed to reduce bias while preserving accuracy.

Conclusion: Deep knowledge tracing models exhibit context-dependent demographic bias, the most accurate architectures are the most biased, and current mitigation techniques are unreliable - highlighting the need for better fairness-aware methods in adaptive learning systems.

Abstract: Deep knowledge tracing (DKT) models implicitly decide which students an adaptive system believes have mastered a skill, yet almost all evidence on their demographic fairness comes from Bayesian knowledge tracing; the deep models that power modern systems have received no comparable cross-architecture audit. We close this gap: four architectures (DKT, DKVMN, SAKT, AKT) trained under three regimes (standard, reweighting, adversarial) on two public datasets with demographic metadata, Eedi (15.9M interactions) and OULAD (167k after preprocessing), evaluated with ABROCA, student-level bootstrap confidence intervals, and permutation tests addressing recent critiques of fairness-metric instability. Three findings emerge. (i) Bias is real but context-dependent: every architecture shows a significant socioeconomic ABROCA on Eedi (0.018-0.023, $p<0.005$), with per-group AUC lower for economically disadvantaged students, while gender bias is significant on OULAD for three of four architectures after multiplicity correction yet negligible on Eedi. (ii) The most accurate architecture is the most biased: AKT gains about 4 AUC points from item-level Rasch embeddings and shows the largest socioeconomic ABROCA, exceeding every other architecture under a paired bootstrap ($p\leq0.002$); ablating only the Rasch embeddings removes the accuracy gain and the excess bias together. (iii) Standard mitigation is unreliable: reweighting and adversarial debiasing leave ABROCA essentially unchanged in every configuration that preserves accuracy, even though the adversary is pinned at chance at full reversal strength and a weak-strength positive control rules out a dead probe.

</details>


### [161] [How Far Can Sub-3B Open Language Models Go in Zero-Shot Essay Scoring on an 8 GB Consumer GPU?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20250)
*Nguyen Dung Son,Dang Quang Minh,Nguyen Huu Loi,Truong Viet Vu,Nguyen Thai Anh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Small open-source LLMs (<3B) can run locally for private essay scoring but fall short of human-level accuracy, with rubric decomposition improving results and careful calibration needed for trait aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many educational settings (like public schools) need automated essay scoring but cannot use third-party APIs due to privacy concerns. There's a need to understand how much scoring capability survives when using small (<3B), locally runnable open models instead of large proprietary ones.

Method: Controlled study of four instruction-tuned open models (Qwen2.5: 0.5B/1.5B/3B, SmolLM2: 1.7B) on eight ASAP-AES prompts, running fully locally on an 8GB consumer GPU. Used bootstrap confidence intervals, Holm-corrected paired tests, and deployment-realistic design choices. Tested holistic vs rubric-decomposed prompting and different aggregation methods (batch min-max, mean).

Result: 1) Rubric-decomposition beats holistic prompting for all models with batch min-max aggregation. 2) Trait score aggregation is fragile: naive mean collapses for miscalibrated models, but Multi-Trait Specialization with min-max normalization improves macro QWK from 0.204 to 0.388. 3) Signed error decreases with essay length (opposite of large LLM verbosity bias), with normalized rubric decomposition flattening this slope for well-calibrated models.

Conclusion: Best local configuration (0.388 QWK) remains far below human inter-rater agreement (0.769) and length-only baseline (0.523). Sub-3B local models should be positioned strictly for formative, human-supervised feedback rather than high-stakes automated scoring.

Abstract: Zero-shot essay scoring with large language models is usually demonstrated with proprietary API models, yet the settings where automated scoring is most needed, such as public schools grading thousands of essays under strict privacy rules, are often those where sending student writing to a third-party API is unacceptable. We ask how much capability survives when the model must be a sub-3B open model running fully locally in FP16, with a controlled study of four instruction-tuned models from two families (Qwen2.5 at 0.5B/1.5B/3B, SmolLM2 at 1.7B) on all eight ASAP-AES prompts on a single 8 GB consumer GPU, with bootstrap confidence intervals, Holm-corrected paired tests, and deployment-realistic variants of the key design choices. Three findings emerge. (i) Rubric-decomposed prompting beats holistic prompting for every model under batch min-max aggregation (though Qwen2.5-3B drops significantly on one prompt), and under mean aggregation two unrelated families land within 0.01 at the 1.5-1.7B scale. (ii) Mapping trait scores into the prompt range is fragile to grader calibration: one model compresses traits into a narrow low band (2-4 on 0-10) and naive mean aggregation collapses, while the min-max normalization of Multi-Trait Specialization repairs it (macro QWK 0.204 to 0.388) and stays within 0.03 when its statistics are frozen on 30 held-out essays. (iii) Signed error falls with essay length in eleven of twelve configurations, opposite to the verbosity bias reported for large LLM judges; normalized rubric decomposition largely flattens this slope for well-calibrated models. We anchor results honestly: the best local configuration (0.388) remains far below both the human inter-rater ceiling (0.769) and a length-only baseline (0.523), so we position sub-3B local models strictly for formative, human-supervised feedback.

</details>


### [162] [Improving Online Reinforcement Learning via Bidirectional Behavior Prior Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20268)
*Gong Gao,Xiao Lai,Jiaji Shen,Ning Jia,Xianhui Liu,Weidong Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: B2PD algorithm improves online RL sample efficiency by generating high-value behavior priors through action-value guided CVAE and bidirectional knowledge distillation, reducing critic-induced errors and enabling stable policy optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online RL suffers from poor sample efficiency and unstable learning due to critic estimation errors amplified by greedy policy updates. Existing behavior-prior methods rely on limited-quality offline datasets that fail to provide effective guidance, resulting in low sample efficiency and suboptimal performance.

Method: Proposes Bidirectional Behavior Prior Distillation (B2PD) algorithm that: (1) leverages action-value priors to guide a conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) to generate high-value behavior support sets, (2) distills resulting expert behavior priors into the agent, establishing bidirectional knowledge flow that reduces inefficient exploration and enables stable policy optimization.

Result: Empirical evaluations on both state- and pixel-based tasks show B2PD substantially improves sample efficiency while maintaining stable policy optimization. The approach effectively mitigates critic-induced error amplification and enables structured behavior priors to guide policy updates in a principled manner.

Conclusion: Enforcing high-quality behavioral support during online learning effectively addresses critic-induced error amplification, demonstrating that structured behavior priors can guide policy updates in a principled and sample-efficient manner, moving beyond limitations of conventional behavior prior approaches.

Abstract: Online reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms frequently exhibit poor sample efficiency and unstable learning dynamics, stemming from systematic critic estimation errors that are exacerbated by greedy policy updates. Existing behavior-prior reinforcement learning methods attempt to alleviate this issue by relying on offline pre-training to learn behavior models from fixed datasets and using policy priors to constrain online policy updates. However, the limited quality of offline datasets often hinders the ability to provide high-value policies that can effectively guide policy updates. The absence of expert trajectories significantly impairs online policy learning, leading to low sample efficiency and suboptimal performance. To address these challenges, we depart from conventional behavior prior approaches and propose a Bidirectional Behavior Prior Distillation (B2PD) algorithm. B2PD leverages action-value priors to guide a conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) in generating a high-value behavior support set. The resulting expert behavior priors are further distilled into the agent, effectively reducing inefficient exploration and enabling stable policy optimization, while establishing a bidirectional knowledge flow mechanism. Empirical evaluations on both state- and pixel-based tasks verify that B2PD substantially improves sample efficiency while maintaining stable policy optimization. More broadly, this work shows that enforcing high-quality behavioral support during online learning effectively mitigates critic-induced error amplification, enabling structured behavior priors to guide policy updates in a principled and sample-efficient manner.

</details>


### [163] [Placement Is Free, Composition Is Not: The Latin Square as a Provably-Balanced Construction for Heterogeneous Sequence-Mixer Stacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20269)
*Taebong Kim,Youngsik Hong,Minsik Kim,Sunyoung Choi,Jaewon Jang,Minseo Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A balanced Latin square design of multiple attention mechanisms across layers shows heterogeneous composition matters more than exact placement, with homogeneous architectures incurring significant performance penalties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Transformer designs conventionally repeat the same attention mechanism at every layer without systematic testing. The researchers wanted to investigate whether performance improvements come from the choice of mechanisms, their placement in the architecture, or both, but causal attribution is difficult with conventional designs.

Method: Introduced Aether-7B-5Attn, a mixture-of-experts model with 49 layers containing seven sequence-mixing mechanisms arranged in a 7x7 Latin square design. Used parameter-matched proxy experiments with 4 mechanisms in 4x4 Latin square over 16 layers, trained with multiple seeds. Compared distributed heterogeneous stacks, balanced periodic cycles, clustered contiguous bands, and homogeneous stacks.

Result: Rearranging mechanisms in balanced cycles changed validation loss by only 0.16%, showing placement has little effect. Clustering mechanisms into contiguous bands incurred 0.59% penalty, while homogeneous stacks incurred 1.68% penalty (increasing to 2.63% at larger scale). Removing SSM-family mechanisms caused 3.20% degradation. Performance depends primarily on heterogeneous composition distributed across depth.

Conclusion: Transformer performance benefits primarily from heterogeneous composition of different sequence-mixing mechanisms distributed across depth layers, not from specific permutations or exact placements. The Latin square design provides a principled way to study architectural choices while eliminating placement confounds.

Abstract: Since GPT, most Transformers have repeated the same attention mechanism at every layer. Yet this design is largely a convention rather than a tested conclusion. When multiple sequence mixers are combined in one stack, improvements may arise from mechanism choice, placement, or both, making causal attribution difficult. We introduce Aether-7B-5Attn, a 6.59B-parameter mixture-of-experts model ($\approx$2.98B active) whose 49 layers contain seven sequence-mixing mechanisms arranged as a $7\times7$ Latin square. Because each mechanism appears exactly once in every row and column, the design guarantees balanced exposure across depth while eliminating placement confounds. To evaluate this principle, we build a parameter-matched proxy with four mechanisms arranged as a $4\times4$ Latin square over sixteen layers, matched to 700.9M parameters and trained with eight seeds per arm. The results reveal a clear dissociation. Rearranging a distributed heterogeneous stack into a balanced periodic cycle changes validation loss by only 0.16\%, indicating that exact placement has little effect. In contrast, clustering the same mechanisms into contiguous depth bands incurs a 0.59\% penalty, while replacing the heterogeneous stack with a homogeneous one incurs a 1.68\% penalty. These results indicate that performance depends primarily on heterogeneous composition distributed across depth rather than on any particular permutation. We confirm this finding at 2.16$\times$ larger scale (1.514B parameters), where the homogeneous-stack penalty increases to 2.63\% and removing the SSM-family mechanism produces a 3.20\% degradation. We further report per-mechanism cost profiles, English and Korean evaluations, and a causal-safety audit of all 49 layers. We release model weights, training recipes, training code, logs, and architecture source code.

</details>


### [164] [Labeled Incidence Structures for Native Transformer Modeling of Text, Knowledge Graphs, and Hypergraphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20278)
*Mahesh Godavarti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LIS (labeled incidence structures) is a uniform representation for diverse data types that preserves structural roles and relations, enabling a single transformer to natively process text, knowledge graphs, and hypergraphs without flattening, while providing unique structural addresses and avoiding storage-order biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches flatten structured data like text, knowledge graphs, and hypergraphs into token sequences, losing important structural information about distinct roles that elements play within relation instances. There's a need for a representation that preserves this structural information while enabling unified processing across different data types.

Method: Introduces LIS (labeled incidence structures) representing each endpoint as (x_d, s, e): content x_d, role/slot s, and relation instance e. Uses structural addresses A(s,e) = R_s R_e composed from slot and instance operators. Enables relative transport P_{j→i} = A_i^{-1} A_j for attention. Also analyzes persistent knowledge repositories and storage-order issues.

Result: Shows that LIS enables a single standard transformer to process multiple data types natively without flattening. Characterizes conditions for unique, path-independent addresses. Proves that additive encodings miss joint information between slots and relation instances. Demonstrates that content-based relation-instance operators avoid storage-order issues and yield capacity bounds independent of repository size M.

Conclusion: LIS provides a principled, unified framework for representing structured data that preserves essential role and relation information. The approach addresses fundamental limitations of flattening and additive encodings, while solving practical issues in persistent knowledge repositories related to storage-order biases and capacity control.

Abstract: Text, knowledge graphs, and hypergraphs all have elements that play distinct roles within relation instances, structure that is lost when data is flattened into token sequences. We introduce labeled incidence structures (LIS), a uniform representation that encodes each endpoint as $(x_d, s, e)$: content $x_d$, a role or slot $s$, and the relation instance $e$ in which that role appears. Because every data type maps to the same $(x_d, s, e)$ representation without flattening, a single standard transformer can process them all natively, structural differences are carried entirely by the operators, not the architecture.
  LIS assigns a structural address to each endpoint by composing a slot operator and an instance operator, $A(s,e) = R_s R_e$. We characterize when this factorization gives every token a unique, path-independent address. When it does, the natural operator comparing endpoint $j$ to endpoint $i$ is the relative transport $P_{j\to i} = A_i^{-1} A_j$, which gives attention a role- and relation-aware inductive bias without imposing an arbitrary sequence order.
  Additive encodings of the form "position term plus relation term" can miss information that depends jointly on $s$ and $e$. We prove this in a controlled example family: when the journey operator is approximated by the sum of a position-only term and a relation-only term, the approximation cannot capture how position and relation combine, only their separate effects.
  We also analyze persistent knowledge repositories. Identifiers tied to storage locations make models sensitive to storage order, while freely learned identifiers can become harder to control as the repository size $M$ grows relative to the sample size $n$. Computing relation-instance operators from content avoids this storage-order issue and yields a capacity bound independent of $M$, under fixed architectural and Lipschitz assumptions.

</details>


### [165] [Personalising a Cross-User Surface Electromyography Encoder Under a Small Calibration Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20296)
*Jethro Odeyemi,W. J. Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that full fine-tuning consistently outperforms other adaptation methods for myoelectric interfaces, but prototypical adaptation (a gradient-free method) achieves 52-78% of the benefit without needing optimizers or per-user weight copies, enabling on-device personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While myoelectric interfaces require calibration, previous work hasn't systematically examined what to do with collected calibration repetitions. The paper treats personalization of cross-user encoders as a design decision with costs, aiming to find optimal ways to use limited calibration data.

Method: Tested four alternative approaches using the same labeled repetitions: prototypical adaptation, linear probes, scaled fine-tuning, and full fine-tuning. Evaluated each method at every budget level up to maximum allowed (5 repetitions on DB1, 4 on DB2 and DB5) across 77 subjects from three databases.

Result: Full fine-tuning is the most accurate at every budget level, with no exceptions among subject subsets. However, the key practical finding is that gradient-free prototypical adaptation recovers 52-78% of full fine-tuning's benefit without optimizers or per-user weight copies, enabling wearable device personalization at donning time.

Conclusion: The common intuition that a good representation only needs a fresh classifier is incorrect for myoelectric interfaces. Full fine-tuning works best, but prototypical adaptation provides a practical compromise for on-device personalization. Performance relative to clinic-fitted per-user classifiers depends on the specific database.

Abstract: A myoelectric interface needs calibration from the user before it will function. Earlier work has treated calibration as a quantity, but has not asked the question of what a device should do with the calibration repetitions once they have been collected. This paper views personalizing the cross-user encoder as a design decision with a cost. Four alternative approaches to using exactly the same labeled repetitions were tested from a single cross-user encoder per held-out subject. Prototypical adaptation, linear probes, scaled fine-tuning and full fine-tuning were tested at every budget up to the maximum each database allows, five repetitions on DB1 and four on DB2 and DB5. Comparing four ways to spend a small calibration budget across 77 subjects, full fine-tuning is the most accurate at every budget, consistently enough that there is no exception among subsets of subjects. The result which impacts how one might make an engineering decision however is that a gradient free prototypical rule recovers 52 to 78 per cent of its benefit with no optimiser and no per-user copy of the weights, which makes personalisation something a worn device can do at donning time. The widespread intuition that a good representation only needs a fresh classifier is incorrect here. How well each method may perform relative to a per-user classifier that would be fitted by a clinic will depend on the specific database.

</details>


### [166] [Intact-to-Amputee Transfer in Surface-EMG Gesture Decoding: Training Source and Calibration Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20297)
*Jethro Odeyemi,W. J. Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A montage-agnostic cross-user EMG encoder shows promising transfer learning from intact limbs to amputees, outperforming per-user classifiers but requires some labeled calibration data from new users.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current EMG pattern recognition systems require extensive user-specific calibration and perform poorly in zero-shot transfer between users, especially for amputee populations who are underrepresented in research (only ~1 in 6 studies).

Method: Developed a montage-agnostic cross-user encoder trained on intact-limb EMG data, tested on 11 transradial amputees using a matched protocol. Compared zero-shot transfer, transfer after minimal calibration (1-3 repetitions), and different training data combinations (intact subjects vs amputees).

Result: Zero-shot cross-population transfer failed completely. However, with just 3 labeled repetitions from new users, the encoder achieved 0.779 macro-F1 vs 0.589 for per-user classifiers (19% improvement). Training on 40 intact subjects produced better transfer than training on 10 amputees. Combining both data sources yielded the best results. The pre-registered prediction about amputee EMG separability only held after calibration and data enrichment.

Conclusion: While zero-shot transfer remains challenging, minimal calibration (3 repetitions) enables effective cross-user EMG decoding. Combining intact-limb and amputee training data optimizes performance. The study identifies boundaries of current theoretical accounts about EMG separability differences between populations.

Abstract: A recogniser trained on one person rarely transfers to the next, and useful performance usually demands a fresh round of labelled calibration from the end user. A systematic review of 1077 studies quantifies where the evidence is thin: amputees appear in about one in six. Here a montage-agnostic cross-user encoder is carried to eleven transradial amputees on a protocol matched to its intact-limb training data. Zero-shot cross-population transfer fails outright: the encoder requires labeled data from the new user before it begins decoding, and it then exceeds the per-user classifier a clinic would fit by 0.190 macro F1 at three repetitions and for every subject in the cohort. Given three labelled repetitions it reaches 0.779 macro-F1 against 0.589 for the per-user pipeline. Training on forty intact subjects produces better transfers to a new amputee than training on ten other amputees, and combining the two produces better transfers than either individually. The prediction pre-registered for this study, which extends the encoder's baseline-strength account with the premise that amputee EMG is less separable, holds true only after a few repetitions become available and after enriching the source pool with additional amputees. At a single repetition, and at every budget under a source matched to the intact-limb comparison, it fails. Thus, it locates the boundary of the proposed account.

</details>


### [167] [A Learning Algorithm for Threshold Boolean Networks with Prescribed Fixed Points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20298)
*Gonzalo A. Ruz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learning algorithm for threshold Boolean networks that enforces fixed point constraints, penalizes spurious attractors, encourages binary outputs, and promotes sparsity via L1 regularization, demonstrating superior performance over standard methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop a method for inferring threshold Boolean networks that accurately captures prescribed fixed points while avoiding spurious attractors. Standard methods like Perceptron and Logistic Regression can recover desired fixed points but often introduce many unwanted spurious attractors, which distort the network dynamics.

Method: The method uses a custom differentiable loss function with multiple components: (1) fixed point preservation, (2) penalty for spurious attractors, (3) encouragement of binary outputs, and (4) L1 regularization for sparsity. It employs a learning algorithm optimized for threshold Boolean networks with prescribed dynamical constraints.

Result: Applied to Arabidopsis thaliana's FOS-GRN model, the method achieved perfect reconstruction (all 10 desired fixed points, no spurious ones) in 5 out of 30 runs, with average recovery of 8.53 ± 0.90 correct fixed points and no spurious attractors. Standard methods recovered up to 10 fixed points but introduced 8-31 spurious ones. Performance remained robust across practical regularization strengths (λ up to 0.01).

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm effectively captures meaningful network dynamics under prescribed constraints, demonstrating superior stability and accuracy compared to standard methods by successfully balancing fixed point preservation, spurious attractor avoidance, and network sparsity.

Abstract: We present a learning algorithm for inferring threshold Boolean networks (TBNs) with a prescribed set of fixed points. The proposed method employs a custom differentiable loss function that jointly enforces fixed point preservation, penalizes spurious attractors, encourages binary outputs, and promotes sparsity through L1 regularization. Applied to the FOS-GRN model of Arabidopsis thaliana, the approach achieved perfect reconstruction (i.e., all 10 desired fixed points and no spurious ones) in 5 out of 30 independent runs, recovering on average 8.53 $\pm$ 0.90 correct fixed points with no spurious attractors. In contrast, standard methods such as the Perceptron and Logistic Regression recovered up to 10 fixed points but introduced between 8 and 31 spurious ones. An additional analysis varying the sparsity coefficient ($λ$) confirmed that the method's performance and the structural properties of the inferred networks remain robust within a practical range (up to 0.01) of regularization strengths. Overall, the results demonstrate the effectiveness and stability of the proposed algorithm in capturing meaningful network dynamics under prescribed dynamical constraints.

</details>


### [168] [Improving Generalization and Robustness in Offline Reinforcement Learning via Boundary-Aware Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20300)
*Gong Gao,Weidong Zhao,Xianhui Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BADA proposes boundary-aware data augmentation for offline RL to improve in-distribution generalization by generating synthetic data that preserves original distribution characteristics, outperforming existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current offline RL algorithms suffer from poor in-distribution generalization and robustness when deployed to real environments. Existing regularization techniques from computer vision are insufficient because low-level physical signals are highly sensitive to distributional shifts.

Method: The method theoretically analyzes error bounds of behavior policy and action-value function with random episode interpolation, finding error scales with state distance. BADA uses neighboring states to construct interpolation boundaries, generating synthetic data that better preserves the original data distribution.

Result: Qualitative studies in toy environments show BADA preserves policy smoothness and accurately reconstructs multimodal value distributions. Extensive experiments on limited offline datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across diverse benchmarks.

Conclusion: BADA effectively addresses the in-distribution generalization problem in offline RL by generating high-quality synthetic data through boundary-aware interpolation, achieving superior performance compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Current offline reinforcement learning (ORL) algorithms tend to overfit the training dataset and exhibit poor in-distribution generalization and robustness performance when deployed to real environments, thus compromising their effectiveness. Existing methods typically enhance in-distribution generalization and robustness by leveraging regularization techniques widely used in computer vision. However, due to the high sensitivity of low-level physical signals to distributional shifts, these methods still suffer from notable limitations in in-distribution generalization and robustness, making it difficult to achieve stable performance in complex environments. To address this issue, we theoretically analyze the error bounds of the behavior policy and action-value function trained with random episode interpolation, revealing that the error scales positively correlated with the distance between states. Based on this insight, we propose a method called $\bf{B}$oundary-$\bf{A}$ware $\bf{D}$ata $\bf{A}$ugmentation (BADA), which leverages neighboring states to construct interpolation boundaries, enabling the generation of synthetic data that more faithfully preserves the original data distribution. We first conduct qualitative studies in a toy environment, showing that BADA generates mixed samples that preserve desirable policy smoothness while accurately reconstructing multimodal value distributions. Extensive experiments on limited offline datasets further demonstrate that BADA attains state-of-the-art performance across diverse benchmarks.

</details>


### [169] [SAGG: Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating for Robust Multimodal Learning under Heterogeneous Corruption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20302)
*Wentao Zhang,Yifan Zhu,Yutong Zhang,Wentao Mo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGG (Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating) addresses heterogeneous modality corruption in multimodal learning by making per-sample binary decisions to retain or discard corrupted modalities, outperforming existing batch-level methods especially in high-corruption regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal gradient balancing methods assume uniform corruption across training batches, but in practice corruption is sample-heterogeneous - different samples within the same mini-batch may have different modalities corrupted. Batch-level methods with shared modulation parameters incur irreducible bias under this heterogeneous corruption model.

Method: Proposes Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating (SAGG), which makes binary retain-or-discard decisions per sample via an online feature-norm quality test. Includes a truncation mechanism for variance control. Analyzed within a distribution-free estimator class, proving that sample-level all-or-nothing gating is the unique unbiased strategy.

Result: Theoretical: Proves SAGG-based SGD converges at O(1/√T) rate to stationary points of clean loss without corruption-dependent error floor. Derives certified robustness radius for independent-encoder architecture connecting per-modality Lipschitz constants to classification margin. Experimental: SAGG consistently outperforms ten existing methods on Kinetics-Sounds and UCF-101 datasets under Gaussian noise injection, partial modality missing, and natural contribution imbalance, with largest gains in high-corruption regimes.

Conclusion: Sample-adaptive gradient gating is essential for handling heterogeneous modality corruption, with SAGG providing both theoretical guarantees and empirical superiority over batch-level methods, especially when corruption is severe and varies across samples within a batch.

Abstract: Multimodal gradient balancing methods modulate encoder gradients with a shared scalar per modality, implicitly assuming that corruption is uniform across the training batch. In practice, corruption is sample-heterogeneous: within a single mini-batch, different samples may have different modalities corrupted. We prove that under this heterogeneous corruption model, any batch-level sample-agnostic linear estimator with a shared modulation parameter incurs an irreducible bias with respect to the clean-data gradient, and that sample-level all-or-nothing gating is the unique unbiased strategy within a natural distribution-free estimator class. Motivated by this result, we propose Sample-Adaptive Gradient Gating (SAGG), which makes a binary retain-or-discard decision per sample via an online feature-norm quality test and incorporates a truncation mechanism for variance control. We prove that SAGG-based SGD converges at the standard O(1/sqrt(T)) rate to stationary points of the clean loss without a corruption-dependent error floor, and derive a certified robustness radius for the independent-encoder architecture that connects per-modality Lipschitz constants to the classification margin. Experiments on Kinetics-Sounds and UCF-101 under Gaussian noise injection, partial modality missing, and natural contribution imbalance show that SAGG consistently outperforms ten existing methods, with the largest gains in high-corruption regimes where batch-level bias is most severe.

</details>


### [170] [Hypernetwork-Parameterized Spatially Adaptive Neural Operators for PDE Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20309)
*Jiaquan Zhang,Chaoning Zhang,Shuxu Chen,Meng Ye,Xiaofeng Zhang,Qiang He,Weifeng Huang,Guoqing Wang,Yang Yang,Caiyan Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SANNO introduces a spatially adaptive neural operator that generates location-dependent operator parameters across the domain, outperforming existing methods on various PDE benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural operators have spatially shared update rules that underfit boundaries and high-gradient regions, with localized errors accumulating during autoregressive rollout. Current hypernetwork methods only generate global parameterizations per PDE instance, failing to capture spatial variations effectively.

Method: SANO uses Fourier-encoded coordinates and a coordinate-conditioned hypernetwork to generate spatial operator-conditioning codes at sampling points. A Hyper-Neural Element (HNE) mechanism interpolates these codes within local subregions, coupling neighboring operators while allowing their update rules to vary across space. Partition-of-unity weights assemble overlapping local predictions.

Result: Experiments on one-, two-, and three-dimensional PDEs and two perforated-domain elliptic benchmarks show SANO consistently outperforms competitive neural-operator, hypernetwork-based, and physics-informed baselines.

Conclusion: SANNO successfully addresses the limitations of spatially shared parameterizations by introducing spatially continuous operator parameters, demonstrating superior performance on spatially heterogeneous PDE problems through localized adaptation.

Abstract: Spatially heterogeneous partial differential equations (PDEs) exhibit location-dependent dynamics arising from variations in geometry and physical coefficients. Existing neural operators improve localized modeling through multiscale features, attention mechanisms, or domain decomposition, yet their update rules often remain spatially shared. Hypernetwork-based methods adapt parameters across PDE instances but typically generate only one global parameterization per instance. Consequently, shared operators may underfit boundaries and high-gradient regions, with these localized errors accumulating during autoregressive rollout. We propose a spatially adaptive neural operator (SANO), which replaces this spatially shared parameterization with a spatially continuous field of location-dependent operator parameters. SANO uses Fourier-encoded coordinates and a coordinate-conditioned hypernetwork to generate spatial operator-conditioning codes at sampling points. A Hyper-Neural Element (HNE) mechanism interpolates these codes within local subregions, coupling neighboring operators while allowing their update rules to vary across space, and partition-of-unity weights assemble the overlapping local predictions. Experiments on one-, two-, and three-dimensional PDEs and two perforated-domain elliptic benchmarks show that SANO consistently outperforms competitive neural-operator, hypernetwork-based, and physics-informed baselines.

</details>


### [171] [ZeroHAT: Behavior-Conditioned Zero-Shot Human Activity Trace Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20310)
*Rongchao Xu,Dahai Yu,Lin Jiang,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ZeroHAT: A zero-shot synthetic human activity trace generation framework that transfers behavioral patterns from source regions to target regions using publicly available contextual information, without needing real HAT data from the target region.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accessing large-scale Human Activity Traces (HATs) is challenging due to high collection costs and privacy concerns. Existing synthetic HAT generation methods rely on real data from the same region, which is infeasible for regions where real HATs are unavailable.

Method: ZeroHAT uses a behavior-conditioned framework with three novel components: 1) multidimensional consistency-aware intent extractor, 2) cross-region behavioral cloning module, and 3) behavior-conditioned activity realization module. It transfers patterns from source regions and adapts them using publicly available contextual information about target regions.

Result: On a ten-city benchmark, ZeroHAT achieves 4.5-6.4x the normalized downstream utility of the strongest baseline and improves average fidelity by 15.6%-40.8% across target regions.

Conclusion: ZeroHAT successfully enables zero-shot synthetic HAT generation by transferring learned behavioral patterns and adapting them with contextual information, addressing the data scarcity problem in regions without real HAT data.

Abstract: Human activity traces record individuals' timestamped visits to points of interest and are essential for applications such as mobility prediction and urban simulation. However, accessing large-scale HATs is challenging due to high collection costs and privacy concerns. Synthetic HAT generation offers a promising way to make such data available and has attracted growing interest from both industry and academia. Although many efforts have been devoted to this topic, most of them rely on real data from a region to generate synthetic data for the same region, which is infeasible for the many regions where real HATs are unavailable. To fill this gap, we propose ZeroHAT, a behavior-conditioned framework that generates synthetic HATs for a target region in a zero-shot manner by transferring behavioral patterns learned from real HATs in source regions and adapting them with publicly available contextual information about the target region. ZeroHAT has three key novel components: (i) a multidimensional consistency-aware intent extractor; (ii) a cross-region behavioral cloning module; and (iii) a behavior-conditioned activity realization module. We evaluate ZeroHAT on a ten-city benchmark, where extensive experiments show that ZeroHAT achieves 4.5-6.4x the normalized downstream utility of the strongest baseline and improves average fidelity by 15.6%-40.8% across target regions.

</details>


### [172] [EviRec: Continual Evidence Learning for Dual Cold-Start POI Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20313)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EviRec: A continual evidence-learning framework for POI recommendation that addresses dual cold-start problems by estimating how much historical evidence to trust for each candidate POI through three complementary views and adaptive reliability gating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most POI recommendation methods assume fixed user populations and POI catalogs, but real-world data shows substantial POI churn, user turnover, category drift, and decay in static POI memory. This motivates studying continual dual cold-start POI recommendation where both new users and new POIs constantly appear.

Method: EviRec proposes a continual evidence-learning framework that scores candidates from three views: matching view (user's recent mobility profile), transition-memory view (repeated mobility routines), and lifecycle view (candidate maturity). It qualifies evidence using each candidate's observation state and applies a reliability gate to adaptively route between transition-memory and lifecycle evidence based on reliability.

Result: EviRec consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on a full-year, five-city dataset with 30,000+ users and 684,200+ trajectories. It achieves largest gains on cold-start queries, with 20.4% improvement in NDCG@10 on Dual-New cases over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: The proposed continual evidence-learning framework effectively addresses dual cold-start problems in POI recommendation. Candidate-specific reliability gating is crucial for performance gains while preserving learned mobility routines, making EviRec practical for real-world dynamic environments.

Abstract: Point-of-Interest (POI) recommendation is a core task in location-based services, yet most existing methods assume a fixed user population and POI catalog. Through a large-scale data-driven analysis of 10 U.S. cities, we identify substantial POI churn, user turnover, category drift, and decay in static POI memory, motivating the study of continual dual cold-start POI recommendation. To address this setting, we propose EviRec, a continual evidence-learning framework that estimates how much historical evidence should be trusted separately for each candidate POI. EviRec scores each visible candidate from three complementary views: a matching view based on the user's recent mobility profile, a transition-memory view that captures repeated mobility routines, and a lifecycle view that reflects candidate maturity. Because a near-zero transition score may indicate either irrelevance or insufficient observation, EviRec qualifies the evidence using each candidate's observation state and applies a reliability gate to adaptively route between transition-memory and lifecycle evidence. We evaluate EviRec on a full-year, five-city POI check-in dataset containing more than 30,000 users and 684,200 trajectories. Experimental results show that EviRec consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, with the largest gains concentrated on cold-start queries. In particular, EviRec improves NDCG@10 by 20.4\% on Dual-New cases over the strongest baseline. In-depth analyses further confirm that these gains arise primarily from candidate-specific reliability gating while largely preserving previously learned mobility routines.

</details>


### [173] [Sharp Reconstruction Bounds for Autoencoders Using the Same Forward Map](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20333)
*Patricia Medina,Hy P. G. Lam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies reconstruction error bounds in autoencoders, showing that affine maps achieve optimal reconstruction-derivative error under certain conditions, and validates theoretical predictions on LiDAR forest scan data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand reconstruction capabilities of autoencoders that apply the same forward map before and after zeroing observed coordinates, particularly investigating theoretical error bounds and their practical relevance in real-world data.

Method: Theoretical analysis of autoencoders with equal odd input/hidden dimensions, studying orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms with bounded Jacobian singular values, and empirical validation on a 798,452-point terrestrial LiDAR forest scan across four spatial regions, two depths, and three seeds.

Result: For odd dimensions ≥3, the least uniform reconstruction-derivative error is max{1-M(M-m)/2,0}, with affine maps achieving this bound. Translated radial rotations can reconstruct balls exactly with singular values close to 1. Empirical results show theoretical bound (0.155) captures 84% of mean normalized training error (0.185) at input scale 0.05, and adding one hidden coordinate reduces reconstruction error below 6×10^{-6}.

Conclusion: The paper establishes sharp theoretical bounds on autoencoder reconstruction errors, demonstrates that affine maps achieve optimal performance under certain constraints, and shows these theoretical predictions have practical relevance in real-world LiDAR data, suggesting additional conditions may be needed for finite-data bounds.

Abstract: We study reconstruction in autoencoders that apply the same forward map before and after setting the observed coordinates to zero. For equal odd input and hidden dimensions $d\geq 3$, among orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms whose Jacobian singular values lie in $[m,M]$, we show that the least uniform reconstruction-derivative error is $\max\{1-M(M-m)/2,0\}$, with affine maps attaining this sharp bound at every prescribed depth. A translated radial rotation can nevertheless reconstruct any prescribed ball exactly with singular values arbitrarily close to one, motivating additional conditions for a finite-data bound. We test this prediction on a 798,452-point terrestrial LiDAR forest scan. At input scale $0.05$, the mean theoretical bound is $0.155$, about $84\%$ of the mean normalized training error $0.185$ across four spatial regions, two depths, and three seeds. At this scale, adding one hidden coordinate reduces the mean reconstruction error below $6\times10^{-6}$.

</details>


### [174] [COMPASS: Ordered Clustered Routing at 100K Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20352)
*Ido Greenberg,Hugo Linsenmaier,Piotr Sielski,Shie Mannor,Alex Fender,Gal Chechik,Eli Meirom*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: COMPASS algorithm solves the Ordered Clustered Traveling Salesman Problem (OCTSP) by combining search with learning-accelerated routing, achieving scalable solutions up to 100K nodes with no quality ceiling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale routing problems often involve visiting clusters of nodes in a prescribed order (OCTSP). While optimizing clusters independently seems natural, this approach misses non-local dependencies between clusters. Existing methods lack scalability and quality guarantees for very large instances.

Method: COMPASS algorithm orchestrates parallel sub-solvers, combining search with learning-accelerated routing. It exploits the clustered structure of OCTSP and can reach exact solutions in time exponential in cluster size rather than instance size. Unlike common routing solvers, COMPASS consumes general distance matrices and is not limited to coordinate inputs.

Result: COMPASS consistently outperforms alternative methods, scales to 100K synthetic nodes and 28.5K real e-commerce nodes. The latter represents the largest reported routing solution over asymmetric distances, 9x beyond established ATSP benchmarks. The algorithm has no quality ceiling - solutions keep improving with compute.

Conclusion: COMPASS provides an effective solution for large-scale OCTSP problems, achieving unprecedented scalability while maintaining solution quality. Its ability to handle general distance matrices and exploit clustered structure makes it superior to existing methods for real-world routing applications.

Abstract: Large-scale routing often requires visiting clusters of nodes in a prescribed order, giving rise to the Ordered Clustered Traveling Salesman Problem (OCTSP). Optimizing each cluster independently seems natural, but misses non-local dependencies. We introduce the COMPASS algorithm for OCTSP, which combines search with learning-accelerated routing by orchestrating parallel sub-solvers. COMPASS has no quality ceiling and its solutions keep improving with compute. It exploits the clustered structure, and can reach exact solutions in time exponential in cluster size rather than instance size. Empirically, COMPASS consistently outperforms alternative methods. Unlike common large-scale routing solvers, COMPASS consumes general distance matrices and is not limited to coordinate inputs. We demonstrate scaling to 100K synthetic nodes and to 28.5K real e-commerce nodes. To our knowledge, the latter is the largest reported routing solution over asymmetric distances, 9x beyond established ATSP benchmarks.

</details>


### [175] [Minimax-Optimal Online Contract Design with Unrestricted Bounded Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20353)
*Rui Ai,David Simchi-Levi,Han Zhong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TLDR: Minimax regret for repeated contract design with bounded outcome-contingent payments is Θ(T^{m/(m+1)}) (up to log factors) for m≥2 outcomes, with novel techniques for handling discontinuous agent responses and benchmark normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses repeated contract design under information asymmetry where the principal observes outcomes but not the actions generating them. The key challenge is that agent's best responses can make expected profit discontinuous in payments, making standard learning approaches ineffective. The research aims to understand the fundamental limits of learning optimal contracts in this setting and how additional contractible outcomes affect learning complexity.

Method: The approach uses minimax regret framework with three main components: 1) Effective-dimension reduction allowing benchmark normalization even without shift-invariant tie-breaking, 2) Revealed preference analysis yielding monotone response map in payment-difference coordinates, 3) Lipschitz-parametrized learning policy using only observed outcome categories. The method handles arbitrary action spaces and agent heterogeneity without requiring smoothness or monotone-surplus assumptions.

Result: For m≥2 outcomes, the minimax regret over T rounds is Θ(T^{m/(m+1)}) up to logarithmic factors. Upper bound achieved via the proposed learning policy, while lower bound construction shows each additional contractible outcome creates a precise and unavoidable increase in worst-case learning cost.

Conclusion: The paper establishes tight minimax regret rates for repeated contract design, showing how learning difficulty scales precisely with the number of contractible outcomes. The results reveal fundamental tradeoffs in contract design under incomplete information and provide optimal learning algorithms that work under minimal assumptions about agent behavior and action spaces.

Abstract: We study repeated contract design when a principal observes outcomes but not the actions that generate them. The principal may use any bounded outcome-contingent payment vector, and the agent's best response can make expected profit discontinuous in those payments. For every fixed number $m\ge2$ of outcomes, the minimax regret over $T$ rounds is of order $T^{m/(m+1)}$, up to logarithmic factors. The upper bound allows arbitrary action spaces and agent heterogeneity, without smoothness or monotone-surplus assumptions. Its key is an effective-dimension reduction that the benchmark can be normalized even when fixed tie-breaking is not shift invariant, after which revealed preference yields a monotone response map in payment-difference coordinates. A learning policy built on a Lipschitz parametrization of this map attains the rate using only observed outcome categories. The lower-bound construction accounts for how incentive losses accumulate across outcome dimensions. It shows that each additional contractible outcome creates a precise and unavoidable increase in the worst-case cost of learning.

</details>


### [176] [Learning Principal-Agent Contracts for Equitable Smallholder Carbon Farming under Moral Hazard and Adverse Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20404)
*Rishi Bharadwaj,Yadati Narahari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A reinforcement learning approach reveals that profit-maximizing carbon farming contracts systematically exclude smallholder farmers, amplifying existing disparities instead of addressing them, due to disproportionate per-hectare measurement costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agricultural soils are a major untapped carbon sink, and smallholder farmers in South Asia and sub-Saharan Africa are crucial for scaling climate mitigation through carbon farming. However, real-world carbon programs largely fail to reach these farmers, creating an important gap that needs to be addressed.

Method: The researchers study the problem through contract design theory, modeling an aggregator offering pooled contracts to heterogeneous smallholder farmers with private adoption costs (adverse selection) and unobserved effort (moral hazard). They formulate this evolving contracting problem as a POMDP (Partially Observable Markov Decision Process) and use reinforcement learning to learn dynamic profit-maximizing contracts.

Result: A profit-maximizing aggregator amplifies rather than reduces exclusion of smallholders. Adoption rates reach 87.7% on large farms but only 8.2% on smallholdings. Per-hectare MRV costs decrease as farm size increases, and pooling contracts compound this gradient rather than offsetting it. Counterfactual analysis shows that making MRV costs purely area-proportional would eliminate this disparity.

Conclusion: Profit-maximizing carbon farming contracts systematically exclude smallholder farmers due to disproportionate measurement costs, but well-designed contracts and policies could open carbon income opportunities for smallholders while enabling agricultural soils to contribute to climate mitigation at scale.
