Abstract: Agricultural soils are a major untapped carbon sink. Carbon farming is emerging as a promising practice for tapping this potential. Smallholder farmers, who dominate agriculture across South Asia and sub-Saharan Africa, are key to scaling climate mitigation via carbon farming. It is ironic that real-world carbon programs largely fail to reach them. We study this important gap through the lens of contract design. An aggregator offers a single pooled contract to a heterogeneous population of smallholder farmers who have private adoption costs (adverse selection) and exert unobserved effort (moral hazard), with agronomic outcomes evolving over multiple seasons. We formulate this evolving contracting problem as a POMDP and use reinforcement learning to learn a dynamic profit-maximising contract. We analyse the performance of the aggregator under various conditions. We find that a profit-maximising aggregator does not merely inherit the exclusion of smallholders, it amplifies it. On large farms the aggregator realises 87.7% of achievable adoption, against only 8.2% on smallholdings. Per-hectare Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) costs fall as farm size rises, and the aggregator's pooling contract compounds this gradient rather than offsetting it. A counterfactual that makes MRV costs purely area-proportional eliminates this disparity. Our results and simulation can guide contract and policy design that opens carbon income to smallholders while enabling agricultural soils to contribute to climate mitigation at scale.

</details>


### [177] [The Bias of Nonlinear Two-Time-scale Stochastic Approximation under Constant Step-Sizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20409)
*Djamel Rassem Lamouri,Dorian Baudry,Nicolas Gast*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Nonlinear two-timescale stochastic approximation analysis with tight mean-squared error bounds scaling as O(α+β²/α²) under constant step-sizes, revealing qualitative differences from linear TTSA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-timescale stochastic approximation (TTSA) is fundamental for analyzing coupled iterative algorithms in reinforcement learning, optimization, and control, but finite-time guarantees for nonlinear TTSA schemes under constant step-sizes remain difficult to obtain.

Method: Studied nonlinear TTSA with step-sizes α ≫ β under standard stability, regularity, and Markovian noise assumptions, separating contributions of initial conditions, fast-timescale tracking error, Markovian dependence, and timescale coupling in the analysis.

Result: Proved tight upper bounds on mean-squared error and bias scaling as O(α+β²/α²) when β ≤ α^{3/2}, showing this bound is tight and revealing additional finite-time effects in nonlinear dynamics absent in linear TTSA.

Conclusion: Nonlinear TTSA exhibits fundamentally different finite-time behavior than linear TTSA, with additional effects introduced by nonlinear dynamics, and provides important theoretical understanding of convergence rates in practical iterative algorithms.

Abstract: Two-timescale stochastic approximation (TTSA) is a fundamental tool for analyzing coupled iterative algorithms in reinforcement learning, optimization, and stochastic control. However, finite-time guarantees for nonlinear two-timescale schemes remain difficult to obtain, especially under constant step-sizes. In this paper, we study nonlinear TTSA with step-sizes $α\ggβ$. Under standard stability, regularity, and Markovian noise assumptions, we upper bound the mean-squared error and the bias of both iterates around their limiting equilibria. Our bounds scale as $O(α+β^2/α^2)$, which we prove to be tight when $β\leα^{3/2}$. The analysis separates the contributions of initial conditions, fast-timescale tracking error, Markovian dependence, and timescale coupling, thereby clarifying the origin of the $β^2/α^2$ term. Our results reveal qualitative differences from the linear TTSA setting previously studied, showing that nonlinear dynamics introduce additional finite-time effects that are absent in the linear case.

</details>


### [178] [SCGFM-ART: Amortized Relational Transport for Structure-Centric Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20419)
*Xiaodong He,Xincheng Wang,Zhao Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCGFM-ART is a graph foundation model that aligns diverse graphs onto a shared relational atlas using amortized transport plans, enabling unified representation learning across heterogeneous graph domains with efficient inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current graph foundation models struggle with severe domain shifts in topology, graph scale, and feature semantics across different graph datasets, preventing the creation of a unified, domain-agnostic representation space.

Method: Proposes a structure-centric framework that uses a relational atlas with finite relational landmarks as universal coordinate system. Employs Amortized Relational Transport (ART) to predict reusable graph-to-base transport plans, decomposing graphs into global relational response coordinates and local node-to-role correspondences.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art transferability across 14 cross-domain graph- and node-level classification tasks (average ranks 2.29 and 1.14). Provides 44.2 to 85.1 times faster inference by avoiding iterative alignment at test time. Theoretical analysis shows coordinate fidelity bounds and stability guarantees.

Conclusion: SCGFM-ART successfully addresses heterogeneity in graph foundation models through structure-centric alignment onto a shared relational atlas, enabling efficient cross-domain transfer with theoretical guarantees and practical performance improvements.

Abstract: Graph foundation models (GFMs) aim to learn transferable representations across severely heterogeneous graph domains. However, severe domain shifts in topology, graph scale, and feature semantics impede the construction of a unified, domain-agnostic representation space. To address this, we propose SCGFM-ART, a structure-centric GFM framework that aligns arbitrary graphs onto a shared relational atlas via Amortized Relational Transport (ART). The relational atlas serves as a universal coordinate system defined by a finite set of relational landmarks (bases), while ART directly predicts reusable, end-to-end graph-to-base transport plans, bypassing costly runtime Gromov-Wasserstein optimizations. Under this formulation, SCGFM-ART decomposes a graph into a unified representation: globally via its relational response coordinates relative to the atlas, and locally via its node-to-role structural correspondences. These correspondences project disparate node attributes into a canonical role space, resolving structural and semantic heterogeneity within a singular alignment interface. Rigorously modeling graphs and atlas bases as finite measured relational spaces, we establish coordinate fidelity bounds, prove stability under predicted transport plans, and derive an amortized coverage bound that guarantees our learning objective tightly surrogates ideal relational coverage. Benchmarked across 14 cross-domain graph- and node-level classification tasks, SCGFM-ART achieves state-of-the-art transferability, securing superior average ranks of 2.29 and 1.14, respectively. Topological perturbation analyses demonstrate that node-role transport retains fine-grained structural nuances beyond global coordinates. On real-world benchmarks, the amortized formulation yields 44.2 to 85.1 times faster frozen target-domain inference by avoiding iterative alignment at test time.

</details>


### [179] [Seismic Site Response Prediction from Sparse Observations Using Finite-Element-Pretrained Latent Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20451)
*Yi Zhu,Su Chen,Xiaojun Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FLARE-T is a machine learning framework that improves site-response predictions by learning low-dimensional latent dynamics from dense simulations and calibrating them with limited field records, reducing errors in acceleration histories and response spectra.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical site-response predictions often deviate from observations, but correcting these discrepancies is difficult due to limited sensor coverage and number of events in field records.

Method: FLARE-T learns a low-dimensional response manifold and input-driven dynamics from dense finite-element simulations, trains a sparse encoder to map simulated sensor responses into learned coordinates, and uses limited field records to calibrate the dynamics. It uses a short response window to initialize predictions while the complete base motion drives the response.

Result: FLARE-T improved multi-depth acceleration histories and 5%-damped pseudoacceleration response spectra relative to original finite-element models, reducing errors at every evaluated sensor for motions of different intensities and for both horizontal components at Lotung. Two source models with different constitutive parameters achieved comparable accuracy.

Conclusion: FLARE-T provides a data-efficient means of combining dense numerical response information with limited field records to improve future site-response predictions, with reduced dependence on precise prior calibration.

Abstract: Numerical site-response predictions often deviate from observations, yet correcting these discrepancies is difficult because records are limited in both sensor coverage and number of events. This study proposes the Transfer-Enabled Forced Latent Autoencoder for Response Equations (FLARE-T) to improve these predictions by learning and calibrating low-dimensional latent dynamics that connect the base acceleration input to acceleration outputs at multiple depths. FLARE-T learns a low-dimensional response manifold and input-driven dynamics from dense finite-element simulations. It then trains a sparse encoder to map simulated sensor responses into the learned coordinates and uses limited records to calibrate the dynamics within them. A short response window initializes each prediction, while the complete base motion drives the response. The framework was evaluated using a layered-soil centrifuge test and the Lotung field vertical array. Test-set results show that FLARE-T improved multi-depth acceleration histories and 5%-damped pseudoacceleration response spectra relative to the original finite-element models, reducing errors at every evaluated sensor for motions of different intensities and, at Lotung, for both horizontal components. Two Lotung source models with different constitutive parameters achieved comparable test-set accuracy, indicating reduced dependence on precise prior calibration. FLARE-T therefore provides a data-efficient means of combining dense numerical response information with limited field records to improve future site-response predictions.

</details>


### [180] [Training Neural Networks to Approach the Optimum Bayes Estimator in Dense Multi-Emitter Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20465)
*Yi Sun,Mona Sharifi,Muzna Yumman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural networks trained on synthesized frames can approach optimal Bayes estimator for dense emitter localization, enabling future high-throughput super-resolution microscopy


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve optimal dense emitter localization in super-resolution microscopy (SMLM) that approaches theoretical limits (Bayes estimator)

Method: Train neural networks on synthesized/simulated frames containing emitter data

Result: Neural networks can approach the optimum Bayes estimator for dense emitter localization

Conclusion: This success justifies and enables future work on training neural networks to achieve high-throughput, large-field-of-view super spatiotemporal resolution in single-molecule localization microscopy

Abstract: We train neural networks on synthesized frames to approach the optimum Bayes estimator for dense emitter localization. The result justifies the future work on training neural networks to achieve high-throughput large-FOV super spatiotemporal resolution SMLM.

</details>


### [181] [Deep Learning-Based Classification of Cognitive and Resting States Using Electroencephalography Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20467)
*K. A. Januka S. Fernando,Harshit Srivastava*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A CNN-GRU deep learning framework for distinguishing resting vs. cognitive states from EEG signals achieves high accuracy on mathematical (83.2%), memory (76.1%), and music (83.4%) task classifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG provides non-invasive measurement of brain activity, and understanding cognitive vs. resting state fluctuations is important for brain state analysis. Deep learning enables automatic feature extraction from complex EEG data.

Method: Proposes a deep learning framework combining CNN (Convolutional Neural Network) stacked with GRU (Gated Recurrent Unit) for feature extraction from EEG signals. Uses time-frequency analysis to explore signal characteristics, then evaluates features with conventional deep learning and machine learning classifiers including a proposed 2D-Net architecture.

Result: Achieved accuracies of 83.177% for resting vs. mathematical task classification, 76.107% for resting vs. memory task classification, and 83.432% for resting vs. music task classification, outperforming comparative methods.

Conclusion: The integration of signal processing with deep learning methodologies is effective for discriminating resting from cognitive states using EEG signals, demonstrating the framework's superiority over other evaluated methods.

Abstract: The categorization of cognitive and resting states derived from electroencephalography (EEG) signals is crucial for comprehending fluctuations in brain activity linked to various mental states. EEG provides a non-intrusive approach for documenting brain function in both resting and task-oriented cognitive conditions, whilst deep learning techniques enable the automatic extraction of significant patterns from intricate EEG data. This study presents a deep learning framework to distinguish between resting and cognitive states through EEG records. The proposed framework integrates a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) stacked with a Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) for the extraction of features from EEG signals. Time-frequency analysis is conducted to explore the salient aspects of signals, and the derived features are then assessed utilizing conventional deep learning and machine learning classifiers, including the suggested 2D-Net architecture. The proposed approach and feature extraction strategy outperform the evaluated comparative methods, achieving accuracies of 83.177% for resting-versus-mathematical task classification, 76.107% for resting-versus-memory task classification, and 83.432% for resting-versus-music task classification. The findings illustrate the efficacy of integrating signal processing with deep learning methodologies to discriminate resting from cognitive states utilizing EEG signals.

</details>


### [182] [Distributionally Robust Federated Learning with Multi-Source Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20501)
*Yingzhu Liu,Zhongkui Li,Pengcheng You,Ashish Cherukuri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated learning with dual uncertainty handling: addresses both cross-client mixture uncertainty and within-client distributional ambiguity using global ambiguity sets, with theoretical guarantees and practical algorithm


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In federated learning, client data distributions differ and the true mixture across clients is often unknown. Existing methods assume accurate client-wise distribution estimates, but these can be unreliable with finite samples, creating both cross-client mixture uncertainty and within-client distributional ambiguity.

Method: Constructs a global ambiguity set as the union of admissible mixtures of local ambiguity sets, allowing client-specific ambiguity radii. This enables a client-wise separable reformulation. Develops a federated algorithm for a penalty-based reformulation.

Result: Establishes high-probability out-of-sample performance guarantee. Proves algorithm convergence under milder regularity conditions. Simulations demonstrate the algorithm's effectiveness in handling both types of uncertainty.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully addresses both cross-client mixture uncertainty and within-client distributional ambiguity in federated learning, providing theoretical guarantees and practical effectiveness through a novel ambiguity set construction and federated algorithm.

Abstract: Federated learning trains a shared model from private client data. In practice, data-generating distributions may differ, and the true mixture across clients is often unknown, making the underlying group distribution difficult to specify. Existing approaches address cross-client mixture uncertainty by optimizing against the worst-case mixture, yet assume accurate client-wise distribution estimates. However, these estimates can be unreliable when based on finite samples. To handle both cross-client mixture uncertainty and within-client distributional ambiguity, we construct a global ambiguity set as the union of admissible mixtures of local ambiguity sets. The construction allows client-specific ambiguity radii and admits a client-wise separable reformulation. Leveraging this structure, we establish a high-probability out-of-sample performance guarantee. We further develop a federated algorithm for a penalty-based reformulation and prove its convergence under milder regularity conditions. Simulations validate the algorithm's effectiveness.

</details>


### [183] [Radio Frequency Detection and Classification of Microplastics in Water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20507)
*Jaden Tolbert,Md Saiful Islam,Pingshan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ML-assisted RF dielectric spectroscopy enables rapid, label-free classification of 10 μm microplastic particles in aqueous environments with good accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's an urgent need for rapid, label-free detection methods for micro- and nano-plastic particles as conventional optical/spectroscopic techniques become challenging for small particles due to limited throughput and complex sample preparation.

Method: Developed a machine learning-assisted radio-frequency dielectric spectroscopic cytometry platform. Characterized eight types of 10 μm MP particles in DI water at four frequencies (0.2-9 GHz). Used measured RF scattering parameters (S-parameters) to train supervised ML models for material classification.

Result: Achieved macro-average F1-score, precision, and recall values exceeding 0.71 for eight MP classes in DI water. Maintained PET classification performance in saline carrier media (3.3% and 6.6% sea salt). Demonstrated classification of MPs in mixed samples and saline environments.

Conclusion: ML-assisted RF DiSC is feasible for rapid, single-particle MP classification in aqueous environments. Future work will focus on improved classification through enhanced RF calibration, increased spectral coverage, larger datasets, and validation with environmentally aged/contaminated microplastics.

Abstract: Micro- and nano-plastic particles (MPs/NPs) are ubiquitous environmental contaminants whose increasing abundance and potential health impacts have created an urgent need for rapid, label-free detection methods. As particle size decreases to the low-micrometer range, conventional optical and spectroscopic techniques become increasingly challenging because of limited throughput and/or complex sample preparation. In this work, we present a machine learning (ML)-assisted radio-frequency (RF) dielectric spectroscopic cytometry (DiSC) platform for the label-free detection and classification of MPs. Eight types of $ 10 $ μm nominal-diameter MP particles suspended in deionized (DI) water were characterized at four frequencies spanning $ 0.2\text{-}9\text{ GHz} $. The measured alterations in RF scattering parameters (S-parameters), referenced to the carrier medium, were used to train supervised ML models for material classification, including the identification of MPs in mixed samples and saline-water environments. For eight MP classes suspended in DI water, the proposed method achieved macro-average F1-score, precision, and recall values exceeding $ 0.71 $. Furthermore, PET classification performance was largely maintained in saline carrier media containing $3.3\% $ and $ 6.6\% $ sea salt. These results demonstrate the feasibility of ML-assisted RF DiSC for rapid, single-particle MP classification in aqueous environments. Future work will focus on improving classification performance through enhanced RF calibration, increased spectral coverage, larger training datasets, and validation using environmentally aged and biologically contaminated microplastics.

</details>


### [184] [When EOS Tokens Disagree: Understanding Length Inflation in On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20511)
*Yuxiao Yang,Tianrun Yu,Shangzhe Li,Kaixiang Zhao,Xuchao Zhang,Chetan Bansal,Huaxiu Yao,Taylor W. Killian,Weitong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Termination-token mismatch between teacher and student models in on-policy distillation causes excessive response length (length inflation), which can be mitigated by treating functionally equivalent EOS tokens as shared semantic stopping actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the problem of length inflation in on-policy distillation (OPD), where student responses become excessively long and can exhaust generation budgets. This is a practical issue in model distillation that affects deployment efficiency.

Method: The authors identify termination-token mismatch between base students and post-trained teachers as the key cause. They study this across Qwen3, Llama, and Gemma models, showing that models can place stopping probability on different EOS tokens even with identical declared stopping sets. They propose treating functionally equivalent EOS tokens as shared semantic stopping actions, and conduct stage-wise analysis across different K2-Horizon training stages.

Result: Termination-token mismatch suppresses the student's preferred termination action without reliably transferring teacher-preferred alternatives. Aligning decoding stopping sets alone is insufficient. The proposed treatment of functionally equivalent EOS tokens as shared semantic stopping actions substantially mitigates mismatch-induced length inflation across all three model families. Stage-wise analysis shows termination preferences shift substantially during training, with distinct late-stage length inflation that persists beyond termination alignment.

Conclusion: Termination mismatch is an important but not exhaustive source of OPD length dynamics. The findings highlight the need for careful handling of termination behavior in model distillation, and the authors release an implementation incorporating the proposed termination-handling corrections.

Abstract: We study length inflation in on-policy distillation (OPD), where student responses can become excessively long and even exhaust the generation budget. We identify \emph{termination-token mismatch} between base students and post-trained teachers as an important source of this behavior. Across Qwen3, Llama, and Gemma, the two models can place their stopping probability on different EOS tokens, even when their declared stopping sets are identical. This mismatch can suppress the student's preferred termination action without reliably transferring the teacher-preferred alternative. We show that aligning the decoding stopping set alone is insufficient, while treating functionally equivalent EOS tokens as a shared semantic stopping action substantially mitigates mismatch-induced length inflation across all three model families. To further understand how termination behavior evolves over training, we study OPD across different K2-Horizon training stages. This stage-wise analysis shows that termination preferences can shift substantially during training, while also revealing a distinct length inflation late in the OPD run that persists beyond termination alignment. Together, these results identify termination mismatch as an important, but not exhaustive, source of OPD length dynamics. We release an implementation incorporating the proposed termination-handling corrections.

</details>


### [185] [Parallelism, critical windows, and separations among diffusion language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20539)
*Sitan Chen,Liye Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper compares parallelism capabilities of three diffusion LLM paradigms (masked, uniform, Gaussian), finding uniform/Gaussian can sample with fewer forward passes scaling with distribution complexity rather than context length, while proving masked diffusion requires more passes due to narrower critical windows in sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While diffusion LLMs are touted for parallelism advantages over autoregressive models, there's limited understanding of how different dLLM paradigms (masked, uniform, Gaussian diffusion) compare in their capacity for parallelism, despite competing proposals.

Method: The authors conduct a fine-grained theoretical comparison of parallelism capabilities among masked, uniform, and Gaussian diffusion approaches. They use complexity theory measures like dual total correlation and analyze scaling behavior with dimension d, proving necessary and sufficient forward pass requirements.

Result: 1) Uniform/Gaussian diffusion can sample with forward passes scaling with dual total correlation (intrinsic complexity), potentially much smaller than context length. 2) For certain distributions, uniform/Gaussian diffusion need only ~√d passes while masked diffusion requires ~d passes, establishing first provable separation between paradigms. 3) The separation arises from masked diffusion's narrower critical windows during sampling.

Conclusion: The theoretical analysis challenges common intuition about parallelism in diffusion LLMs, showing uniform/Gaussian diffusion offer superior parallelism scaling compared to masked diffusion, with performance differences stemming from sampling window characteristics rather than token commitment constraints.

Abstract: A popular selling point of diffusion large language models (dLLMs) is their capacity for parallelism: the ability to generate sequences of text far more efficiently than autoregressive models, which require one forward pass per token. Yet among the many competing paradigms for dLLMs, from masked to uniform to Gaussian diffusion, principled understanding of how these different proposals compare in parallelism remains limited. In this work, we initiate a fine-grained comparison of the capacity for parallelism among these three leading approaches and prove the following:
  - Uniform and Gaussian diffusion can sample in a number of forward passes which scales with the dual total correlation of the underlying distribution, a measure of intrinsic complexity which can be much smaller than the context length. Previously, it was only known how to achieve this using masked diffusion.
  - For a certain family of random empirical measures, we show that $\widetildeΘ(\sqrt{d})$ forward passes are necessary and sufficient to sample using uniform or Gaussian diffusion, yet there exist approximate score oracles for which $\widetildeΩ(d)$ forward passes are needed for masked diffusion. This establishes the first provable separation in parallelism between the three prevailing dLLM paradigms.
  Contrary to popular intuition that masked diffusions are harder to parallelize because they must commit to token values, the latter separation instead comes from the fact that the critical windows in masked diffusion sampling are asymptotically narrower than those in uniform and Gaussian diffusion sampling.

</details>


### [186] [Mitigating Retaliatory Algorithmic Collusion in Repeated Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20548)
*Karthik Sivachandran,Rohan Paleja*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CURB is a reward-shaping framework that detects and prevents reinforcement learning agents from converging to collusive outcomes in repeated games by penalizing policy dependence on punishment histories, effectively converting collusive equilibria into competitive ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to prevent RL agents from colluding in repeated interactions are limited to specific economic settings (two-sided platforms, auctions), leaving a gap for general repeated games. The paper addresses how to design interventions that prevent supra-competitive outcomes resembling explicit collusion without communication.

Method: The paper formalizes the connection between Q-learning collusion and classical Simple Penal Codes theory, showing that any non-trivial SPC creates detectable conditional dependence in agents' policies via total variation distance between action distributions across cooperation/defection histories. Building on this, they propose CURB - a reward-shaping framework that penalizes this TV distance signal during Q-learning.

Result: CURB is theoretically guaranteed to convert any SPC fixed point into a trivial one, precluding collusive equilibria sustained by punishment threats. Empirically, it substantially reduces collusion by Q-learning agents in Bertrand and Cournot Competition Repeated Games, and also extends to deep Q-network agents in Bertrand competition.

Conclusion: The CURB framework provides a generalizable approach to prevent collusion in RL agents across various repeated game settings, with theoretical guarantees and empirical effectiveness demonstrated in multiple economic competition scenarios, including extension to deep RL methods.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning agents trained to maximize their own reward in repeated interactions can converge to supra-competitive outcomes resembling explicit collusion, without communication or shared design. Existing mitigation approaches are largely tied to specific economic settings, like two-sided platforms and auctions, leaving open how to design interventions for general repeated games. We address this gap by formalizing the connection between empirical observations from prior work on Q-learning collusion and classical theory of Simple Penal Codes (SPCs). We show any non-trivial SPC induces a quantifiable conditional dependence in agents' policies, detectable via the total variation distance between an agent's action distributions across cooperation and defection histories. Building on this connection, we propose CURB (Collusion Unwinding via Reward shaping and Belief injection), a reward-shaping framework that penalizes this Total Variation (TV) distance signal during Q-learning and is guaranteed to convert any SPC fixed point of the dynamics into a trivial one, thus precluding collusive equilibria sustained by punishment threats. Empirically, CURB substantially reduces collusion by Q-learning agents in both Bertrand and Cournot Competition Repeated Games. We further demonstrate that CURB extends to deep Q-network agents in Bertrand competition, suggesting the mechanism generalizes beyond tabular Q-learning.

</details>


### [187] [CrystalMO-TuRBO: Multi-Objective Trust-Region Bayesian Optimization for High-precision Joint Crystal Structure Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20592)
*Joseph Agada,Yishu Wang,Arpan Biswas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CrystalMO-TuRBO: A multi-objective Bayesian optimization method for joint crystal structure refinement using X-ray and neutron diffraction data, combining global exploration with local refinement for improved performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional crystal structure refinement methods struggle with non-convex, noisy parameter landscapes and face challenges in joint refinement of X-ray and neutron diffraction data. Current approaches require manual weighting of complementary data modalities and often lead to suboptimal solutions.

Method: Two-phase multi-objective trust region Bayesian optimization: Phase 1 performs global exploration using parallel trust-region Bayesian optimization across multiple scalarizations to identify promising parameter regions; Phase 2 conducts localized refinement within a shrinking region for high-precision solutions.

Result: The method demonstrates improved convergence, robustness, and parameter precision compared to classical refinement methods and Bayesian optimization baselines when applied to experimentally collected X-ray and neutron diffraction data from single-crystal Ho2Ti2O7.

Conclusion: CrystalMO-TuRBO successfully addresses the challenges of joint crystal structure refinement by separating global search from fine-grained optimization, providing a more effective approach for handling complementary diffraction data modalities.

Abstract: Crystal structure refinement is a fundamental inverse problem in materials characterization, where structural parameters are optimized to reproduce experimental diffraction data. Conventional approaches, such as least-squares and likelihood-based optimization, rely on local search and often struggle with non-convex, noisy, and highly correlated parameter landscapes, particularly when integrating multiple diffraction modalities. Joint refinement of X-ray and neutron data is especially challenging due to their complementary but competing sensitivities, which are typically combined through scalarized objectives requiring manual weighting and leading to suboptimal solutions. We propose CrystalMO-TuRBO, a multi-objective trust region Bayesian optimization architecture for joint crystal structure refinement. The method models X-ray and neutron discrepancies as separate objectives and transforms the problem into a normalized maximization setting. A two-phase optimization strategy is introduced: Phase 1 performs global exploration using parallel trust-region Bayesian optimization across multiple scalarizations to identify promising regions of the parameter space, while Phase 2 conducts localized refinement within a shrinking region to achieve high-precision solutions. This design explicitly separates global search from fine-grained optimization, addressing the unique accuracy requirements of refinement tasks. We evaluate the proposed method on experimentally collected X-ray and neutron diffraction data from single-crystal Ho2Ti2O7. Results demonstrate improved convergence, robustness, and parameter precision compared to classical refinement methods and Bayesian optimization baselines on refinement of a single-crystal pyrochlore material system.

</details>


### [188] [Recursive Quantum Long Short-Term Memory for Stable Short-Horizon Temperature Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20594)
*Mu-En Lee,Yen-Ku Liu,Samuel Yen-Chi Chen,Yun-Cheng Tsai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recursive QLSTM architecture improves stability and performance for quantum temporal weather prediction compared to standard QLSTM


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum LSTM models show varying optimization behavior across random initializations and temporal contexts, requiring more stable and better performing architectures for hybrid quantum-classical temporal models

Method: Compared recursive QLSTM vs standard QLSTM for one-step-ahead daily temperature prediction using Toronto weather data, identical training settings, and 20 random seeds across 8, 16, and 32-day input windows

Result: Recursive model consistently reaches near-optimal test loss earlier, reduces MAE and RMSE, and exhibits smaller generalization gap compared to standard QLSTM

Conclusion: Recursive quantum feature transformations can improve stability and out-of-sample performance for compact hybrid quantum-classical temporal models

Abstract: Quantum long short-term memory (QLSTM) models extend recurrent sequence learning with variational quantum circuits, but their optimization behavior can vary substantially across random initializations and temporal contexts. This paper evaluates a recursive QLSTM architecture against a standard QLSTM for one-step-ahead prediction of daily minimum and maximum temperature. Using daily weather observations from Toronto and identical training settings, we compare convergence, predictive accuracy, and generalization across input windows of 8, 16, and 32 days over 20 random seeds. The recursive model consistently reaches a near-optimal test loss earlier, reduces mean absolute error and root mean squared error, and exhibits a smaller generalization gap. These results indicate that recursive quantum feature transformations can improve stability and out-of-sample performance for compact hybrid quantum--classical temporal models.

</details>


### [189] [Multi-center Medical Data Mining with FL-Net - A One-stop Shop for Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20650)
*Simon Süwer,Julian Klemm,Elisa Acitelli,Mathieu Almeida,Lucia Altucci,Zsolt Bagyura,Michelangela Barbieri,Zsolt-Zoltán Bedő,Rosaria Benedetti,Béla Bihari,Csongor Csalóka,Lucia Dicunta,Stanislav Ehrlich,Bjoern M. Eskofier,Sándor-József Fejér,Georg Fröwis,Walter Hötzendorfer,Alexandra Kautzky-Willer,Jens Johann Georg Lohmann,Marianna Maranghi,Lorenzo Marconi,Rudolf Mayer,Wouter Leonard Megchelenbrink,Monika Moga,Adham Mottalib,Sanjeev Mehta,Madeleine Müller,Thomas Nyström,Balázs-Attila Orbán,Paul O'Toole,Giuseppe Paolisso,Paolo Parini,Matteo Pedrelli,Enrico Petrillo,Philipp Poindl,Niklas Probul,Anastasia Pustozerova,Tanja Šarčević,Lukas Weilguny,Jan Baumbach,Andreas Maier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FL-Net is a federated clinical research framework that addresses limitations in existing FL frameworks by providing modular data harmonization, secure containerized workflow execution, and enabling re-use of harmonized data across studies, evaluated with up to 50 concurrent clients across international healthcare datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most federated learning studies remain simulations, and existing FL frameworks fail to meet key requirements for real-world clinical research. The authors identified five requirements from literature and found that none of 14 analyzed FL frameworks fully satisfied these requirements for practical clinical implementation.

Method: Developed FL-Net framework with modular data harmonization, data discovery, disclosure control, securely built versioned FL-Net-Tools, and containerized federated workflow execution within a persistent network. Evaluated through harmonization processes, cross-study patient discovery across MIMIC and US-130 datasets, and reproducible federated workflows with up to 50 concurrent clients.

Result: FL-Net demonstrated end-to-end capabilities including successful data harmonization, cross-study patient discovery, and reproducible audited federated workflows. The framework is being developed within two EU projects covering over 800,000 patients across 10 hospitals in 9 countries, handling both longitudinal and single-point-in-time data.

Conclusion: FL-Net provides a practical foundation for interoperable, reproducible, and privacy-preserving multicenter clinical research by fulfilling all five requirements identified from literature analysis, enabling real-world deployment beyond simulations and supporting re-use of harmonized data and workflows across studies.

Abstract: Federated learning enables collaborative training without sharing patient-level data, but most studies remain simulations. Based on five requirements derived from the literature, we analyzed 14 FL frameworks and found that none fully satisfied these requirements. We present FL-Net, a novel federated clinical research framework to fulfill all requirements. It integrates modular data harmonization, data discovery, disclosure control, securely built versioned FL-Net-Tools and containerized federated workflow execution into a persistent network. It enables the re-use of harmonized data and workflows across studies. FL-Net's end-to-end capabilities were evaluated through harmonization, cross-study patient discovery across MIMIC and US-130, and reproducible, audited federated workflows with up to 50 concurrent clients. FL-Net is being developed within the dAIbetes and Microb-AI-ome EU projects and will cover over 800,000 patients across 10 hospitals in 9 countries covering longitudinal and single point in time data, FL-Net provides a practical foundation for interoperable, reproducible, and privacy-preserving multicenter clinical research.

</details>


### [190] [Epidemiological Causal Graph Identification: Challenges, Identifiability and Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20676)
*Sambit Mishra,Yingying Wang,Christine K. Johnson,Urbashi Mitra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves identifiability of causal direction between ordinal and exponential family variables in DAGs, extending beyond additive noise models, and develops computational methods for practical discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current causal discovery research focuses primarily on continuous variables under additive noise models, neglecting mixed datasets containing ordinal scales, counts, and continuous measurements, creating a gap for real-world mixed-type data analysis.

Method: 1) Theoretical proof of distributional identifiability for edge direction between ordinal (ordered logit model) and exponential family nodes; 2) Computational methods including score-based exhaustive search and masked continuous optimization using DAGMA for larger graphs.

Result: The authors prove that edge direction between an ordinal and an exponential family node is distributionally identifiable for generic parameter values, generalizing previous Ordinal-Poisson results to broader exponential family, with numerical validation showing recovery of edge orientations within Markov equivalence classes.

Conclusion: The research advances causal discovery for mixed data types by establishing theoretical identifiability results and developing practical computational approaches that go beyond classical structural equation models, enabling causal inference from observational data with ordinal and exponential family variables.

Abstract: Causal discovery from observational data is fundamental to statistics and machine learning, yet determining causal direction without interventions necessitates structural assumptions. Existing identifiability research primarily focuses on continuous variables under additive noise models, often neglecting mixed datasets containing ordinal scales, counts, and continuous measurements. This paper investigates causal discovery in Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) where nodes follow either an ordinal distribution (via an ordered logit model) or a regular one-parameter exponential family distribution. We prove that the edge direction between an ordinal and an exponential family node is distributionally identifiable for generic parameter values. Our findings generalize previous Ordinal-Poisson results to the broader exponential family. Computationally, we introduce a score-based exhaustive search and a masked continuous optimization framework using DAGMA for larger graphs. Numerical results validate the theory, recovering edge orientations within a Markov equivalence class that are unidentifiable under classical structural equation models.

</details>


### [191] [RISC-V and machine learning: a survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20677)
*Shriman Keshri,Apparna Singh,Chinmaya Kumar Palo,Shreya Adya,Subhankar Mishra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey examines the state of RISC-V ISA in machine learning applications, analyzing current capabilities, challenges, and future directions based on recent research, and proposes research directions to advance RISC-V as a foundational platform for next-gen ML systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The intersection of open-source processor architectures and machine learning is driving demand for customizable, efficient, and accessible hardware.

Method: The survey examines RISC-V ISA in ML applications through analysis of academic/commercial implementations, software frameworks, and real-world applications. It evaluates the RISC-V ML ecosystem from instruction set extensions to compiler optimizations and deployment strategies.

Result: Findings reveal progress in energy efficiency, specialized instruction development, and framework integration, while highlighting challenges in standardization, verification complexity, and ecosystem fragmentation.

Conclusion: The analysis proposes four research directions: specialized neural processing extensions, adaptive/modular processor architectures, security frameworks, and energy-efficient multi-domain architectures to advance RISC-V as a foundational platform for next-generation machine learning systems.

Abstract: The intersection of open-source processor architectures and machine learning is driving the demand for customizable, efficient, and accessible hardware. This survey examines the state of the RISC-V ISA in machine learning applications, analyzing current capabilities, challenges, and future directions based on recent research. The analysis covers academic and commercial implementations, software frameworks, and real-world applications. The RISC-V machine learning ecosystem is evaluated, from instruction set extensions and core implementations to compiler optimizations and deployment strategies. Key contributions include a unified taxonomy of RISC-V ML implementations, a comparative analysis of performance and design trade-offs, an evaluation of software toolchain maturity, and the identification of emerging trends in instruction set extensions and specialized accelerators. Findings reveal progress in energy efficiency, specialized instruction development, and framework integration, while highlighting challenges in standardization, verification complexity, and ecosystem fragmentation. The analysis proposes four research directions to address current limitations: specialized neural processing extensions, adaptive and modular processor architectures, security frameworks, and energy-efficient multi-domain architectures. These directions provide a roadmap for advancing RISC-V as a foundational platform for next-generation machine learning systems.

</details>


### [192] [Don't Mask the Environment: Observation Supervision Changes How Agents Explore Under RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20715)
*Juzheng Zhang,Disha Makhija,Manoj Ghuhan Arivazhagan,Vinayshekhar Bannihatti Kumar,Rashmi Gangadharaiah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ActObs introduces joint supervision of both action and observation tokens in agent trajectories during SFT, improving downstream RL performance without adding extra data or compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard SFT only supervises agent-authored action tokens while treating environment observations as context, not prediction targets. The paper questions whether this convention provides optimal initialization for subsequent reinforcement learning, hypothesizing that learning to predict observations would encourage better modeling of action consequences.

Method: ActObs extends standard SFT by supervising both action tokens and observation tokens already present in trajectories. Although deployed agents never generate observations, this joint supervision encourages the policy to model action consequences without requiring additional data, parameters, sequence tokens, or forward passes.

Result: ActObs improves downstream RL performance across multiple benchmarks: On Qwen3-4B, GRPO from ActObs achieves higher pass@k at every sampling budget on Terminal-Bench 2.0. On Qwen3-8B, it trades some pass@1 reliability for higher pass@k (+3.4 pp at pass@16) and solves more distinct tasks. Cross-domain code editing on aider-polyglot shows +4.2 pp improvement at pass@1 for 4B model. ActObs retains more entropy during RL while requiring less policy movement, keeping the final policy closer to SFT initialization.

Conclusion: Joint supervision prevents one-sided specialization that occurs with action-only training, where action and observation gradients become orthogonal. ActObs preserves consequence prediction capability, prepares policies better for downstream exploration, and improves RL performance without additional computational cost.

Abstract: Agent trajectories record what an agent does and what happens next. Yet standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) applies loss only to agent-authored action tokens, using environment observations as context but not as prediction targets. We ask whether this convention provides the best initialization for subsequent reinforcement learning. We introduce ActObs, which also supervises the observation tokens already present in each trajectory. Although deployed agents never generate observations, learning to predict them encourages the policy to model action consequences without adding data, parameters, sequence tokens, or forward passes. The methods perform similarly after SFT but diverge after GRPO. On Qwen3-4B, GRPO from ActObs achieves higher pass@k at every evaluated sampling budget than its action-only counterpart on Terminal-Bench 2.0. On Qwen3-8B, it trades some pass@1 reliability for higher pass@k (+3.4 pp at pass@16) and solves more distinct tasks. The advantage extends to cross-domain code editing on aider-polyglot (+4.2 pp at pass@1 at 4B), whose tasks are unseen during SFT and RL. ActObs retains more entropy during RL while requiring less policy movement, leaving the final policy closer to its SFT initialization. Our analysis traces this difference to SFT: action and observation gradients rapidly become orthogonal, while action-only training leaves a large residual observation gradient and degrades environment prediction below the base model. Joint supervision prevents this one-sided specialization, preserving consequence prediction and preparing the policy for downstream exploration.

</details>


### [193] [Video DeltaNet: A Video-Native Hybrid Attention for Livestream Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20744)
*Haocheng Xi,Yiming Xie,Hexu Zhao,Yiwen Zhang,Michael Liu,Thomas Creavin,Kurt Keutzer,Xiuyu Li,Zhaoyang Lv,Chenfeng Xu,Haiwen Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Video DeltaNet (VDN) combines local Softmax attention with bidirectional linear memory for efficient long-range video context modeling, achieving 14.5x speedup over dense attention baselines for video generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video diffusion models suffer from computational bottlenecks due to repeated processing of long spatiotemporal token sequences during denoising. While linear attention offers efficiency benefits, directly applying it to video models often fails to preserve the fine-grained interactions needed for high-quality video generation.

Method: VDN introduces a hybrid approach combining local Softmax attention with Video Delta Attention (VDA) for long-range video context. VDA uses a bidirectional linear memory that updates once per frame by jointly incorporating spatial tokens. The system includes separate output projections, learnable gates for branch calibration, and a staged teacher-alignment recipe to integrate the new pathway into pretrained models. Applied to MiniMax H3 architecture, VDN uses hybrid attention for video-to-video interactions while retaining Softmax for text/audio interactions.

Result: With eight-step distillation and optimized SGLang serving stack, VDN-H3 completes DiT denoising for a 14.3-second, 768p video in 6.70 seconds on eight NVIDIA B200 GPUs, achieving a 14.5x speedup over the 50-step dense H3 baseline on the same GPU configuration.

Conclusion: Video DeltaNet successfully combines the efficiency of linear attention with the fine-grained modeling capabilities of Softmax attention, enabling significantly faster video generation while maintaining quality through careful architectural design and distillation techniques.

Abstract: Video diffusion models repeatedly process long spatiotemporal token sequences during denoising, making attention a major computational bottleneck. Linear attention offers an appealing alternative and has been widely adopted in recent large language models, but directly applying it to video models often fails to preserve the fine-grained interactions required for high-quality generation. We present Video DeltaNet (VDN), which combines local Softmax attention with bidirectional linear memory for long-range video context. Its linear branch introduces Video Delta Attention (VDA), which updates memory once per frame by jointly incorporating its spatial tokens. Separate output projections and learnable gates calibrate the two branches, while a staged teacher-alignment recipe progressively introduces the new pathway into pretrained models. We instantiate VDN on MiniMax H3, applying the hybrid to video-to-video interactions while retaining Softmax for interactions involving text or audio. With eight-step distillation and an optimized SGLang serving stack, VDN-H3 completes DiT denoising for a 14.3-second, 768p video in 6.70 seconds on eight NVIDIA B200 GPUs, corresponding to a 14.5x speedup over the 50-step dense H3 baseline on the same GPU count.

</details>


### [194] [Calibrated RF-Fingerprinting Under Interference With Heterogeneous Transmission Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20765)
*Tariq Abdul-Quddoos,Xiangfang Li,Lijun Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RF-fingerprinting for co-channel interference using 1D CNN with calibrated confidence thresholds to guarantee false negative bounds in multi-label classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RF-fingerprinting research is limited to single-transmitter scenarios, but real-world environments often have co-channel interference with multiple signals overlapping in time and frequency, necessitating solutions for realistic high-contention wireless environments.

Method: Formulate RF-fingerprinting with co-channel interference as a multi-label classification problem, employ a 1D convolutional neural network (CNN), and calibrate models to derive confidence thresholds that guarantee upper bounds on average false negatives.

Result: Validation on real-world data from POWDER 5G testbed with 802.11a (Wi-Fi), 4G LTE, and 5G NR waveforms shows accuracy from 97% to 73% after calibration depending on channel conditions, with micro recall scores approximately equal to (1 - calibrated false negatives), demonstrating robustness to out-of-distribution interference.

Conclusion: The proposed method shows potential for realistic RF-fingerprinting in high-contention wireless environments with co-channel interference, providing confidence guarantees on false negative rates through calibrated thresholds.

Abstract: Radio Frequency(RF)-Fingerprinting is a spectrum monitoring technique that identifies specific transmitters based on hardware impairments imprinted within the emitted signal. Although widely researched, studies almost exclusively consider scenarios where only one transmitter is emitting at a time, limiting real world applicability. In this work, we further the study of RF-Fingerprinting by considering co-channel interference, with multiple emitted signals interfering with each other, overlapping in time and frequency. Specifically, we formulate this problem as a multi-label classification problem and employ a 1D convolutional neural network (CNN). Furthermore, the models are calibrated such that the confidence thresholds for the label probabilities are derived, with guarantees on the upper bound on the average number of False Negatives, providing a degree of confidence in not missing a true spectrum policy violation. The proposed method is validated using real world data from the POWDER 5G testbed on devices transmitting 802.11a(Wi-Fi), 4G LTE, and 5G NR waveforms. The results show accuracy as high as 97% and as low as 73% after calibration depending on channel conditions. Also calibrating for various average false negatives upper bounds achieves micro recall scores of approximately (1 - calibrated false negatives) with the calibration robust to out-of-distribution interference, demonstrating the potential of the proposed method in a realistic high contention wireless environment

</details>


### [195] [PosteriorBench: From Point Estimates to Posterior Matching in Evaluating Generative Inverse Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20794)
*Jiachen Yao,Zi-Siang Hsu,Xi Deng,Aditi Gupta,Xin Ju,Sally M Benson,Gege Wen,Anima Anandkumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PosteriorBench is a new benchmark that evaluates the distributional accuracy of generative inverse solvers on physics-based inverse problems, rather than just pointwise reconstruction accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations for generative models in scientific inverse problems focus primarily on single plausible reconstructions, which is insufficient for ill-posed problems where multiple solutions may be consistent with observations. Current methods can achieve good pointwise accuracy while failing to capture the true posterior through issues like mode collapse, overconfident uncertainty, or averaging incompatible solutions.

Method: The authors create PosteriorBench with four physics-based inverse problems: Darcy flow inversion, Poisson source recovery, carbon capture and storage, and light transport material inference. They construct high-fidelity reference posteriors using heavy computational methods (rejection sampling, MCMC) to enable direct assessment of whether solvers recover the full set of solutions. They use a five-metric evaluation suite: posterior-mean error, posterior-standard-deviation error, maximum mean discrepancy, sliced Wasserstein distance, and radially averaged power-spectrum error.

Result: The benchmark reveals substantial distribution-matching gaps across current solvers. Experiments show that neural operators improve resolution robustness, and that guidance weights and generation noise are key to posterior-variance calibration.

Conclusion: PosteriorBench provides a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating the distributional accuracy of generative inverse solvers, highlighting important gaps in current methods and identifying key factors for improving posterior-variance calibration in ill-posed inverse problems.

Abstract: Generative models are increasingly used to solve scientific inverse problems, but existing evaluations still focus primarily on whether a method can produce a single plausible reconstruction. This is insufficient for ill-posed problems, where multiple solutions may be consistent with the same sparse or noisy observations. In these settings, a method can achieve strong pointwise accuracy while still failing to capture the true posterior through mode collapse, overconfident uncertainty, or averaging incompatible solutions. We introduce PosteriorBench, a benchmark for evaluating the distributional accuracy of generative inverse solvers. PosteriorBench evaluates four physics-based inverse problems: Darcy flow inversion, Poisson source recovery, carbon capture and storage, and light transport material inference. For each task, we construct high-fidelity reference posteriors using computationally heavy but established procedures such as rejection sampling and Markov chain Monte Carlo, enabling direct assessment of whether solvers recover the full set of solutions rather than the single best sample. We pair these references with a five-metric posterior evaluation suite: posterior-mean error, posterior-standard-deviation error, maximum mean discrepancy, sliced Wasserstein distance, and radially averaged power-spectrum error. These metrics assess pointwise accuracy, marginal uncertainty, distributional alignment, and global frequency fidelity. The benchmark spans sparse sensing, low-resolution observations, nonlinear forward models, varying noise levels, and multimodal priors, with a unified pipeline for distribution matching and uncertainty quantification. Our experiments reveal substantial distribution-matching gaps across current solvers, while showing that neural operators improve resolution robustness, and guidance weights and generation noise are key to posterior-variance calibration.

</details>


### [196] [Score Centering Stabilizes Off-policy Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20807)
*Martin Marek,Max Ryabinin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Score centering correction eliminates training-inference mismatch drift in RL for large language models, stabilizing training without rollout efficiency costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for large language models is highly sensitive to training-inference mismatch (TIM), but eliminating TIM completely is impractical due to major rollout efficiency costs.

Method: Proposes an additive "score centering" correction term that cancels drift (persistent bias between training and inference that accumulates over time) to stabilize RL under TIM.

Result: Score centering alone matches or outperforms importance sampling methods under quantization across models from 0.6B to 30B parameters, with gap increasing as mismatch severity grows. It also composes with importance sampling for even better performance.

Conclusion: Score centering provides a practical solution to TIM-induced instability in RL for LLMs by addressing the root cause (drift), offering better performance than existing methods while maintaining rollout efficiency.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) of large language models is notoriously sensitive to small differences between training and inference engines, often referred to as the training-inference mismatch (TIM). However, completely eliminating TIM is impractical, as it would come at a major cost to rollout efficiency. In this paper, we show that the instability of RL under TIM is primarily caused by drift: a persistent bias between training and inference engines that accumulates with every training step. We derive an additive "score centering" correction term that stabilizes RL under TIM by canceling drift. When training models from 0.6B to 30B parameters, score centering alone matches or outperforms methods based on importance sampling under quantization, with the gap growing as the mismatch becomes more severe. Because the correction is additive, score centering also composes with importance sampling -- their composition outperforms pure importance-sampling baselines in our staleness experiments.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [197] [Message capacity and claim wording set the transition points of collective truth-finding in language-model networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19183)
*Makoto Fukushima*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LLM collectives can reach wrong consensus even with majority starting correct; this is determined by message capacity (how many messages agents read) and claim wording bias (threshold before reading messages).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how reading limitations (message capacity) alone determine whether LLM collectives reach correct or wrong consensus, especially when agents can only read a few messages due to cognitive/contextual/cost constraints.

Method: Model message capacity as a single parameter determining communication network; analyze 31,824 queries with 8B-parameter LLM; measure claim-dependent bias (field/threshold) from wording; test on second 8B model and 70B model for comparison.

Result: Collective outcomes reduce to logistic function of weighted inbox sum; wrong consensus becomes unreachable when agents read <6.4 of 31 sources on average; but predictions fail due to claim wording bias (field below calibration mean); assertion bias detected in 8B models but not in 70B model.

Conclusion: A collective's fate is largely determined by two single-agent measurements: the threshold set by claim wording and the message capacity that sets the transition point between correct/wrong consensus.

Abstract: Whether human or large language model (LLM), an agent in a discussion reads only a few of the others' contributions, bounded by cognition, context, or cost. LLM collectives can settle on a wrong consensus even when a majority starts out correct; we ask how far that reading bound alone decides the outcome. We model the bound with one number, the message capacity, which sets how many of the others' messages an agent reads, and generate the communication network from it. Over 31,824 randomized queries, we found that an 8-billion-parameter model's judgment of a claim effectively reduces to a logistic function of a weighted sum of its inbox, the update rule of a stochastic binary neuron with divisively normalized weights. From these weights and the network's degree statistics alone, the wrong consensus should become unreachable from any start once agents read, on average, fewer than 6.4 of their 31 sources. In 1,414 episodes with assigned starts the prediction failed: the correct side won in fewer than 50% of episodes from every start, and in only 28-45% when 75% of agents started correct. The failure traces to the field, the threshold that a claim's wording sets for the agent's answer before any message is read: the experimental claims' fields lay below the calibration mean, and with each claim's own field the same weights reproduce the outcomes. Reversing the wording showed that the threshold follows what a claim asserts, not whether it is true. On a second 8B model the pipeline predicts claim-dependent bistability; transition points appeared where computed, and an eight-claim calibration matched in 15 of 16 conditions. At 70B the assertion bias is not detected. Thus a collective's fate is largely set by two single-agent measurements: the threshold a claim's wording sets, and the message capacity that sets the transition point.

</details>


### [198] [CC-OPI: Online Distributed Task Allocation for UAV Swarms under Communication Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19208)
*Biao Liu,Tong Zhang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: CC-OPI is an event-driven algorithm for multi-robot task allocation under intermittent connectivity, replacing the "allocate-then-execute" paradigm with interleaved negotiation and execution for robust performance in communication-constrained environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-robot missions like search and rescue, short communication ranges fragment UAV swarms into transient information islands, breaking down the conventional "allocate-then-execute" paradigm that requires global consensus before physical movement.

Method: CC-OPI is an event-driven algorithm that interleaves task negotiation with physical execution, replanning only at discrete physical/topological events. It includes: 1) two cost-evaluation metrics adapted to dynamic topologies (spatial locality penalty for regionalized operation and deadline-aware urgency term) with non-preemptive state locks, and 2) a decentralized fault-tolerance layer with version-based state synchronization and global-time-driven emergency pools.

Result: CC-OPI terminates in finite time without stale-completion deadlock or unbounded reassignment. At 250m communication radius, it achieves ~0.80 task completion rate, outperforming modified PI and CBBA by ~7 percentage points, exceeding static baselines by ~20 points, and staying close to full-connectivity PI/CBBA performance. It degrades gracefully with weaker connectivity and handles packet loss, terrain occlusion, and runtime task arrivals.

Conclusion: CC-OPI provides robust multi-robot task allocation under intermittent connectivity through deliberate trade-offs—accepting more messages and some redundant travel in exchange for maintaining high task completion rates despite communication constraints—making it suitable for real-world applications like post-disaster search and rescue.

Abstract: In multi-robot missions such as post-disaster search and rescue, a short communication range fragments a swarm of Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) into transient information islands. Under such intermittent connectivity, the prevailing "allocate-then-execute" paradigm--which requires global consensus before any physical movement--breaks down. This paper proposes the Communication-Constrained Online Performance Impact (CC-OPI) algorithm, an event-driven method that interleaves task negotiation with physical execution. CC-OPI replans only at discrete physical and topological events and integrates two further elements. The first is a pair of cost-evaluation metrics adapted to dynamic topologies--one with a spatial locality penalty that promotes regionalized operation, the other with a deadline-aware urgency term--complemented by a non-preemptive state lock that shields each UAV's ongoing action. The second is a decentralized fault-tolerance layer that pairs version-based state synchronization with a global-time-driven emergency pool. We establish that CC-OPI terminates in finite time, free of stale-completion deadlock and of unbounded reassignment within the mission horizon. In simulations at a 250 m communication radius, CC-OPI sustains a task completion rate of about 0.80: it leads a matched online execution of the unmodified Performance Impact (PI) and Consensus-Based Bundle Algorithm (CBBA) rules by about seven percentage points, exceeds the naively transferred static baselines by roughly 20 points, and remains within several points of PI and CBBA under full connectivity. Within the tested settings, CC-OPI degrades gracefully as connectivity weakens and absorbs packet loss, terrain occlusion, and runtime task arrival. The price is more messages and some redundant travel--a deliberate trade-off of efficiency for robustness.

</details>


### [199] [Reputation as Community Memory for the Agentic Web](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19502)
*Ryan Chard,Gus Ellerm,Alexander Brace,Alok Kamatar,Suman Raj,Ian Foster,Kyle Chard*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Cairn is a community reputation platform for AI agents that aggregates collective knowledge about resources (data sources, services, tools) using a time-decayed Beta model with confidence shrinkage, allowing agents to query community opinions and submit evidence-backed ratings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent memory systems are typically private to single agents, but trustworthy knowledge about shared environments cannot be established by any single agent alone - it requires corroboration across many independent observers. Agents need collective memory to reliably assess resources they depend on.

Method: Cairn captures collective knowledge through a reputation engine that uses a time-decayed Beta model with confidence shrinkage to aggregate observations. It allows agents to query community opinions of resources before use and submit evidence-backed ratings afterward, supporting semantic discovery over reviewer rationales.

Result: The system was evaluated under adversarial simulation (lying, collusion, camouflage), benchmarked for retrieval performance, and tested in a case study rating heterogeneous agents in production, demonstrating effectiveness in aggregating trustworthy community knowledge.

Conclusion: Agentic memory benefits from being collective rather than private, and Cairn provides an effective platform for establishing trustworthy knowledge of shared environments through community reputation mechanisms that handle adversarial behaviors while supporting semantic discovery.

Abstract: Agents can now externalize experience into memory, consolidating historical traces into semantic knowledge and procedural shortcuts that persist between sessions. Such memory is typically private to a single agent. We argue that agentic memory benefits from being collective, because trustworthy knowledge of the shared environment---the data sources, services, and tools agents depend on---cannot be established by any single agent, only corroborated across many independent observers. We present Cairn, a community reputation platform that captures collective knowledge, allowing agents to query the community's opinion of a resource before use and to submit evidence-backed ratings afterward. Cairn aggregates observations via a time-decayed Beta model with confidence shrinkage and supports semantic discovery over reviewer rationales. We evaluate Cairn's reputation engine under adversarial simulation (e.g., lying, collusion, camouflage), benchmark its retrieval performance, and report a case study of rating heterogeneous agents in production.

</details>
